# XDNA2 NPU: technical notes

What this project knows about the AMD XDNA2 NPU, where each fact comes from, and what is still unknown. The XDNA backend is built on these notes, so every entry says whether it was **measured** on the development laptop, **read** in a source, or is an **estimate** that still has to be checked.

2. [The NPU of the development laptop](#the-npu-of-the-development-laptop)

## Sources

| Source | What it gives | Kind |
|---|---|---|
| Linux 7.3.4, `drivers/accel/amdxdna/` and `include/uapi/drm/amdxdna_accel.h` | The host side of the firmware protocol, the ioctl contract, clock tables, scheduling | AMD, open (GPL-2.0) |
| Linux 7.3.4, `Documentation/accel/amdxdna/amdnpu.rst` | Architecture overview, execution flow | AMD |
| [mlir-aie / IRON](https://xilinx.github.io/mlir-aie/latest/) and Peano (`llvm-aie`) | Toolchain to program the tiles; device `npu2` (AIE2P) covers Strix, Strix Halo and Krackan Point | AMD, open |
| AM027 and AM029 (Versal AIE-ML v2 architecture manual and register reference) | Tile architecture of the same generation, according to third parties; AMD does not link them to Ryzen | AMD, for Versal |
| [Getting peak TOPS on a Ryzen AI 7 350 NPU](https://destevez.net/2026/05/getting-peak-tops-on-a-ryzen-ai-7-350-npu/) | Tile memories, clock, 56 TOPS measured in int8 with data in local memory | Third party |
| [Cyronius/zest](https://github.com/Cyronius/zest), [Nulifyer/llama-xdna-hybrid](https://github.com/Nulifyer/llama-xdna-hybrid), llama.cpp PR #28800 and issue #21725 | Working prompt-processing designs and their measurements | Third party |

There is no official architecture manual of XDNA2 for Ryzen.

## The NPU of the development laptop

Lenovo ThinkPad L14 Gen 6, AMD Ryzen AI 7 PRO 350 (Krackan Point), Debian 13, Linux 7.3.4. Measured on 2026/11/12 through the driver, without XRT (`_/xdna2-lab/sonda_npu.py`, informational ioctls only).

| Item | Value |
|---|---|
| PCI device | `1022:17f0` revision `0x20`, `0000:c5:00.1`; the driver names it `RyzenAI-npu6` |
| Driver | `amdxdna_accel_driver` 0.10.0 (in-tree), device node `/dev/accel/accel0` (group `render`) |
| Firmware | 1.1.2.64 since 2026/11/12 (`amdnpu/17f0_10/npu_7.sbin`, from linux-firmware); before, the 1.0.0.63 of Debian (`npu.sbin`). AIE version 1.1 |
| Array | 8 columns x 6 rows |
| Core tiles | rows 2 to 5 (32 tiles), 2 DMA channels, 16 locks each |
| Memory tiles | row 1 (8 tiles), 6 DMA channels, 64 locks each |
| Shim tiles | row 0 (8 tiles), 2 DMA channels, 16 locks each |
| Maximum clock | H clock 1800 MHz; the driver reports 58 TOPS at that clock |
| Idle | H clock 88 MHz, MP-NPU clock 52 MHz, 2 mW |
| Hardware contexts | up to 16 at the same time |

Read, not measured here: every core tile has 64 KiB of data memory and 16 KiB of program memory, and every memory tile 512 KiB (4096 KB of L2 in all).

## Driver interface

The NPU is a DRM accel device. Eleven ioctls (`DRM_IOCTL_AMDXDNA_*`): `CREATE_HWCTX`, `DESTROY_HWCTX`, `CONFIG_HWCTX`, `CREATE_BO`, `GET_BO_INFO`, `SYNC_BO`, `EXEC_CMD`, `WAIT_CMD`, `GET_INFO`, `GET_ARRAY` and `SET_STATE`.

 - **Permissions (read).** Only `SET_STATE` (power mode, preemption) needs root. Contexts, buffers, commands and queries work for any user who can open the device node.
 - **Queries (measured).** `GET_INFO` and `GET_ARRAY` answer without creating anything: AIE version and metadata, firmware version, clocks, power mode, resources, sensors (total power, use of every column), hardware contexts, last asynchronous error, buffer usage of a process.
 - **Execution flow (read).** A workload is an array overlay (configuration of the stream switches and the programs of the core tiles) plus control code, a sequence of `XAie_TxnOpcode` operations that the firmware runs for every command; the DMA transfers between host memory and the array are operations of that control code. Userspace creates a context, gives it the overlay, copies the control code into an instruction buffer and submits command buffers that point to the input, output and instruction buffers.
 - **Contexts (read).** With the firmware feature `AIE2_TEMPORAL_ONLY`, every context gets all the columns and the contexts share the array in time. Not checked yet on this firmware.

### Buffers

Read in `amdxdna_gem.c`. All of them are created with `CREATE_BO` and closed with the generic `DRM_IOCTL_GEM_CLOSE`.

| Type | What it is | Address the NPU uses |
|---|---|---|
| `SHARE` (1) | Host memory for data: allocated by the driver and mapped with `mmap` at the offset that `GET_BO_INFO` returns, or the user's own memory, given as a table of address ranges or a dma-buf (`vaddr` of `CREATE_BO`) | With PASID (the case here), the user virtual address of the mapping |
| `CMD` (4) | A `SHARE` buffer that the driver counts as internal. Holds one command | Not used by the NPU |
| `DEV_HEAP` (2) | The device memory of the process: a multiple of 64 MiB, up to 512 MiB, at device address `0x4000000` onwards. It has to exist, and be mapped, before a context is created | Device address |
| `DEV` (3) | A piece of the heap, aligned to 32 KiB: instruction buffers and overlays (PDI). Its user address is inside the mapping of the heap | Device address |

`SYNC_BO` flushes the CPU cache of a range: the NPU is not cache coherent, so data written by the host has to be synced before a command and data written by the NPU after it.

Measured on 2026/11/12 with `llama-xdna-info --check-buffers`:

 - A 64 MiB heap gets the device address `0x4000000`, and the first `DEV` buffer starts there.
 - The NPU address of a `SHARE` or `CMD` buffer is the address of its mapping in the process: the NPU reads and writes the memory of the process directly, with no copy in between.
 - A 16 MiB `SHARE` buffer was created, mapped and written with the locked-memory limit of the process at 8 MiB: the buffers of the driver do not count against that limit. Neither do they when a command uses them (three of 16 MiB each, measured with the matrix multiplication of 4096).
 - `SYNC_BO` in the direction "from device" fails with `EINVAL` on every ordinary buffer: the driver only takes it for the debug buffer of a context. The direction "to device" is the cache flush to use in both cases.

**A buffer the NPU writes must have nothing modified in the cache of the processor when the NPU writes it** (found on 2026/11/13):

 - When a `SHARE` buffer is mapped, the driver puts all its pages in at once (`amdxdna_insert_pages` faults every page of the mapping), and the kernel zeroes them through the cache of the processor (read in `amdxdna_gem.c`). The zeros stay in the cache as modified data: up to the 16 MiB of the L3 of this processor, shared by its 8 cores, and the 1 MiB L2 of every core.
 - The NPU writes to memory behind the cache. `SYNC_BO` (`clflushopt` on every line, `drm_clflush_virt_range`) writes back the modified lines and drops them, so the flush that has to come before the host reads what the NPU wrote also writes those zeros back over it.
 - **Measured**: `llama-xdna-gemm` with 512 x 1024 x 16128, whose C (33 MB) was created right before the command, got 38 % of the values it checked as zeros and the rest exact, with no other sign of a fault (`_/xdna2-lab/probar_backend.sh 3`). The same command with C filled and flushed before it got every value right, and the NPU had written every word of C (`... 3d`, which fills C with bytes `0xff` and maps afterwards the tiles that still hold them).
 - **Confirmed** (`... 3a`): with one core kept busy while the command ran, so that the processor does not sleep and write its L3 to memory, the same multiplication without the flush got 37 % of C as zeros. They are whole lines of 64 bytes, the lines of the cache: 190,938 of them (11.65 MiB), and only 7 lines with loose zeros, the real zeros of the product. The share of zeroed lines grows along C, from 0 % at its start to 98 % at its end, which is the part the kernel zeroed last (in 2 MiB pieces: 0, 0, 0, 0, 1, 1, 3, 17, 12, 38, 65, 85, 93, 95, 97 and 98 %).
 - Smaller multiplications, and every one before the conversions were made faster, had not shown it: their zeros had left the cache before the NPU wrote, pushed out by the conversion of A (136 ms for 4096 rows before) or when all the cores slept while the NPU started (the first command of a context takes 2 to 4 ms more). Bytes `0xff` left modified in C before a command, without a busy core, also came back right once (`... 3x`). It depends on timing, so it can appear at any size.
 - What the runner does since then (`XdnaRunner::mEnsure`): it flushes every buffer it creates, whole, before anything else. The NPU only writes C, and after that flush the host only reads it, which leaves no modified lines. **Checked** (`... 3b`): the same multiplication, with the core kept busy, got every value right; the flush of a new C of 33 MB costs about 0.5 ms, once.
 - A second way to leave modified lines, without any write of the host to them: the line right after a run of its stores (measured on 2026/11/15, see [The chained overlay](#the-chained-overlay)). In a buffer that both the host and the NPU write, what the host wrote is flushed up to the end of its page and one page more.

**How much memory the NPU reaches** (read on 2026/11/15 in the driver of Linux 7.3.4 and in mlir-aie):

 - Inside the array: 2 MiB of data memory in the 32 core tiles (64 KiB each, plus 16 KiB of program memory) and 4 MiB in the 8 memory tiles. The NPU has no memory of its own besides that: everything else is the memory of the laptop, which it shares with the processor and the GPU (46.7 GiB as Linux sees it, `MemTotal`). The laptop has 64 GB: on 2026/11/15 the firmware kept 16 GiB of them for the GPU (the frame buffer of the UMA, a setting of the BIOS; `mem_info_vram_total` of amdgpu), which the NPU cannot use, since the driver takes its buffers from the memory of Linux. Besides that, the GPU can use up to half of the memory of Linux (GTT: 23.35 GiB, the `pages_limit` of TTM).
 - A `SHARE` buffer allocated by the driver has no limit of size of its own: `CREATE_BO` only rejects a size of 0 and hands the rest to `drm_gem_shmem_create` (`amdxdna_gem.c`). With PASID the driver does not map it in the IOMMU either (`amdxdna_gem_obj_open`), so what bounds the buffers of a process is the free memory of the laptop. Their pages are real memory from the moment they are mapped (`amdxdna_insert_pages`). Without PASID (not the case here) the driver maps every buffer into an IOVA range of its own (`amdxdna_iommu.c`).
 - The user's own memory given as a `SHARE` buffer (`vaddr` of `CREATE_BO`) is pinned with `pin_user_pages_fast` and counts against the locked-memory limit of the process (8 MiB here) unless the process has `CAP_IPC_LOCK` (`amdxdna_ubuf.c`). The backend does not use it.
 - The heap (`DEV_HEAP`: instruction buffers and overlays) goes up to 512 MiB per process, in pieces of 64 MiB (`AIE2_DEVM_MAX_SIZE`, `npu6_regs.c`).
 - A buffer descriptor of a shim tile holds a byte address of 48 bits (`Base_Address_High`, bits 47 to 32, in `lib/Dialect/AIE/Util/aie_registers_aie2.json` of mlir-aie; that file describes AIE2, and the AIE2P one is not there). With PASID the addresses are virtual addresses of the process, which in the user space of x86-64 stay below 2^47, so the width of the address does not bound anything either.
 - The IOMMU group of the NPU is `identity` for the traffic without PASID (`/sys/bus/pci/devices/0000:c5:00.1/iommu_group/type`), with a DMA mask of 64 bits.
 - Checked up to 33.7 GB (31.4 GiB) of `SHARE` buffers on Linux `amdxdna` (Flint 40B-A2.5B with all 47 MoE layers and 2 dense layers on the NPU, 2026/11/19): no damaged data and KL 0.0547 in `-ub 1` (zest got damaged data without an error above about 26 GB of buffers, on Windows; see [What other projects found](#what-other-projects-found)).

### Running a workload

Read in `amdxdna_ctx.c`, `aie2_ctx.c` and `aie2_message.c`.

 1. Create the heap (`DEV_HEAP`) and map it.
 2. `CREATE_HWCTX` with `num_tiles` = columns x 4 (32 for the whole array) and a zeroed quality of service. It returns the context handle and a syncobj.
 3. Copy the overlay (PDI) into a `DEV` buffer and give it to the context with `CONFIG_HWCTX` (`DRM_AMDXDNA_HWCTX_CONFIG_CU`): one entry per compute unit, with the buffer handle and its function. A context is configured once; it cannot be reconfigured.
 4. Copy the control code (instruction stream) into a `DEV` buffer.
 5. Write a command into a `CMD` buffer and submit it with `EXEC_CMD`, passing the handles of the buffers it uses. It returns the sequence number of the command in its context; the first one is 0.
 6. Wait for the command on the syncobj of the context, with the generic `DRM_IOCTL_SYNCOBJ_TIMELINE_WAIT` at the timeline point equal to the sequence number, and then read the state in the header of the command.

Waiting (measured on 2026/11/12): `WAIT_CMD` returns `EOPNOTSUPP` on this NPU. The driver implements it only for the next generation (`aie4`); for this one it signals the end of command N at point N of the timeline of the syncobj that `CREATE_HWCTX` returns. The limit of that wait is an absolute time of the monotonic clock, and it fails with `ETIME` when it passes. Point 0 of a timeline stands for its last point, so waiting for command 0 waits for every command submitted until then. The syncobj handle belongs to the process: it is destroyed with `DRM_IOCTL_SYNCOBJ_DESTROY`.

A command is a 32-bit header followed by 32-bit words. Header: state in bits 0 to 3, number of extra compute-unit masks in bits 10 and 11, number of words in bits 12 to 22, opcode in bits 23 to 27. The first word (or words) is the mask of compute units; the rest is the payload:

 - `ERT_START_CU` (0): the arguments of the kernel, as its register map. For a design built with mlir-aie: opcode (64 bits, value 3 = transaction), address of the instruction buffer (64 bits), number of instruction words (32 bits) and then the address of every data buffer (64 bits each). At most 19 words, so 7 data buffers.
 - `ERT_START_NPU` (20): address and size of the instruction buffer, a property count, and then properties and arguments (at most 34 words).
 - `ERT_CMD_CHAIN` (19): a list of command buffers that the firmware runs as one message. The slots of a chain have to fit in 4 KiB.

The state of the header tells the result: 4 = completed, 5 = error, 6 = abort, 8 = timeout.

Limits and timers: 4 commands in flight per context; a command that does not finish in 2 seconds is detected and its context restarted (`tdr_timeout_ms`); messages to the firmware time out after 2 seconds; at most 32 compute units per context.

How the driver passes a command to the firmware (read in `aie2_ctx.c` and `aie2_message.c`):

 - The module parameter `force_cmdlist` is on by default in Linux 7.3.4, so even a single command travels as a command list of one entry: the driver copies it into a slot of the device heap and sends `MSG_OP_CHAIN_EXEC_BUFFER_CF`, or `MSG_OP_CHAIN_EXEC_NPU` with a firmware of protocol 6.15 or later. Only with the parameter off does a single command go as `MSG_OP_EXECUTE_BUFFER_CF`. The parameters of the module can only be read and written by root.
 - The state `abort` does not come from the firmware: the driver sets it when the answer is missing (the channel of the context was closed with the command pending), when it does not have the expected size, or when the list failed but the failed entry reports success. After that the driver marks the channel of the context as bad. The state `error` is a failure that the firmware reported for an entry.
 - `CONFIG_HWCTX` sends the overlay to the firmware without waiting for the answer, so it returns at once and a bad overlay is not reported there.
 - The NPU suspends itself after 5 seconds without use; the next context wakes it up.
 - The driver pins the pages of the buffers of a command. With PASID the firmware reads and writes them through the mappings of the process: the driver faults every page in when a buffer is mapped and tracks later changes of the mapping.

Without privileges the driver also reports, for every context, the commands submitted and ended and, with a firmware of protocol 6.18 or later, a health report (last operation of the control code, program counter, fatal error); and the last asynchronous error of the firmware (`GET_ARRAY`).

### First run on the laptop

Measured on 2026/11/12 with `llama-xdna-run` and the official whole-array matrix multiplication of mlir-aie (int8 in and out, 8 columns, tiles of 64 x 64 x 64), through the device layer of this project, without XRT. Every result was compared with a reference computed on the CPU (`_/xdna2-lab/gemm_i8.py`), with the output buffer zeroed before every run.

**The NPU runs the design, and the results are right**: 512, 1024, 2048 and 4096 (square matrices), all values equal to the reference.

 - The context over the 32 core tiles is created in about 0.4 ms with the NPU awake and in about 40 ms when it has to wake up. The driver gives it columns 0 to 7.
 - The overlay lands at device address `0x4000000` and the instruction buffer at `0x4038000`; the driver takes the slots of its command lists from the same heap, in between.
 - The first command of a context takes 2 to 4 ms longer than the rest, presumably because the firmware loads the overlay then.
 - One overlay serves every size: the four sizes build byte-identical overlays (86544 bytes) and differ only in their instruction stream (1956, 3332, 6420 and 12596 words).
 - Three buffers of 16 MiB each ran with the locked-memory limit of the process at 8 MiB: the buffers of a command do not count against it.
 - The clocks that the driver reports are a slow average, not the level in use: the H clock read 145 MHz right after creating a context and 1801 MHz after 5 seconds of load, while the time of every run was the same from the first one. So a context without quality-of-service hints does run at the highest level.

| Size | Multiply-accumulates | Time of a command (median) | Multiply-accumulates per second | TOPS |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 512 x 512 x 512 | 1.34 x 10^8 | 0.099 ms | 1.4 x 10^12 | 2.7 |
| 1024 x 1024 x 1024 | 1.07 x 10^9 | 0.289 ms | 3.7 x 10^12 | 7.4 |
| 2048 x 2048 x 2048 | 8.59 x 10^9 | 2.59 ms | 3.3 x 10^12 | 6.6 |
| 4096 x 4096 x 4096 | 6.87 x 10^10 | 17.6 ms (300 runs, 17.0 to 21.7) | 3.9 x 10^12 | 7.8 |

The time is from the submission to the end of the wait, so it includes the round trip of the command and the transfer of the data. The fixed cost of a command is under 0.1 ms: the smallest size takes that in all.

What that says about the data path (4096, read in `whole_array.py`): the design streams A 8 times and B 16 times, 403 MB from host memory into the array per command, over 12 shim channels, and 16.8 MB back. That is **22.9 GB/s from the host into the array, 1.9 GB/s on each channel**. That is a third of what the transfers reach alone (see [Transfers alone](#transfers-alone)), and its cores stay at 13 % of their arithmetic peak. What bounds this design is the way it reads host memory (see [What holds the whole-array example back](#what-holds-the-whole-array-example-back)): with the host buffers laid out for reads in one run, the same multiplication takes a third of the time (see [Linear host buffers](#linear-host-buffers)).

The same multiplication of 4096 computed in fewer columns (measured on 2026/11/12; the variants for 1, 2 and 4 columns are still overlays for the whole array, run in a context of 8 columns; all results equal to the reference). The bytes streamed are computed from the access patterns of `whole_array.py`: B goes 16 times in every variant, and A 64, 32, 16 and 8 times.

| Columns (cores) | Time of a command (median) | Multiply-accumulates per second | TOPS | Per core | Host to array | On every A channel | On every B channel |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 (4) | 175.2 ms | 3.9 x 10^11 | 0.78 | 9.8 x 10^10 | 7.7 GB/s over 2 channels | 6.1 GB/s | 1.5 GB/s |
| 2 (8) | 61.1 ms | 1.1 x 10^12 | 2.3 | 1.4 x 10^11 | 13.2 GB/s over 4 | 4.4 GB/s | 2.2 GB/s |
| 4 (16) | 27.8 ms | 2.5 x 10^12 | 4.9 | 1.5 x 10^11 | 19.3 GB/s over 8 | 2.4 GB/s | 2.4 GB/s |
| 8 (32) | 17.6 ms | 3.9 x 10^12 | 7.8 | 1.2 x 10^11 | 22.9 GB/s over 12 | 1.9 GB/s | 1.9 GB/s |

What it shows:

 - The total grows less and less with the channels: 7.7, 13.2, 19.3 and 22.9 GB/s. From 4 to 8 columns the cores double and the speed grows 1.58 times, with every core doing less than before. The transfers alone reach three times that (next section). With 8 columns the reason is the strided reads of host memory (see [What holds the whole-array example back](#what-holds-the-whole-array-example-back)); the variants with fewer columns were not measured again.
 - The best core rate, 1.5 x 10^11 multiply-accumulates per second, is 86 per cycle: 17 % of the 512 of the arithmetic peak.

#### Transfers alone

Measured on 2026/11/12 with two designs of this project, built with IRON for the whole array and run in a context of 8 columns.

`_/xdna2-lab/dma_sink.py`: sixteen streams, one for every input channel of the eight shim tiles (two per tile), each ending in a core tile that takes the data in pieces of 8 KiB and throws it away. The sequence fills the first N channels at once, each from its own slice of one host buffer, and waits for all. The overlay is the same for every N. With 32 MiB per channel:

| Channels | Time of a command (median) | Host to array | Per channel |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 2.69 ms | 12.5 GB/s | 12.5 GB/s |
| 2 | 3.01 ms | 22.3 GB/s | 11.1 GB/s |
| 4 | 3.38 ms | 39.8 GB/s | 9.9 GB/s |
| 8 | 5.34 ms | 50.3 GB/s | 6.3 GB/s |
| 12 | 5.97 ms | 67.4 GB/s | 5.6 GB/s |
| 16 | 7.60 ms | 70.7 GB/s | 4.4 GB/s |

 - **One channel carries 12.4 GB/s**, and the time is proportional to the size: 0.38, 0.74, 1.36 and 2.69 ms for 4, 8, 16 and 32 MiB. The length written to the register of the channel is the number of 32-bit words asked for (read in the instruction stream).
 - **All together they level off at about 70 GB/s**, which is in the range of the memory of the laptop and not of the array.
 - A sink cannot show that the data arrives, so the rate of one stream was checked with `_/xdna2-lab/dma_loop.py` (shim, memory tile and back to the same shim, like the `passthrough_dmas` example): 16 MiB go in and come back, **identical to the input**, in 1.54 ms. That is 10.9 GB/s through one stream in each direction at the same time.
 - So a stream carries more than the 7.2 GB/s of 32 bits per cycle at 1800 MHz that these notes assumed before. Either the streams are wider or faster than that; it is not known which.

**The way back** (measured on 2026/11/16 with `_/xdna2-lab/dma_source.py`, the reverse of `dma_sink.py`: sixteen streams, each starting in a core tile that hands out its buffers as they are, into the output channels of the shim tiles, two per tile, drained into slices of one host buffer; built with `compilar_source.sh` into `_/xdna2/work/source-ch<n>-w8388608`). With 32 MiB per channel, the median of 20 runs:

| Channels | Time of a command (median) | Array to host | Per channel |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 2.51 ms | 13.4 GB/s | 13.4 GB/s |
| 2 | 2.44 ms | 27.5 GB/s | 13.8 GB/s |
| 4 | 2.98 ms | 45.1 GB/s | 11.3 GB/s |
| 8 | 5.90 ms | 45.5 GB/s | 5.7 GB/s |
| 12 | 8.07 ms | 49.9 GB/s | 4.2 GB/s |
| 16 | 9.80 ms | 54.8 GB/s | 3.4 GB/s |

 - **Writing levels off at about 55 GB/s**, against some 70 GB/s reading: four channels already give 45. Whatever the NPU writes back (C, S of the attention) costs about a quarter more per byte than what it reads.

#### The kernel alone, in one core

Measured on 2026/11/12 with `_/xdna2-lab/mm_core.py`: one core tile takes one tile of A and one of B (64 x 64, int8) from the host, runs the matrix multiplication kernel of mlir-aie (`kernels.mm`, vectorized, built with Peano) a fixed number of times on them and sends C back. A command takes 1.11 ms with 2000 products and 2.08 ms with 4000.

 - **One product of 64 x 64 x 64 takes 0.485 microseconds**: 5.4 x 10^11 multiply-accumulates per second in one core, about 300 per cycle at 1800 MHz, 59 % of the arithmetic peak. Thirty-two cores at that rate would be 1.7 x 10^13 per second (35 TOPS).
 - In the whole-array example every core does 1.2 x 10^11 per second, less than a quarter of that: the cores wait for data.
 - The values of C were not checked here: the kernel wants A and B already reordered in blocks, which the memory tiles do in the example and this design does not.

#### What holds the whole-array example back

Measured on 2026/11/12, one suspect at a time.

 - **Not the computation.** A copy of the example with the call to the kernel taken out of the cores (`_/xdna2-lab/whole_array_probe.py` with `XDNA2_SKIP_MM=1`; same instruction stream) takes the same 17.6 ms for 4096 as the original. All its time is data movement.
 - **Not the path from a memory tile to the cores.** `_/xdna2-lab/dma_fan.py`: one stream from the host, a memory tile and 1, 4 or 8 core tiles that take every tile of 64 x 64 bytes and throw it away, with and without the block reordering of the kernel. 16 MiB (4096 tiles) take 1.3 to 1.8 ms in the six variants: a tile every 0.3 to 0.45 microseconds, faster than the kernel uses them.
 - **The way it reads host memory.** `_/xdna2-lab/dma_strided.py`: one channel, 2 MiB, to a core that throws them away. Read in one run they take 0.24 ms (12.4 GB/s without the fixed cost of the command); read as the example reads B on a channel, 64 bytes of a row and then the next row, they take 0.61 ms (3.9 GB/s). The example does that on its 12 channels at once and gets 1.9 GB/s on each.

So what the host sends has to be laid out in the order the array consumes it, so that every channel reads its part in one run. For a backend that converts the weights once, that is a matter of how it stores them.

#### Linear host buffers

Measured on 2026/11/12 with the first design of this project that computes: `_/xdna2-lab/mm_linear.py` (its data and the check of its output: `_/xdna2-lab/gemm_linear.py`). Inside the array it is the whole-array example: the same fifos and the same kernel of mlir-aie, int8 in and out, 8 columns. The host side is what changes:

 - The host buffers hold A, B and C in the exact order of the streams: A by bands of m rows, every band as its tiles already in the blocks of 8 x 8 of the kernel; B by columns of the array; C by columns and row blocks. Every shim transfer is one run of consecutive bytes (read in the lowered design: every buffer descriptor has one dimension), and a band of A repeats with the repeat count of its channel.
 - The memory tiles reorder nothing: they pass the tiles on.
 - As in the example, one overlay serves every M, K and N (byte-identical from 512 to 4096).

Every result was compared with the reference computed on the CPU: **all values equal**, for every size and every tile below.

With the tiles of the example (64 x 64 x 64):

| Size | Example (median) | Linear host buffers (median) | Multiply-accumulates per second | TOPS |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 512 x 512 x 512 | 0.099 ms | 0.096 ms | 1.4 x 10^12 | 2.8 |
| 1024 x 1024 x 1024 | 0.289 ms | 0.170 ms | 6.3 x 10^12 | 12.6 |
| 2048 x 2048 x 2048 | 2.59 ms | 0.845 ms | 1.0 x 10^13 | 20.3 |
| 4096 x 4096 x 4096 | 17.6 ms | 5.83 ms (fastest 5.42) | 1.2 x 10^13 | 23.6 |

The size 4096 with other tiles (m x k x n: the rows of A, the part of K and the columns of B that a core multiplies in one call). A goes N / (8 n) times and B M / (4 m) times, so larger tiles stream fewer bytes. Times of 100 runs, median and fastest; the last column is the same design with the call to the kernel taken out of the cores (`--compute 0`), which leaves the data path alone:

| Tiles | Host to array | On every A channel and B channel | Time | TOPS | Without the multiplication |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 64 x 64 x 64 | 403 MB | 33.6 and 33.6 MB | 5.83 ms; 5.42 | 23.6; 25.4 | 5.88 ms; 5.41 |
| 128 x 64 x 64 | 268 MB | 33.6 and 16.8 MB | 5.32 ms; 4.89 | 25.8; 28.1 | 4.98 ms; 4.55 |
| 128 x 32 x 128 | 201 MB | 16.8 and 16.8 MB | 7.17 ms; 6.70 | 19.2; 20.5 | 3.38 ms; 2.91 |
| 128 x 64 x 128, one tile of C in every core (`--c-depth 1`) | 201 MB | 16.8 and 16.8 MB | 4.55 ms; 4.09 | 30.2; 33.6 | 3.39 ms; 2.97 |

What it shows:

 - **Reading host memory in runs is worth a factor of 3** at 4096, with nothing else changed: 17.6 ms become 5.83.
 - With the tiles of the example the design is still bound by the transfers, since it takes the same without the multiplication. But now they run at their ceiling: 403 MB in 5.4 to 5.9 ms are 68 to 74 GB/s from the host, what the sixteen channels reach alone.
 - Fewer bytes take less time, and the channel with the most bytes sets it: 128 x 64 x 64 halves B and leaves A as it was (5.0 ms); 128 rows and 128 columns halve both (3.4 ms). With the 12 channels at once every channel carries 5 to 7 GB/s.
 - The kernel of mlir-aie pays for a short reduction: with k = 32 the cores take 7.2 ms for what the data path delivers in 3.4, and with k = 64 the same multiply-accumulates take 4.55. In every call it loads and stores its sums for every block of C (read in `mm_aie2p.h`), so half the k per call is twice as many of those.
 - **128 x 64 x 128 is the best measured: 4.55 ms (median), 1.5 x 10^13 multiply-accumulates per second, 30 TOPS; 4.09 ms the fastest run (33.6 TOPS).** The kernel alone in 32 cores would take 3.98 ms (35 TOPS): with these tiles the design is bound by its kernel and no longer by the transfers, which would leave room up to about 40 TOPS (3.4 ms).
 - Those tiles fit because C is 8-bit: two tiles of A and two of B of 8 KiB and one of C of 16 KiB take 48 of the 64 KiB of a core. The sums of this kernel wrap around at 8 bits, which a model cannot use; with 32-bit sums the tile of C alone would take the 64 KiB.
 - The times of consecutive runs cluster in steps of about 0.45 ms (around 4.1, 4.55 and 5.0 ms in 300 runs of the last design, the last 75 of them the slowest). Not explained.
 - The placer of mlir-aie put the four A channels alone on the shim tiles 1 to 4 and the eight B channels in pairs on the tiles 0, 5, 6 and 7 (read in the lowered design).

#### Number formats, in one core

Measured on 2026/11/12 and 13 with `_/xdna2-lab/mm_core.py`, extended to every number format the matrix multiplication kernels of mlir-aie have (`kernels.mm` and `kernels.mm_bfp`): one core, tiles of 64 x 64 x 64, the same tile of A and of B multiplied 2000 and 4000 times in two commands; the difference gives the time of one product. The count now reaches the core as a runtime parameter, so one overlay serves both counts; that way the int8 kernel takes 0.529 microseconds, against the 0.485 measured with the count fixed at compile time. The values of C were not checked (see [The kernel alone, in one core](#the-kernel-alone-in-one-core)).

| What A and B hold; what the sums are | Bytes per value | One product (median) | Multiply-accumulates per second, one core | Per cycle at 1800 MHz | 32 cores at that rate |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 8-bit integers; 8-bit sums | 1 | 0.529 us | 5.0 x 10^11 | 275 | 31.7 TOPS |
| 8-bit integers; 16-bit sums | 1 | 0.568 us | 4.6 x 10^11 | 256 | 29.5 TOPS |
| Block floating point (bfp16ebs8: 8 values of 8 bits share an exponent), sums included | 1.125 | 0.587 us | 4.5 x 10^11 | 248 | 28.6 TOPS |
| 8-bit integers; 32-bit sums | 1 | 0.700 us | 3.7 x 10^11 | 208 | 24.0 TOPS |
| A and the sums in 16-bit floats, B in block floating point | 2 and 1.125 | 0.762 us | 3.4 x 10^11 | 191 | 22.0 TOPS |
| 16-bit floats multiplied through block floating point; 16-bit sums | 2 | 0.870 us | 3.0 x 10^11 | 167 | 19.3 TOPS |
| The same; 32-bit sums | 2 | 0.910 us | 2.9 x 10^11 | 160 | 18.4 TOPS |
| 16-bit integers; 16-bit sums | 2 | 1.558 us | 1.7 x 10^11 | 94 | 10.8 TOPS |
| 16-bit integers; 32-bit sums | 2 | 1.699 us | 1.5 x 10^11 | 86 | 9.9 TOPS |
| 16-bit floats, native; 16-bit sums | 2 | 5.43 us | 4.8 x 10^10 | 27 | 3.1 TOPS |
| The same; 32-bit sums | 2 | 5.54 us | 4.7 x 10^10 | 26 | 3.0 TOPS |

 - **Block floating point multiplies almost as fast as 8-bit integers**, and faster than 8-bit integers with the 32-bit sums a model needs (0.587 against 0.700 microseconds). Its kernel keeps the sums in block floating point between calls, that is, rounded to 8-bit mantissas (read in `mm_bfp.cc`); with 32-bit float sums it has not been measured, and no kernel of mlir-aie does that.
 - Wider sums cost time: 8-bit integers take 0.529, 0.568 and 0.700 microseconds with sums of 8, 16 and 32 bits.
 - 16-bit integers are 3 times slower than 8-bit ones, and native 16-bit floats 10 times. 16-bit floats that the core converts to block floating point are 1.5 times slower than block floating point that comes converted from the host.

Unpacking 4-bit weights in the core, measured on 2026/11/13 with `_/xdna2-lab/q4_core.py` and the kernel `kernels.q4nx_dequant` of mlir-aie (`aie_kernels/quant/q4nx_dequant.cc`). A packed tile of 64 outputs x 64 inputs holds a 4-bit code for every weight and, for every 32 inputs of every output, a scale and a minimum in 16-bit floats (weight = minimum + scale x code): 2560 bytes for 4096 weights, 0.625 bytes per weight. The kernel turns it into 4608 bytes of block floating point in the order the multiplication reads B.

 - **The output equals the reference of mlir-aie byte for byte.**
 - One call takes 0.637 microseconds (median; 2000 and 4000 calls): 6.4 x 10^9 weights per second in one core.
 - Unpacking a tile of B costs about as much as multiplying 64 rows of A by it (0.587). Done in the same core, a tile of B used for 64 rows takes 2.1 times the time of the multiplication alone, and for 128 rows 1.5 times.
 - So packed weights pay where the transfers bound and every weight does little work, which is the generation of tokens: there a weight is used once, and 0.625 bytes per weight instead of 1.125 are 1.8 times as many weights per second through the same channels. With large batches the cores are the limit, and the unpacking is time on top.
 - That layout is close to the 4-bit blocks of GGUF (`block_q4_K`: 4-bit codes with a scale and a minimum for every 32 weights, read in `ggml-common.h`).

#### The kernel of this project

Measured on 2026/11/13. `ggml/src/ggml-xdna/kernels/xdna2-mm-bfp16.cc` is the first kernel for the core tiles written for this project. `xdna2_mm_bfp16(A, B, C, first)` multiplies a tile of A by a tile of B, both in block floating point (bfp16ebs8), and keeps the sums of C in 32-bit floats; with `first` set it starts the sums instead of adding to them, so C is never zeroed. It is built with Peano through mlir-aie (an `ExternalFunction` of IRON) and uses the multiplier the kernel of mlir-aie uses (`mac_8x8_8x8T`: 8 x 8 values of A by 8 x 8 of B added to 64 sums, in one instruction).

What writing it showed (read in the code Peano generates, with `llvm-objdump -d` of the object):

 - A core has five registers that hold a set of 64 float sums (2048 bits; a load or a store moves 512). The first version read the sums of the next step before writing the ones of the current step, as the kernel of mlir-aie does with its 9-byte sums. Eight sets alive at once do not fit, and the compiler sent them to the stack and back: 1.19 microseconds for a product of 64 x 64 x 64, twice the kernel of mlir-aie.
 - **Reading in one run pays inside the core too.** A group of 8 blocks is 72 bytes, more than a load of 512 bits, so it is read through a stream register that has to be refilled after every jump. With A, B and C laid out in the exact order the kernel walks them it never jumps: the loop went from about 110 cycles to 71 for every step (16 rows by 16 columns of C along 64 values of K).
 - What is left is loading and storing the four sets of sums of a step, 16 loads and 16 stores, which Peano does not overlap with the next step. A longer K per call spreads that cost: 107 cycles for 128 values of K.

In one core (`_/xdna2-lab/bfpmm_core.py`; 2000 and 4000 calls, as for the other kernels):

| Version | Tiles | One call (median) | Per 64 x 64 x 64 | Multiply-accumulates per second, one core | 32 cores at that rate |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| First | 64 x 64 x 64 | 1.19 us | 1.19 us | 2.2 x 10^11 | 14.1 TOPS |
| Tiles read in one run | 64 x 64 x 64 | 0.734 us | 0.734 us | 3.6 x 10^11 | 22.9 TOPS |
| Tiles read in one run | 64 x 128 x 64 | 1.035 us | 0.518 us | 5.1 x 10^11 | 32.4 TOPS |

 - **The results are exact**: one product, and three products added up, equal the reference in every value. The reference is the product of the two operands as the kernel reads them (rounded to block floating point), computed in 64-bit floats. After 4000 products of the same tiles added up, the largest error was 1.8 x 10^-4 of the largest value (first version), which is what adding 4000 times in 32-bit floats gives.
 - With 128 values of K per call it is faster than every kernel of mlir-aie measured with sums wider than 8 bits (0.700 microseconds for 8-bit integers with 32-bit sums, 0.587 for block floating point with sums rounded to 8-bit mantissas).

On the whole array (`_/xdna2-lab/bfpmm_linear.py`; data and check: `_/xdna2-lab/gemm_bfp_linear.py`), with the data path of [Linear host buffers](#linear-host-buffers), tiles of 64 x 128 x 64 and one tile of C in every core: two tiles of A and two of B of 9216 bytes and one of C of 16384 take 53 of the 64 KiB of a core. **Every value equals the reference in every size**:

| Size | Time of a command (median) | Multiply-accumulates per second | TOPS |
|---|---|---|---|
| 512 x 512 x 512 | 0.103 ms | 1.3 x 10^12 | 2.6 |
| 1024 x 1024 x 1024 | 0.221 ms | 4.9 x 10^12 | 9.7 |
| 2048 x 2048 x 2048 | 1.054 ms | 8.2 x 10^12 | 16.3 |
| 4096 x 4096 x 4096 | 7.29 ms (fastest 6.85) | 9.4 x 10^12 | 18.8 (20.1) |

 - The size 4096 without the multiplication (`--compute 0`) takes the same, 7.33 ms: **the transfers bound it**. The design streams 453 MB from the host (A 8 times and B 16 times, at 9 bytes for 8 values) and 67 MB of sums back: 62 GB/s in. The cores alone would take 4.2 ms (32 TOPS).
 - What sets the time is the bytes of the busiest channels: each of the 12 carries 37.7 MB. Four input channels are idle. More rows and more columns per tile would send fewer bytes, but with 32-bit sums they do not fit: 128 x 64 x 128 needs the 64 KiB of a core for C alone.

#### Bands of A kept in the memory tiles

Measured on 2026/11/13 with `ggml/src/ggml-xdna/overlays/designs/bfpmm_resident.py` (data and check: `gemm_bfp_linear.py` with the layout `columns`), the kernel of this project and tiles of 64 x 128 x 64. Against the design above, the rows and the columns of cores swap roles:

 - A goes to the eight columns of cores. The host sends every band of 64 rows once, as one object. The memory tile of the column keeps it and sends it to its four cores once for every tile of C they produce with it: an object fifo with a repeat count, whose consumers take it one tile at a time. A memory tile holds one band, 295 KB of its 512 KiB for K = 4096.
 - B goes to the four rows of cores, every tile to the eight cores of its row.
 - So a pass of B through the array meets 8 bands of A instead of 4, and A is streamed once: for 4096 x 4096 x 4096 the host streams 170 MB instead of 453 (404 multiply-accumulates per streamed byte).

The array has 16 input channels in its shim tiles and 6 in every memory tile, of which the tiles of C of the four cores of a column take 4. That rules out feeding every stream of B from two host channels and keeping the bands of A in the memory tiles at once, which was the first plan. And with A streamed every time, its 4 streams and the 8 of B carry the same bytes each, so the 4 idle channels cannot be shared out evenly among them. The `merge()` of IRON, which would take one channel for several inputs, delivers the objects in the order they arrive, not in a fixed one (read in its source).

The repeat was checked alone first (`_/xdna2-lab/dma_replay.py`): a band kept by a memory tile came back to the host the right number of times, identical, and again after 20 commands in a row.

| Design | Host to array | Time (median; fastest) | TOPS | Without the multiplication |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Linear host buffers, A streamed every time | 453 MB | 7.29 ms; 6.85 | 18.8; 20.1 | 7.33 ms; 6.87 |
| Bands of A in the memory tiles | 170 MB | 5.51 ms; 5.07 | 24.9; 27.1 | 4.49 ms; 4.03 |
| The same, with 8 tiles of B held in the memory tile (`--b-depth 8`) | 170 MB | 5.32 ms; 4.88 | 25.8; 28.1 | not measured |

 - **Every value equals the reference**: 4096, and 512 after 100 commands in a row.
 - The host is no longer the limit: 170 MB in 5.3 ms are 32 GB/s, half of what the channels carry. What is left is inside the array. Every core needs a tile of A and a tile of B for every call, which at the rate of the kernel is 8.9 GB/s on each of its two streams, about what one stream carries: without the multiplication the four channels of B run at 8.4 to 9.4 GB/s. The cores alone would take 4.2 ms and the data path alone takes 4.5; together they take 5.3 to 5.5.
 - K and N are built into this overlay as mlir-aie builds it: the size of a band (m x K) and the times it repeats (N / n / 4). M is not. The repeat is made of lock values and of the length in the buffer descriptors of the memory tile (read in the lowered design), and an instruction stream of this project sets them before every multiplication, so that one overlay serves any K up to the one it was built for and any N (see [Instruction streams](#instruction-streams)).

#### The command list of the driver and firmware 1.0.0.63

With the firmware that Debian ships, every command came back in 0.1 ms with the state `abort`. Measured with the debug messages of the driver (`_/xdna2-lab/depurar_npu.sh`):

 - The driver sent the command as a list (`MSG_OP_CHAIN_EXEC_BUFFER_CF`, opcode `0x12`) in a message of 24 bytes. The request of that opcode has 16: the driver of Linux 7.3.4 sends the size of the union it shares with the newer list message (`union exec_chain_req`, read in `aie2_message.c`), which is the likely reason of the rejection. The firmware answered in 74 microseconds with 12 bytes, and the handler of the driver rejected the answer (`Message callback ret -22`): a list that failed with no failed entry.
 - The same command sent alone (`force_cmdlist=0`, which needs root; `MSG_OP_EXECUTE_BUFFER_CF`, opcode `0xc`, 80 bytes) completed in 2.4 ms with the right result. So the command, the overlay and the buffers were right, and what failed was the list path of this driver with that firmware.
 - With firmware 1.1.2.64 the driver as it is (lists) works. That firmware has the health report, so its protocol is 6.18 or later.

#### A context narrower than the partition of the overlay hangs the NPU

Measured on 2026/11/12, by mistake. The variants of the example that compute in 1, 2 or 4 columns are still built for the whole array (`aie.device(npu2)`; `partition_main.json` says `column_width` 8). The 4-column variant was run in a context of 4 columns (`num_tiles` 16):

 - The command never ended. The driver reported `timeout` after 7.3 seconds (its detection is at 2 seconds; the rest is its recovery).
 - In its recovery the driver asked the firmware for the health report of the context and got no answer in 5 seconds (`Wait for completion timeout`, `-ETIME`). It closes its management channel when that happens, and nothing reopens it while the device is awake: from then on every management message fails with `ENODEV`, and so does `CREATE_HWCTX`.
 - When the NPU suspended itself, 5 seconds later, the power command to the SMU failed (`smu cmd 4 failed, 0xff`, `Power off failed`), and it fails again at every attempt to wake the NPU up (`aie2_hw_start: failed to init smu`, `Resume failed: -22`): every query that needs the NPU fails with `EINVAL`.
 - **Reloading the driver does not bring it back, and must not be tried**: `rmmod amdxdna` blocked for good inside the kernel, in uninterruptible sleep. Read in the driver and in the kernel: `amdxdna_remove` holds the device lock of the driver (`dev_lock`) when `amdxdna_pm_fini` calls `pm_runtime_forbid`, which resumes a suspended device at once, and the resume callback takes the same lock. With a healthy NPU the PCI core has already woken the device up before that, so the reload works (it did, to load the new firmware); with an NPU that cannot wake up, it deadlocks. The blocked `rmmod` keeps the lock of the PCI device, which the shutdown of the system also needs.
 - The only way back is to restart the laptop. Measured: after the restart the NPU answers, a context is created and the matrix multiplication of 512 gives the right result again. The kernel log of the hang is in `_/xdna2/work/hang-2026-11-12@14-09-01.749/`.

The width of a context has to be the `column_width` of the partition the overlay was built for. `llama-xdna-run` now reads it from the `partition_main.json` next to the overlay and refuses any other width.

### Firmware

 - The driver of Linux 7.3.4 asks for `amdnpu/17f0_10/npu_7.sbin` first and for `npu.sbin` if that one does not exist (read in `aie2_pci.c`). It accepts protocol 6.12 or later, and switches features on by protocol version (read in `npu4_regs.c`): 6.15, the unified command message and property updates; 6.18, the health report and more than one heap chunk; 6.24, the device revision; 7, all of them.
 - Debian 13 (`firmware-amd-graphics` 20250410) ships only `npu.sbin.1.0.0.63`, SHA-256 `81831072efc3db9e0a1038cded24afd2e67a29434de550e51a19544de05ba91a`, the same file as in linux-firmware (measured).
 - linux-firmware also has `amdnpu/17f0_10/npu.sbin.1.1.2.64`, linked as `npu_7.sbin`, SHA-256 `7ed0f242f253b581eb75c8c8b34638d92fba598063b635a541beb94869245469` (measured: the same file from gitlab.com and from git.kernel.org). **Installed on the laptop on 2026/11/12** (`_/xdna2-lab/instalar_firmware_npu.sh`), next to the one of Debian; the driver loads it after a reload, without restarting.
 - Third parties report `abort` with a firmware and a driver that do not match. That is what happened here with 1.0.0.63 (see [First run on the laptop](#first-run-on-the-laptop)).

## Clock and power

Read in `npu4_regs.c`, `aie2_pm.c` and `aie2_solver.c`; this NPU uses the tables of `npu4`.

 - Eight clock levels, as (MP-NPU, H clock) in MHz: (396, 792), (600, 1056), (792, 1152), (975, 1267), (975, 1267), (1056, 1408), (1152, 1584), (1267, 1800).
 - The driver computes the capacity as `4096 x columns x H clock in MHz / 1000000` TOPS: 4 core tiles per column, 512 multiply-accumulates per cycle each, 2 operations per multiply-accumulate. With 8 columns at 1800 MHz that is 58 TOPS.
 - The level follows the quality of service that the contexts ask for. **A context that passes no quality-of-service hints gets the highest level.** One that passes `gops` together with `fps` or `latency` gets the lowest level that meets them.
 - The power modes (`DEFAULT`, `LOW`, `MEDIUM`, `HIGH`, `TURBO`) are set with `SET_STATE`, so they need root. `TURBO` also switches the clock gating off, and cannot be set while a context exists.

Levels 5 and 7 are 28 % apart (1408 against 1800 MHz). zest saw its speed settle at one of two values about 25 % apart: the clock level is the first suspect.

## Toolchain

Measured on the development laptop on 2026/11/12: the whole chain works on Debian 13 as a normal user, without XRT.

 - `mlir_aie` 1.4.5 and `llvm-aie` (Peano, the compiler for the core tiles) 22.0.0.2026102001, installed with pip in a Python 3.13 virtual environment. The wheels need glibc 2.35 or later.
 - The last step of a build packs the overlay into an xclbin with `xclbinutil`, which the wheels do not bring. The sources of mlir-aie do: `third_party/hrx-xclbinutil` is a trimmed copy that needs neither XRT nor Boost. Built with CMake and copied as `xclbinutil` next to `aiecc`, the build completes.
 - Environment: `MLIR_AIE_INSTALL_DIR` and `PEANO_INSTALL_DIR` point to the two packages, `NPU2=1`, and their `bin`, `python` and `lib` folders go in `PATH`, `PYTHONPATH` and `LD_LIBRARY_PATH`. The script of mlir-aie that sets this up asks `xrt-smi` for the NPU, so here it is done by hand.
 - mlir-aie keeps a cache of what it builds in `~/.npu/cache` unless `NPU_CACHE_HOME` names another folder (read in `aie/utils/compile/__init__.py`).
 - A design is compiled without running it with `--dev npu2 --xclbin-path <file> --insts-path <file>`. The official whole-array matrix multiplication (int8, 8 columns, 512 x 512 x 512) builds its 32 core programs in a few seconds.
 - What a build leaves: the xclbin, the instruction stream (`insts.bin`, 7.8 KB in that example) and, in its project folder, the overlay (`main.pdi`, 86.5 KB) with the description of the kernel (`kernels_main.json`), of the partition (`partition_main.json`: 8 columns from column 0) and of the memory.

For the direct access to the driver only `main.pdi` and `insts.bin` are needed: the xclbin is a container for XRT.

## Instruction streams

Read in mlir-aie 1.4.5 (`include/aie/Runtime/TxnEncoding.h`, the passes in `lib/Dialect/AIEX/Transforms/` and its register database, `aie_registers_aie2.json`) and in the streams it generates for this NPU. This project writes them itself in `ggml/src/ggml-xdna/xdna-insts.cpp`.

A command carries, next to the overlay, an instruction stream: a list of register writes and waits that the firmware of the NPU carries out. It is a header of four 32-bit words and then the operations:

| Operation | Words | Layout |
|---|---|---|
| Header | 4 | version 0.1, device generation 4 and rows, as `major, minor << 8, generation << 16, rows << 24`; `columns, rows of memory tiles << 8`; number of operations; bytes of the stream |
| Write | 6 | `0, 0, address, 0, value, 24` |
| Block write | 4 + n | `1, 0, address, bytes of the operation, n values`: n consecutive registers |
| Mask write | 7 | `3, 0, address, 0, value, mask, 28` |
| Mask poll | 7 | `4, 0, address, 0, value, mask, 28`: waits until the bits of the mask are the ones of the value |
| Sync | 4 | `128, 16, direction, row << 8, column << 16`; `rows << 8, columns << 16, channel << 24`: waits for the token a channel issues at the end of a task that asked for one |
| Address patch | 12 | `129, 48, 0, 0, 0, 0, address, 0, buffer, 0, offset (low), offset (high)`: the register takes the address of that buffer of the command, plus the offset |

An address is `column << 25, row << 20` and the register of the tile. The registers a sequence of transfers uses, in AIE2P:

| Tile | Register | What it is |
|---|---|---|
| Shim (row 0) | `0x1d000 + 0x20 n` | Buffer descriptor n, of 16: 8 words. For a run of consecutive bytes: length in 32-bit words, address, 0, 0, `0xc0000000`, `0x02000000`, 0, `0x02000000` |
| Shim | `0x1d200 + 8 c`, `0x1d210 + 8 c` | Control of the channel c towards the host and from the host. Bits 8 to 12: who gets its tokens (15: the firmware) |
| Shim | control `+ 4` | Queue of the channel: a write queues a task. `descriptor, (repeats - 1) << 16, token << 31` |
| Shim | `0x1d220 + 4 c`, `0x1d228 + 4 c` | Status of the same channels. Bits 20 to 22: tasks in the queue, 4 at most |
| Memory (row 1) | `0xa0000 + 0x20 n` | Buffer descriptor n, of 48. Word 0: length in words (17 bits). Word 1: address in words (its own memory starts at byte `0x80000`), bit 19 and bits 20 to 25: the descriptor that follows. Word 7: `valid << 31, value given << 24, lock given << 16, takes a lock << 15, value taken << 8, lock taken`, the values with sign in 7 bits and its own locks numbered from 64 |
| Memory | `0xa0600 + 8 c`, `0xa0630 + 8 c` | Control of the channel c into memory and out of it. Bit 1: reset. The queue is 4 bytes after it |
| Memory | `0xc0000 + 0x10 n` | Value of the lock n (6 bits) |
| Core (rows 2 to 5) | address in its data memory | The data memory itself: the runtime parameters of a core are written there |
| Core | `0x1f000 + 0x10 n` | Value of the lock n |

How a sequence of transfers becomes a stream, which is how mlir-aie does it and how this project does it too:

 - A transfer between the host and a shim channel is three or four operations: the block write of a descriptor, the patch of its address, a mask write of the control when it asks for a token, and the write to the queue.
 - The descriptor is the lowest free one of the shim tile. It goes back when the transfers of its row block are waited for.
 - A push onto a full queue is lost and its transfer never runs. Before a push, when 4 pushes of the channel are not known to have ended, the stream waits for room: a mask poll of bit 22 of the status. A sync retires the oldest push that issues a token and the ones before it.
 - The transfers of four row blocks are queued at once: those of a row block are waited for after the next three are queued.

**The generator of this project.** `fXdnaGemmPlan` (`xdna-gemm.cpp`) takes the manifest of an overlay and the M, K and N of a multiplication, and gives the instruction stream and the bytes of the three buffers. The manifest (`xdna-overlay.h`) says where the placer of mlir-aie put every stream and every core; `ggml/src/ggml-xdna/overlays/designs/overlay_manifest.py` writes it from the build. `llama-xdna-insts gemm` writes a stream to a file and `llama-xdna-insts dump` prints the operations of any stream.

Checked on 2026/11/13, without the NPU: **its streams are identical, byte for byte, to the ones mlir-aie generates**, in 11 cases. With linear host buffers: 512, 1024, 2048 and 4096 (square), 768 x 384 x 1024, 256 x 128 x 512 and 2304 x 1536 x 3072. With the bands of A in the memory tiles: 512, 4096 (two overlays) and 1536 x 256 x 768.

**Setting the bands of A at run time.** With it one overlay with the bands of A in the memory tiles serves any K whose band fits its buffer and any N: before the transfers, for each of the 8 memory tiles, the stream resets the two channels of the band, writes the length of its two descriptors and the lock values of the one that receives it, sets its two locks and queues its two tasks again. That is the order in which mlir-aie re-arms a fifo that lives in a memory tile (`AIELowerDmaChannelReset.cpp`).

 - Checked without the NPU (`_/xdna2-lab/rearm_check.py`): for the K and the N an overlay was built for, the 56 values the stream writes are the ones the overlay itself configured (its configuration as register writes: `aiecc --get-txn`), in two overlays; and after those operations the stream is identical to the one of mlir-aie.
 - **Measured on the NPU on 2026/11/13** (`_/xdna2-lab/probar_rearme.sh`, five commands, every result compared with the reference): **one overlay, built for K = 4096 and N = 4096, multiplied 512 x 512 x 512, 1024 x 2048 x 1024 and 4096 x 4096 x 4096 with every value equal to the reference.** The overlay built for 512 also gave the right result with the bands set again to its own sizes, once and 20 times in a row.
 - It costs about 0.05 ms per command: 512 takes 0.157 ms (median of 20) against 0.111 ms without it. At 4096 it does not show: 5.28 ms (median of 100; fastest 4.94), 26 TOPS.
 - Limits: a lock counts up to 63, so a band repeats 63 times at most (N up to 16128 with tiles of 64 columns); the band has to fit the buffer of the overlay (K up to 4096 in the one built for 4096).

## From floats to floats

Written on 2026/11/13: `XdnaRunner` (`ggml/src/ggml-xdna/xdna-runner.cpp`) multiplies two matrices of 32-bit floats on the NPU and gives 32-bit floats back, with nothing of Python or mlir-aie at run time. It is the piece a backend calls.

 - **The conversion** (`xdna-bfp16.cpp`): 8 floats become a block of bfp16ebs8. The exponent is the one of the largest of the 8; every mantissa is the value over the step of the block, in 8 bits with sign. Two ways of rounding: towards minus infinity, which is what the encoder of mlir-aie does by default, and to the nearest. A value that is not finite stops the conversion.
 - **Rounding to the nearest is what the runner should use with real data.** Towards minus infinity, a negative zero, or any negative value more than 31 binades below the largest of its block, becomes -1 step instead of 0 (read in `bfp.py` of mlir-aie and measured; the "conv_even" of that encoder does the same): 0.8 to 1.6 % of the largest value of the block, for a value that is zero.
 - **The layout** (`fXdnaGemmPackA`, `fXdnaGemmPackB`, `fXdnaGemmUnpackC` in `xdna-gemm.cpp`): A, and B given transposed (one row of K values for every output, which is how a model keeps its weights), go into the buffers in the order the array consumes them, and C comes back to rows of N values.
 - **Any size**: M, K and N are rounded up to what the overlay takes at a time, and what is added is zeros in A and in B.
 - **The weights are converted once** and stay in a buffer of the NPU; A is converted for every multiplication.

Checked on 2026/11/13 without the NPU (`llama-xdna-gemm pack` and `unpack`):

 - The converted A and B are identical, byte for byte, to the ones the encoder of mlir-aie and `_/xdna2-lab/gemm_bfp_linear.py` give: both layouts, both ways of rounding, square sizes and others (512 x 512 x 512, 768 x 384 x 1024, 1024 x 256 x 768, 512 x 640 x 1280).
 - With values chosen to be hard (magnitudes over 80 binades, zeros, negative zeros, denormals, values one step under a power of two): rounding towards minus infinity is identical to mlir-aie; rounding to the nearest is identical to that rounding computed apart, and differs from the "conv_even" of mlir-aie only in the negative values described above.
 - 300 x 1000 x 700 converts to the same bytes as the same matrices filled with zeros by hand to 512 x 1024 x 768.
 - C is read back identical to the reference from results the NPU had given before, in both layouts (512, 2048, 4096 and 1024 x 2048 x 1024).

**Measured on the NPU on 2026/11/13** with `llama-xdna-gemm` (`_/xdna2-lab/probar_gemm.sh`, five steps): random matrices, values between -1 and 1, rounded to the nearest, on the overlay built for 4096 with its bands set at run time. After the runs, 16 rows of C are compared with the product of the two operands as the NPU reads them, computed in 64-bit floats. **Every value checked equals the reference, in the five steps.**

| Multiplication | On the NPU as | Runs | Writing the stream | Converting A | NPU (median) | Reading C |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 512 x 512 x 512 | the same | 20 | 0.004 ms | 2.2 to 3.5 ms | 0.216 ms | 0.07 ms |
| 300 x 1000 x 700 | 512 x 1024 x 768 | 1 | 0.26 ms | 3.1 ms | 4.3 ms (first command) | 0.31 ms |
| 1024 x 2048 x 1024 | the same | 20 | 0.02 ms | 19 ms | 0.525 ms | 0.4 to 0.5 ms |
| 4096 x 4096 x 4096 | the same | 20 | 0.03 ms | 136 ms | 5.38 ms (fastest 4.93) | 8.5 to 8.8 ms |

 - The whole path works from C++, with sizes that are not multiples of what the overlay takes (300 x 1000 x 700) and with 20 multiplications in a row in one context.
 - The first stream of a size takes longer to write (0.2 to 9 ms) than the following ones.
 - **The conversion of A is what takes the time now**: 8 to 9 nanoseconds per value, in a plain loop, which is 10 to 37 times the time of the NPU. Reading C back takes as long as the NPU from 1024 on. Both have to be much faster for the NPU to pay.
 - With these values the conversion to block floating point loses 0.55 % of the root mean square of the exact product. Values of a model are not uniform: see [The backend of ggml](#the-backend-of-ggml) for what it loses with one.

**The conversions, faster (2026/11/13).** Written after those measures, and not yet run on the NPU:

 - **The encoder** (`fXdnaBfp16EncodeNearest` in `xdna-bfp16.cpp`) rounds to the nearest with integers only: a mantissa is the 24 bits of the value over a power of two, rounded to the nearest and, half way, to the even one. The 8 values of a block go in one 256-bit register (AVX2, chosen when the program runs; without it, the same arithmetic value by value). It takes the blocks at a stride, so the 8 rows of 8 columns that are consecutive in a tile are one call.
 - **Threads** (`XdnaWorkers`, `xdna-workers.cpp`): a band of A, a tile column of B and what a column of cores gives of C in a row block are each a stretch of their buffer, so they are shared between threads without anything else changing. The threads are kept between multiplications, and a matrix of less than 262,144 values is not shared: waking them would cost more.
 - **Zeros**: a tile that is whole outside the matrix is written, or skipped when reading, at once. A multiplication of one row is 511 rows of them.
 - **The decoder** multiplies the mantissas by the value of the exponent, taken from a table of the 256 there are, in the place of one `ldexp` for every value: the same bits for the 65,536 pairs of exponent and mantissa.

Measured with `llama-xdna-gemm pack` and `unpack` (the best of 5, uniform values, no NPU):

| Conversion | Before | Now, 1 thread | Now, 8 threads |
|---|---|---|---|
| A, or B, of 4096 x 4096 | 136 ms (8.1 ns per value) | 9.1 ms (0.54 ns) | 2.0 ms (0.12 ns) |
| B of 16,128 x 1024 | about 140 ms | 10.7 ms | 2.2 ms |
| A of 512 x 3584 | about 15 ms | 0.92 ms | 0.39 ms |
| A of 512 x 1024 | about 4.5 ms | 0.32 ms | 0.085 ms |
| A of 1 x 1024, as 512 rows | about 4.5 ms | 0.025 ms | not shared |
| Reading C of 4096 x 4096 | about 8 ms | 8.1 ms | 3.5 ms |
| Reading C of 512 x 3584 | not measured | 0.34 ms | 0.09 ms |

 - **What they write is the same**: the fast encoder against the one that was there, in 24.6 million blocks (uniform exponents, exponents close together, ties, denormals, zeros, negative zeros and values that are not finite); A and B, byte for byte, in 144 conversions (9 sizes, the two layouts, the two ways of rounding, uniform values and values chosen to be hard, 1 and 8 threads); C, float for float, in 36 readings.
 - 16 threads are no faster than 8: the laptop has 8 cores.
 - Reading C is bound by memory, not by arithmetic: 67 MB in and 67 MB out for 4096 x 4096.

## The backend of ggml

Written on 2026/11/13: `ggml/src/ggml-xdna/ggml-xdna.cpp`, the piece that makes llama.cpp use the NPU. It is the smallest backend that runs a real model: it gives the NPU the multiplications by the matrices of weights and leaves everything else to the processor.

**How it fits in ggml** (read in `ggml-backend.cpp`, `llama-model.cpp`, `llama-model-loader.cpp` and `llama-context.cpp` of this fork):

 - It is an **accelerator device** (`GGML_BACKEND_DEVICE_TYPE_ACCEL`), as the BLAS backend of llama.cpp was. llama.cpp adds the backends of that type to every context, in front of the processor, and the scheduler gives each operation to the first backend that says it can do it (`supports_op`) and can use the buffers of its operands (`supports_buft`).
 - **Its buffers are host memory** with the layout of ggml (`is_host`), so the processor uses them as its own and no tensor is copied between the two backends.
 - **It has a buffer type of its own all the same**, for two reasons. When llama.cpp loads a model it asks every accelerator, with a multiplication of 512 rows, whether it takes the operation of each weight, and puts in the buffers of the accelerator the weights it takes: that keeps them out of the buffers where the processor repacks weights for its own kernels, which no other backend can read. And the backend learns when the weights of a model are freed, to free what the NPU kept of them.
 - Weights that llama.cpp puts in its buffers are read from the file into memory, not mapped: a model takes its size in memory, plus what the NPU keeps.

**What it takes**: `GGML_OP_MUL_MAT` when the first operand is a whole matrix of weights that lives in one of its buffers (two dimensions, not a view, of 32-bit floats or of any type that ggml converts to floats row by row: F16, BF16 and the quantized ones) and the second is rows of 32-bit floats. The check (`fMulMatTaken`) covers everything the conversion and the multiplication rely on, as the kernels check nothing. It does not take:

 - a multiplication marked as the fast transform of a rotation (`GGML_HINT_SRC0_IS_HADAMARD`);
 - a multiplication whose rows the model wants kept with more than 8 bits (`ggml_prec_get_src` gives F32, BF16 or F16): the rows are rounded to block floating point, 8 bits with a shared exponent;
 - a multiplication of fewer rows than `GGML_XDNA_MIN_BATCH` (40 if not set; 72 until 2026/11/16): with fewer the processor alone is faster (see the measures below and [Small batches, and the weights in the mapping of the file](#small-batches-and-the-weights-in-the-mapping-of-the-file)); from 1 to 8 rows the overlay for generating tokens takes them;
 - a matrix that is not in its buffers: the output matrix of a model that shares it with the table of embeddings, for example, which llama.cpp keeps with the processor;
 - with an overlay that gives C in bfloat16, the multiplications that give the logits of the vocabulary (`fGivesLogits`: the weights llama.cpp names `output.weight`, `token_embd.weight` or `blk.<n>.nextn.shared_head_head.weight`): rounded to bfloat16, they cost the model as much precision as all the other multiplications together (see [C in bfloat16](#c-in-bfloat16)).

**What it does with one**:

 - **The weights are converted the first time they are used**: to floats with the function of ggml for their type, and to block floating point, rounded to the nearest, into a buffer of the NPU (`XdnaRunner::mLoadWeights`). They stay there until the buffer of the model they live in is freed. Only what a model keeps as weights (`GGML_BACKEND_BUFFER_USAGE_WEIGHTS`) stays converted; anything else is converted for the one multiplication.
 - **A matrix larger than one multiplication of the overlay goes in pieces** (`fXdnaGemmLimits`): with the overlay in use, up to 4,096 inputs (what fits of a band of A in a memory tile) and 16,128 outputs (63 sends of a band, what a lock counts). The pieces along the outputs fill different columns of the result, and the ones along the inputs are added up on the host. They are cut at multiples of 8 inputs, so the blocks that share an exponent are the same as without cutting.
 - **The rows go in commands of 4,096 at most**, converted for each one. A tensor whose rows are not one after the other is gathered first.
 - **Multiplications that multiply the same rows go together** (`fGraphCompute`, since 2026/11/13): the ones that follow one another in the part of the graph the backend gets, with only views between them, become one matrix with the outputs of all, converted and kept as one, so the rows are converted once and the NPU gets one command. Every result gets its outputs where it is (`XdnaGemmTarget`): the reading of C writes each column into the tensor it belongs to. With Qwen3.5 0.8B that makes 96 commands per batch instead of 183: `attn_qkv`, `attn_gate`, `ssm_beta` and `ssm_alpha` (8,224 outputs), `attn_q`, `attn_k` and `attn_v` (5,120), `ffn_gate` and `ffn_up` (7,168). The first two groups only follow one another because `src/models/qwen35.cpp` of this fork puts the projections into the graph as soon as they are built (`ggml_build_forward_expand`); otherwise the operations of the processor that use each one come between them, and the backend gets them in different calls.
 - **One context of the NPU for the whole process**, opened by the first backend and closed when no backend and no converted weights are left. One multiplication runs at a time.
 - **A command that does not end fails the graph**, and nothing else is sent to the NPU by that process (see [A context narrower than the partition of the overlay hangs the NPU](#a-context-narrower-than-the-partition-of-the-overlay-hangs-the-npu)). A value that is not finite, in the weights or in the rows, fails it too.

**Where it finds the overlay**: `xdna2-gemm-bfp16.pdi` and `xdna2-gemm-bfp16.manifest`, in the folder of the program or in the one `GGML_XDNA_OVERLAY_DIR` names; never in the current folder, as a wrong overlay can leave the NPU unusable until the computer is powered off. They are the `main.pdi` of the build of the overlay and the manifest `overlay_manifest.py` writes for it; CMake does not build them yet. The manifest says whether C comes back in 32-bit floats or in bfloat16 (`output f32|bf16`), and the backend, the emulation included, follows it. When the backend is registered it reads the manifest and queries the NPU (it creates nothing in it; about 0.1 s): if either fails it registers no device, says why, and the programs run on the processor.

| Variable | What it does |
|---|---|
| `GGML_XDNA_DISABLE` | Any value but 0: the backend registers no device |
| `GGML_XDNA_DEVICE` | Device node of the NPU; `/dev/accel/accel0` if not set |
| `GGML_XDNA_OVERLAY_DIR` | Folder of the overlay and its manifest; the folder of the program if not set |
| `GGML_XDNA_MIN_BATCH` | Rows a multiplication needs for the backend to take it, and the tokens an attention of a batch needs; 40 if not set (72 until 2026/11/16; 1 sends every multiplication to the NPU) |
| `GGML_XDNA_COPY_WEIGHTS` | The weights of a model copied into buffers of the backend instead of left in the mapping of its file (see [Small batches, and the weights in the mapping of the file](#small-batches-and-the-weights-in-the-mapping-of-the-file)) |
| `GGML_XDNA_NO_ATTN`, `GGML_XDNA_NO_ATTN_OVERLAP` | The attention of a batch with the processor; or on the NPU with its heads of keys one after the other (see [The attention of a batch on the NPU, as two multiplications](#the-attention-of-a-batch-on-the-npu-as-two-multiplications)) |
| `GGML_XDNA_NO_CONV`, `GGML_XDNA_NO_CONV_FOLD` | The short convolution of a batch with its SiLU, and the copy of its states, with the processor; or on the host after the `CONCAT` instead of while the C of the projection is read (see [The short convolution while C is read](#the-short-convolution-while-c-is-read)) |
| `GGML_XDNA_WEIGHTS_GB` | The memory, in GB, the copies of the weights for the NPU may take; by default 40 % of host memory for copies beside the mapped file, and up to 76 % across total NPU buffers when `MADV_DONTNEED` drops the mapped file pages of 3D experts (see [A model larger than the memory left for its copies: Qwen3.8 27B](#a-model-larger-than-the-memory-left-for-its-copies-qwen38-27b) and [Dense projections and shared experts of a mixture of experts](#dense-projections-and-shared-experts-of-a-mixture-of-experts)) |
| `GGML_XDNA_NO_MOE`, `GGML_XDNA_MOE_MIN_TOKENS` | The routed experts (`MUL_MAT_ID`) of a mixture of experts on the processor (`GGML_XDNA_NO_MOE=1`); or the tokens a batch needs for them to go to the NPU (1 if not set; see [Routed experts on the NPU](#routed-experts-on-the-npu)) |
| `GGML_XDNA_NO_MOE_DENSE`, `GGML_XDNA_MOE_ATTN_OUT` | Leave the dense projections and shared experts of a mixture of experts on the processor when generating (`GGML_XDNA_NO_MOE_DENSE=1`), or also send `ssm_out` and `attn_output` of MoE layers to the NPU (`GGML_XDNA_MOE_ATTN_OUT=1`; off by default, see [Dense projections and shared experts of a mixture of experts](#dense-projections-and-shared-experts-of-a-mixture-of-experts)) |
| `GGML_XDNA_MOE_GEMV` | With a mixture of experts, send every dense multiplication of a few rows through `xdna2-gemv-chain` as before `1.(b)` (see [A mixture of experts: Qwen3.6 35B-A3B](#a-mixture-of-experts-qwen36-35b-a3b)) |
| `GGML_XDNA_EMULATE` | Any value but 0: the processor does what the NPU would do; the NPU is not opened |

**The emulation** (`GGML_XDNA_EMULATE`) runs the same backend with the processor in the place of the NPU: the weights and the rows are rounded to block floating point with the same encoder and the sums are added up in 32-bit floats, so the results differ from the ones of the NPU only in the order of the sums. It is slow, and it is there for two things: to check the backend without the risk of hanging the NPU, and to measure what a number format costs in precision with a real model.

When a backend is freed it reports what it did: multiplications, rows, commands, and the time in the NPU, converting rows, reading results, writing instruction streams and converting weights. The programs of llama.cpp show the messages of the libraries from `-lv 4` on, and the name of every matrix converted with `-lv 5`.

**Checked on 2026/11/13 without the NPU**, emulating, with Qwen3.5 0.8B (`ggml-org/Qwen3.5-0.8B-GGUF`: Q8_0 and BF16, and the BF16 turned into F32 with `llama-quantize`) and Qwen3.5 2B (`unsloth/Qwen3.5-2B-GGUF`, Q8_0). **The backend has not run a model on the NPU yet**: `_/xdna2-lab/probar_backend.sh` has the steps for that.

 - **What reaches the backend** with Qwen3.5 0.8B: 187 matrices of weights, 752 million weights, in eight shapes (inputs x outputs): 1024 x 16, 1024 x 512, 1024 x 2048, 1024 x 3584, 1024 x 4096, 1024 x 6144, 2048 x 1024 and 3584 x 1024, plus the output matrix, 1024 x 248,320, in 16 pieces. llama.cpp loads that one twice: for the processor, which looks the embeddings up in it, and for the backend, which multiplies by it. A token is 187 multiplications and 202 commands.
 - **With 2B** the matrices of 6144 inputs go in two pieces (4096 and 2048 inputs), whose results are added up.
 - **The same text as the processor**: 32 tokens of 0.8B and 24 of 2B, greedy, from the same prompt.
 - **What block floating point loses**, with `llama-perplexity --kl-divergence` over 2,048 tokens of English technical text (8 chunks of 512, in batches of 512), against the processor with the model in F32:

| What multiplies | Weights of the model | Perplexity | KL divergence, mean | Same most likely token |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Processor | F32 (the reference) | 24.07 | | |
| Processor | BF16 | 24.09 | 0.000030 | 99.51 % |
| Processor | Q8_0 | 24.10 | 0.001205 | 97.45 % |
| Backend, emulated | BF16 | 24.12 | 0.001244 | 97.75 % |
| Backend, emulated | Q8_0 | 23.98 | 0.001693 | 97.40 % |
| Backend, **NPU** (measured on 2026/11/13) | BF16 | 24.15 | 0.001239 | 97.40 % |

 - **Block floating point costs what Q8_0 costs on the processor**: with the weights of the BF16 model, the backend is as far from the reference as the processor is with the Q8_0 model. The processor, with quantized weights, rounds the rows to 8 bits too.
 - With the weights in Q8_0 the two losses add up, and the backend is 1.4 times as far as the processor with the same file.
 - **On the NPU the loss is the one the emulation predicted** (`_/xdna2-lab/probar_backend.sh 7`): KL divergence 0.001239 against the processor with F32, where the emulation gave 0.001244.
 - The NPU and the emulation are not as close to each other as the order of their sums alone would make them: KL divergence 0.000744 and the same most likely token in 98.09 % of the tokens. Both are equally far from the reference, so neither of them adds an error the other does not have. **Measured** (2026/11/13, without the NPU): the emulation built with 8 partial sums in its dot product instead of 16 (`XDNA_EMULATION_LANES=8`), which changes nothing but the order of its sums, is as far from the emulation of 16: KL divergence 0.000736 and the same token in 97.94 %. What happens: the sums of two implementations differ in their last bits, the rows of the next layer are rounded to 8 bits from slightly different values, and the few roundings that change move values by a whole step, which changes more roundings in the layers that follow. Two implementations that round their activations, in the same way but with their sums in another order, end up with partly different rounding errors of the same size. The processor does the same with its own 8-bit activations (see `RulesForThisProjectOnly.md`, «Validar un backend de dispositivo frente a la CPU»).
 - **Speed** (`llama-bench`, Qwen3.5 0.8B Q8_0, 8 threads, measured on 2026/11/13): with every multiplication on the NPU, it is faster than the processor alone from 80 tokens per batch on and slower under 64, and the generation of tokens falls from 53 to 11 tokens per second, because a multiplication of one row runs as one of 512. Leaving the multiplications of fewer than 72 rows with the processor, which is the default since then (`GGML_XDNA_MIN_BATCH`), it is never slower than the processor alone:

| Tokens per batch | 32 | 48 | 64 | 80 | 96 | 128 | 256 | 512 | Generation |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Processor alone | 557 | 610 | 631 | 585 | 598 | 659 | 633 | 592 | 53.0 |
| NPU for every multiplication | 292 | | 510 | | | 831 | 1293 | 1481 | 11.0 |
| NPU from 2 rows on | 342 | 464 | 591 | 686 | 754 | 946 | 1459 | 1601 | 53.3 |
| NPU from 72 rows on (the default) | | | 631 | | | | | 1577 | 54.7 |

 - The NPU costs about 90 ms per batch of tokens, almost whatever its size, while the processor costs about 1.7 ms per token: that sets where the NPU starts to pay. In tokens per second the units are tokens of the prompt read, except in the last column.
 - From 2 rows on, the output matrix stays with the processor when only the last token needs it, as in `llama-bench` and in the generation of text: 16 commands fewer per batch, and 8 % more with 512 tokens.
 - The processor reads the weights from the buffers of the backend as fast as from its own: the generation is the same, 53 to 55 tokens per second.
 - With batches of 512 tokens the NPU runs a pass of 512 tokens of perplexity in 0.42 s, where the processor alone takes 0.79 s with the same BF16 file: 1.9 times as fast. The backend took 202 commands per pass: 65 ms of NPU, 29 ms converting rows and 60 ms reading results, most of them the 512 rows of the output matrix (248,320 outputs, 508 MB of floats); the rest of the 0.42 s is the processor running the other operations of the model.
 - **The conversion of the weights**, on the first use of each matrix: 752 million weights in 0.23 s with 8 threads, emulating (7.0 s before the conversions were made faster, see [From floats to floats](#from-floats-to-floats)); the 1,881 million of 2B, in 0.73 s.
 - The emulation takes 0.38 s for a token of 0.8B and 18 s for a batch of 512.

**Measured on the NPU on 2026/11/13**: the shapes of the model, with 512 rows, the ten of them one after the other in one context, twice, with `llama-xdna-gemm` (`_/xdna2-lab/probar_backend.sh 4`, 8 threads for the host). Every value checked equals the reference. The times are those of the second round; the first command of the context took 4.6 ms.

| Multiplication (M x K x N) | Used in Qwen3.5 0.8B by | NPU | TOPS | Converting A | Reading C |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 512 x 1024 x 16 (as 512 x 1024 x 256) | `ssm_alpha`, `ssm_beta` (36) | 0.150 ms | 0.11 | 0.10 ms | 0.01 ms |
| 512 x 1024 x 512 | `attn_k`, `attn_v` (12) | 0.139 ms | 3.9 | 0.10 ms | 0.05 ms |
| 512 x 1024 x 2048 | `attn_gate` (18) | 0.203 ms | 10.6 | 0.10 ms | 0.21 ms |
| 512 x 1024 x 3584 | `ffn_up`, `ffn_gate` (48) | 0.277 ms | 13.6 | 0.14 ms | 0.37 ms |
| 512 x 1024 x 4096 | `attn_q` (6) | 0.297 ms | 14.5 | 0.14 ms | 0.45 ms |
| 512 x 1024 x 6144 | `attn_qkv` (18) | 0.391 ms | 16.5 | 0.14 ms | 0.72 ms |
| 512 x 2048 x 1024 | `ssm_out`, `attn_output` (24) | 0.196 ms | 11.0 | 0.30 ms | 0.11 ms |
| 512 x 3584 x 1024 | `ffn_down` (24) | 0.262 ms | 14.3 | 0.48 ms | 0.11 ms |
| 512 x 1024 x 6400 | the last piece of the output matrix | 0.401 ms | 16.7 | 0.13 ms | 0.70 ms |
| 512 x 1024 x 16128 | the other 15 pieces of the output matrix | 0.906 ms | 18.7 | 0.15 ms | 2.11 ms |

 - A command costs about 0.1 ms whatever its size: the multiplications of the model are small for the NPU, and only the widest pass 15 TOPS.
 - For a batch of 512 tokens, the 187 multiplications of the layers would take about 44 ms of NPU, and converting rows and reading results about as much again on the host, one after the other: the host does not overlap with the NPU yet.
