 - Reading C is now the largest cost of the host for the wide matrices (2.1 ms for 16,128 outputs against 0.9 ms of NPU): it is bound by memory.

**Where the time of a command goes** (measured on 2026/11/13): the same overlay built without the multiplication (`bfpmm_resident.py --compute 0 --b-depth 8`, in `_/xdna2/work/bfpres-4096-b8-c0`, with the same placement and so the same manifest) takes as long as the real one with every shape of the model: 0.129 against 0.139 ms with 512 x 1024 x 512, 0.285 against 0.277 with 512 x 1024 x 3584, 0.388 against 0.391 with 512 x 1024 x 6144, 0.900 against 0.906 with 512 x 1024 x 16128. **The multiplication is hidden: what sets the time is the fixed cost of a command, about 0.11 ms, and the data that goes between memory and the NPU.**

 - With 512 x 1024 x 16128, every turn of 256 columns moves 828 KB (295 KB of weights in, 524 KB of C out and the share of A) in 12.6 µs: **66 GB/s, the ceiling of the NPU towards memory**, which the transfers alone gave here (about 70 GB/s with the 16 channels, see [Transfers alone](#transfers-alone)) and which a third party also measured (65.3 GB/s, https://destevez.net/2026/08/x-engine-correlator-on-a-ryzen-npu/). Every channel, in and out, carries 5 to 6 GB/s.
 - **C is 64 % of those bytes**: the sums come back as 32-bit floats, 4 bytes for every value, against 1.125 for a weight.
 - Bytes per multiply-accumulate set the ceiling: with 512 rows and K = 1024, a multiply-accumulate moves 0.0061 bytes (0.0022 of weights, 0.0039 of C and a little of A), so 66 GB/s feed at most 10.8 x 10^12 multiply-accumulates per second, **about 21 TOPS whatever the kernel**. 50 TOPS need 450 multiply-accumulates or more for every byte that goes to or from memory; now there are about 162.
 - What would cut those bytes: C in 16 bits (bf16, half of its bytes) or in block floating point (1.125 bytes per value); every weight used for more rows before it is sent again (it goes again for every block of 512 rows now); weights of 4 bits (0.625 bytes instead of 1.125); and fewer commands, larger ones, for the fixed 0.11 ms.

**The ceiling of the multiplier** (measured on 2026/11/13 without the NPU, from the code Peano generates, `_/xdna2-lab/peak_bfp16.cc`): a core issues one multiply instruction of 8 x 8 x 8 values in bfp16 per cycle, 512 multiply-accumulates, as with int8, so the array peak is the same 59 TOPS. A set of 64 sums in 32-bit floats can only take another multiply 3 cycles later (with int8 sums, 2), so it takes 3 sets that alternate: with 2 the loop gives 67 % of the cycles, with 3 or 4 it gives 89 %, the rest being loads Peano does not overlap and, with 4 sets, spills to the stack (the core has 5 registers of 64 sums). A stream of blocks gives 8 groups of 72 bytes for every 9 loads only when the loop takes them 8 at a time; otherwise Peano adds a load to every one. The kernel of the overlay reaches 60 % (107 cycles for 64 multiplies in every 16 x 16 block of C) because it loads the 256 sums of the block at its start and stores them at its end, which it cannot overlap with the multiplies with only 5 registers. It does not matter yet: the commands of the model are bound by memory.

### C in bfloat16

Done on 2026/11/13, the first of the three steps the measures above called for (C in fewer bytes, every weight for more rows, then the kernel). The overlay built with `bfpmm_resident.py --c-type bf16` gives C rounded to bfloat16, half the bytes of 32-bit floats; its manifest says `output bf16`. Since then it is the overlay in `_/xdna2/overlays/`; the one with C in 32-bit floats is in `_/xdna2/overlays-f32/`.

**How the cores do it**: every core adds up the tiles of K in 32-bit floats in a buffer of its own (`sums`, 16 KiB), with `xdna2_mm_bfp16` as before, and the last tile of K goes through `xdna2_mm_bfp16_last`, which adds it and writes the tile of C rounded to bfloat16 into the tile that goes out (8 KiB). It rounds to the nearest, ties to even: the core starts in the rounding mode floor, which would push every value down by half a step on average. Peano turns the rounding into stores that convert on the way (`vst.conv.bf16.fp32`), so it costs no instruction of its own. The two functions come from the same source, built twice (`-DXDNA2_LAST_TILE` gives the second), and a core links both objects. The core takes the tile of C only for the last tile of K, so the previous one can still be on its way out; with C in 32-bit floats it waited for it at the start of every tile of C (read in the design: one tile of C per core). The first and the last tile of K are different calls, so K has to be two tiles at least: the host pads it with zeros (`fXdnaGemmTilesKOutside`), and the counter of the loop of the cores is K / k - 2.

**Memory of a core** (read in the placement of mlir-aie): the stack (3,840 bytes), two tiles of B and two of A (9,216 bytes each), the tile of C (8,192), the two parameters (8) and the sums (16,384): 65,288 of the 65,536 bytes.

**Precision** (`llama-perplexity --kl-divergence`, the same 2,048 tokens as above, model BF16, against the processor with F32):

| What multiplies | Logits | Perplexity | KL divergence, mean | Same most likely token |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Backend, emulated, C in 32-bit floats | backend | 24.12 | 0.001244 | 97.75 % |
| Backend, emulated, C in bfloat16 | backend, in bfloat16 | 24.18 | 0.001623 | 96.91 % |
| Backend, emulated, C in bfloat16 | backend, in 32-bit floats | 24.14 | 0.001297 | 97.94 % |
| Backend, emulated, C in bfloat16 | processor (BF16) | 24.12 | 0.001127 | 98.19 % |
| Backend, **NPU**, C in bfloat16 | processor (BF16) | 24.13 | 0.001135 | 98.04 % |

 - **Rounded to bfloat16, the logits cost the model as much as everything else together**: they are large (tens), and a step of bfloat16 there is 0.06 to 0.125, which moves the probabilities of the softmax. **Every other multiplication costs nothing measurable** in bfloat16: 0.001297 against 0.001244, less than what the order of the sums alone changes (0.0007, see above).
 - So the backend leaves the logits to the processor when C comes back in bfloat16 (`fGivesLogits`). When a program reads a prompt or generates text, llama.cpp computes the logits of the last token only, one row, which went to the processor anyway. Only a program that wants the logits of every token, as `llama-perplexity`, leaves the processor more work: 0.45 s per pass of 512 tokens against 0.42 s.
 - The output matrix is no longer loaded twice: 186 matrices and 498 million weights converted, against 187 and 752 million.
 - **On the NPU the loss is the one the emulation predicted**: 0.001135 against 0.001127.

**Checked on the NPU** (2026/11/13, `llama-xdna-gemm`): every value checked equals the reference rounded to bfloat16, with 512 x 1024 x 3584 (the first command of the new overlay, alone), 512 x 256 x 256 (K of two tiles: the loop of the cores runs no time), 512 x 1024 x 16128 (63 sends of every band) and the ten shapes of the model twice in one context. The instruction stream the host writes for 4096 x 4096 x 4096 is identical, byte for byte, to the one of mlir-aie for that build. Times of the second round:

| Multiplication (M x K x N) | NPU, C in 32-bit floats | NPU, C in bfloat16 |
|---|---|---|
| 512 x 1024 x 16 | 0.150 ms | 0.144 ms |
| 512 x 1024 x 512 | 0.139 ms | 0.142 ms |
| 512 x 1024 x 2048 | 0.203 ms | 0.191 ms |
| 512 x 1024 x 3584 | 0.277 ms | 0.256 ms |
| 512 x 1024 x 4096 | 0.297 ms | 0.262 ms |
| 512 x 1024 x 6144 | 0.391 ms | 0.345 ms |
| 512 x 2048 x 1024 | 0.196 ms | 0.210 ms |
| 512 x 3584 x 1024 | 0.262 ms | 0.267 ms |
| 512 x 1024 x 6400 | 0.401 ms | 0.354 ms |
| 512 x 1024 x 16128 | 0.906 ms | 0.706 ms (24 TOPS) |

 - The wide multiplications gain up to 22 %, less than the bytes alone would give (a third): with 512 x 1024 x 16128 the data now goes at 60 GB/s and the cores, at the 60 % of their kernel, would need 0.48 ms: the kernel starts to count. The narrow ones keep the fixed cost of a command, about 0.11 ms.

**With the model** (Qwen3.5 0.8B, 2026/11/13): the text of 32 tokens is the same as the processor's. With every multiplication on the NPU, a generated token takes 66 ms against 90 ms with the previous overlay, as the logits no longer go to the NPU. `llama-bench`, Q8_0, 8 threads, the default threshold of 72 rows:

| Tokens per second | Batch of 64 | Batch of 512 | Generation |
|---|---|---|---|
| Processor alone | 623 | 589 | 52.8 |
| NPU, C in 32-bit floats | 624 | 1604 | 52.7 |
| NPU, C in bfloat16 | 628 | 1688 | 53.2 |

**Where a batch of 512 tokens goes** (measured on 2026/11/13, `llama-bench -p 512 -v`, the report of the backend over 6 batches): 302 ms per batch. The backend takes 115 ms of them: **48 ms of NPU**, 31 ms converting rows, 34 ms reading results and 2.5 ms writing instruction streams. The other 187 ms are the processor running the rest of the model. The 183 commands of a batch do 249 x 10^9 multiply-accumulates: 10.4 TOPS on average while the NPU works, and about 20 of its 48 ms are the fixed cost of the commands (0.11 ms each). What follows from it:

 - The fixed cost of the commands is now as large as their work: fewer commands, larger ones. Several matrices of a layer multiply the same rows (`attn_qkv`, `attn_gate`, `ssm_alpha` and `ssm_beta`; `ffn_gate` and `ffn_up`; `attn_q`, `attn_k` and `attn_v`): they could go in one command, and their rows would be converted once instead of up to four times.
 - Larger batches (`-ub` of llama.cpp) send every weight once for more rows: with 512 rows the weights already go once per command.
 - The host does not overlap with the NPU: converting rows and reading results cost more than the NPU itself.
 - The processor part, 62 % of the batch, now bounds the prompt more than the NPU does.

### Fewer commands

Done on 2026/11/13, the second step: every weight for more rows, and fewer and larger commands. Measured with `llama-bench`, Qwen3.5 0.8B Q8_0, 8 threads; the laptop gives some percent more or less from one hour to the next (it has a desktop running), so every comparison is of runs one after the other, alternated.

**Larger batches do not pay with this model.** With `-ub` (the rows llama.cpp gives every multiplication) at 2,048 instead of 512, a weight goes once for every 2,048 rows and the NPU takes a third less time for the same tokens, but the processor takes more (its attention and its recurrence over a batch grow faster than the batch), and the whole is slower:

| Prompt of 2,048 tokens, tokens per second | `-ub 512` | `-ub 1024` | `-ub 2048` |
|---|---|---|---|
| Processor alone | 534 | 481 | 457 |
| NPU | 1442 | 1367 | 1301 |
| NPU time for the 2,048 tokens | 220 ms | | 147 ms (13.6 TOPS) |
| Processor time for the rest | 1.05 s | | 1.20 s |

With 512 rows a weight already goes once per command, so what was left to do was fewer commands:

 - **Multiplications with the same rows in one command** (see [The backend of ggml](#the-backend-of-ggml)): 94 commands per batch of 512 instead of 183, NPU 35.5 ms instead of 47.8 (14 TOPS on average), converting rows 20.8 ms instead of 30.7. **The batch of 512 goes from 1,661 to 1,691 tokens per second without it to 1,771 to 1,815 with it** (+7 to 9 %). With every multiplication on the NPU a generated token takes 55 ms instead of 66.
 - **Results written past the cache** (`_mm_stream_ps` in `fXdnaGemmUnpackC`): C is written once, and a batch writes 820 MB of results, more than the cache holds; a normal store first reads the line it goes to. Reading results goes from about 32 to 27 ms per batch, and the batch from 1,799-1,819 to 1,881 tokens per second (+4 %).
 - Flushing C from the cache of the processor before reading it is only 4.5 ms of the 31.6 ms of reading results (measured with the report of the backend, which says it since then): the rest is moving 410 MB of bfloat16 in and 820 MB of floats out.
 - **Waiting without sleeping** (`XdnaDevice::mWait`, the first 2 ms): a thread that sleeps until the end of a command wakes up late; with a command of 512 x 4096 x 4096 the median went from 0.90-1.15 ms to 0.75 ms, and with 512 x 1024 x 256 from 0.151 to 0.129 ms. In the model it changes nothing measurable (the threads of the processor keep it awake).

**What the fixed cost of a command is** (measured on 2026/11/13): a command with an instruction stream that has no operation (`llama-xdna-insts empty`, run with `llama-xdna-run`) takes 0.047 ms at best and 0.053 ms in the median: the driver, the firmware and the end of the command. A multiplication of 512 rows has 260 operations in its stream, and taking away the 88 of the setting of the bands of A (a manifest built for one K and one N, the same overlay) saves 15 to 19 µs: about 0.2 µs per operation. So the 0.11 ms of the smallest command are about 0.05 ms of the command itself, about 0.05 ms of its operations and the little data it moves.

**Where a batch of 512 tokens goes now**: 271 ms, 1,890 tokens per second (the processor alone, 633: 3.0 times). The backend takes 87.5 ms of them: NPU 35.3 ms, converting rows 18.8, reading results 31.6 and writing instruction streams 1.5. The processor takes 183.5 ms for the rest of the model: two thirds of the batch.

**Does the kernel count now?** (measured on 2026/11/13, before the third step): the overlay with C in bfloat16 built without the multiplication (`bfpmm_resident.py --c-type bf16 --compute 0`, `_/xdna2/work/bfpres-4096-b8-bf16-c0`, the same placement and manifest), against the real one, medians of 15 runs, twice:

| Multiplication (M x K x N) | With the multiplication | Without it | What the multiplication adds |
|---|---|---|---|
| 512 x 1024 x 8224 (`attn_qkv` + `attn_gate` + `ssm_beta` + `ssm_alpha`) | 0.416-0.422 ms | 0.405-0.410 ms | 3 % |
| 512 x 1024 x 7168 (`ffn_gate` + `ffn_up`) | 0.350-0.367 ms | 0.348-0.357 ms | 2 % |
| 512 x 1024 x 5120 (`attn_q` + `attn_k` + `attn_v`) | 0.278-0.284 ms | 0.275-0.283 ms | 1 % |
| 512 x 2048 x 1024 (`ssm_out`, `attn_output`) | 0.191-0.193 ms | 0.175-0.177 ms | 9 % |
| 512 x 3584 x 1024 (`ffn_down`) | 0.259-0.260 ms | 0.225-0.227 ms | 15 % |
| 512 x 1024 x 16128 | 0.693-0.699 ms | 0.671-0.672 ms | 4 % |
| 4096 x 4096 x 4096 (fastest runs) | 4.99-5.05 ms (27 TOPS) | 3.78-3.85 ms | 31 % |
| 4096 x 1024 x 8224 (fastest runs) | 2.61-2.63 ms | 2.55-2.56 ms | 2 % |
| 2048 x 3584 x 1024 (fastest runs) | 0.75-0.77 ms | 0.62-0.63 ms | 21 % |

 - **With the batches of the model the multiplication is almost hidden**: a perfect kernel would save at most about 1.6 ms of the 35 ms of NPU of a batch of 512. It shows only where C is a small part of the bytes, that is with a long K: with 4096 x 4096 x 4096 a perfect kernel would go from 27 to 36 TOPS.
 - What bounds the commands of the model is still the bytes per multiply-accumulate: a weight is 1.125 bytes and goes once for every 512 rows, and an output is 2 bytes and comes back once for every K inputs. With 512 rows and K = 1024 that is 0.0022 + 0.0020 bytes, about 0.0044 with A: 66 GB/s feed at most 15 x 10^12 multiply-accumulates per second, 30 TOPS. **Weights of 4 bits (0.625 bytes) and C in block floating point (1.125 bytes per value) would lower it to about 0.0025 bytes: 53 TOPS**, and then the kernel, at 60 % of the peak (35 TOPS), would be what bounds. So the kernel waits (decided on 2026/11/13), and the bytes come first.

### C in block floating point

Tried on 2026/11/13: C rounded to bfp16, 1.125 bytes per value instead of 2. Kept as an option (`bfpmm_resident.py --c-type bfp16`, `_/xdna2/overlays-bfp16/`), not as the default: today it gains nothing.

 - **Which values share an exponent matters**: with every 8 outputs of one token in a block, as the 8 x 8 sums of a multiply come out of the core, the emulation gives KL divergence 0.002245 and the same token in 97.11 % (C in bfloat16: 0.001127 and 98.19 %); with every 8 tokens of one output in a block, 0.001458 and 97.55 %. A few outputs of a model are much larger than the rest, and in a block of 8 outputs they leave the other 7 with few bits; 8 tokens of one output have values of a similar size.
 - **The cores give that for free**: with the operands of the multiply swapped (`mac_8x8_8x8T(B, A)`), the 64 sums of an 8 x 8 come out transposed, and 8 consecutive sums are 8 rows of one column; the last tile of K rounds them as it stores them (`vst.push.576.conv.bfp16ebs8.fp32`). The kernel built with `-DXDNA2_C_BFP16` does that in its two functions, as the sums kept in the core are transposed too. A tile of C is then 4,608 bytes instead of 8,192.
 - **Checked on the NPU**: the instruction stream of 4096 x 4096 x 4096 is identical to the one of mlir-aie; with 512 x 1024 x 3584, 512 x 256 x 256, 512 x 1024 x 16128, 300 x 1000 x 700, the shapes of the model and 4096 x 4096 x 4096, 99.2 to 99.95 % of the values checked equal the reference rounded to bfp16 and the others are one step of their block away from it.
 - **The NPU barely gains**: medians of 15 runs, 512 x 1024 x 8224 in 0.391-0.398 ms against 0.416-0.420 with bfloat16, 512 x 1024 x 16128 in 0.645 against 0.687, and the same as bfloat16 for the other shapes of the model. With the model, the NPU takes the same 35 ms per batch, and the host takes 10 ms more to read the results (decoding the blocks), so the batch of 512 is no faster (1,803-1,841 tokens per second against 1,802-1,871).
 - **Why**: the weights come in through 4 shim channels only, one for every row of cores, and with 512 x 1024 x 8224 every one carries 2.4 MB at about 8.5 GB/s; that sets the time of the wide multiplications whatever C weighs. A comes in through the other 8 channels and is small (0.6 MB in all), and 4 of the 16 channels that bring data in are unused. So the next thing that pays is fewer bytes of weights, or the weights spread over more channels.

### Weights of 4 bits

Studied on 2026/11/13, for models whose weights already are of 4 bits (Q4_0, Q4_K_M): 0.5625 to 0.625 bytes per weight instead of 1.125.

**Precision: as the processor with the same file.** The emulation already turns any type of weights into floats and then into bfp16, which is what the NPU would read if it expanded the 4 bits without error. Qwen3.5 0.8B quantized here from the BF16 with `llama-quantize` (Q4_K_M: q4_K, with 27 tensors in q6_K; Q4_0), against the processor with F32, same 2,048 tokens:

| What multiplies | Model | Perplexity | KL divergence, mean | Same most likely token |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Processor | Q4_K_M | 25.73 | 0.0639 | 88.09 % |
| Backend, emulated | Q4_K_M | 25.56 | 0.0623 | 88.09 % |
| Processor | Q4_0 | 27.17 | 0.1139 | 81.03 % |
| Backend, emulated | Q4_0 | 27.23 | 0.1157 | 80.93 % |

The weights of 4 bits cost 50 to 100 times what everything the backend rounds costs (0.0011); the backend adds nothing visible to them.

**Speed: the expansion in the cores eats what the bytes save.** (Read in the AIE API of mlir-aie 1.4.5 and in its kernels; estimates from the measures above.)

 - XDNA2 has no block format of 4 bits for its multiplier: `mx4`, `mx6` and `mx9` are of AIE-MLv2 only (`aie_doc.hpp`, «Supported block vector types»), and a bfp16 block can only be made by converting floats of an accumulator (`to_vector<bfp16ebs8>`): the 4 bits have to become floats first. The kernel of mlir-aie that does it (`aie_kernels/quant/q4nx_dequant.cc`: 4 bits to bf16, scale and minimum in an accumulator, transpose, conversion) took 0.637 µs per 4,096 weights here, 18 cycles per 64; without its transpose and better pipelined, perhaps 9.
 - In this design every tile of weights goes to the 8 cores of a row, and each would expand it: about 0.64 µs per tile of 8,192 weights per core, on top of the 0.95 µs of its multiplication (the kernel at 60 %). With 512 x 1024 x 8224 a core gets a new tile every 1.6 µs now, bound by the data: with 4 bits it would compute for 1.6 µs per tile, as long as the data takes today. Weights of 4 bits only pay with the expansion done once per tile (cores that only expand and pass bfp16 on, which leaves fewer cores to multiply and changes how A is spread) or about 3 times faster than through floats.
 - The multiplier does take int4 (`mmul_8_4`, int8 x int4), but it unpacks it to int8 (`unpack_sign`) and multiplies at the rate of int8: rows in int8 instead of bfp16, and the scales of every group of 32 weights applied in floats to every partial sum, which costs about as much as the multiplies of the group.

### The kernel in steps of three

Written and measured on 2026/11/13: `ggml/src/ggml-xdna/kernels/xdna2-mm-bfp16-s3.cc` (`xdna2_mm_bfp16_s3` and `xdna2_mm_bfp16_s3_last`), built into the overlay with `bfpmm_resident.py --kernel s3`. Since then it is the kernel of the overlay in `_/xdna2/overlays/` (C in bfloat16; from the same day, with the buffers of the cores moved, see [A call of the kernel, and the banks of a core](#a-call-of-the-kernel-and-the-banks-of-a-core)); the one of the kernel before is in `overlays-bf16-2x2/`.

**Where the cycles of the kernel before went** (read in the code Peano generates): every step of 16 rows by 16 columns, 64 multiplies with four sets of sums, took 105 cycles when adding to the sums kept in the core and 92 when starting them: about 19 to load the 256 sums and work out where the step starts (with a multiplication of scalars), about 71 for the multiplies, with the compiler sending sums to the stack and back, and about 15 to store the sums, overlapped with nothing. The core has five registers for sets of 64 sums: with four of them in use there is none to load the sums of the next step while the multiplier works.

**What the new one does**: steps of one group of 8 rows of A by three groups of 8 columns of B (8 x 24), three sets of sums. The 8 groups of columns of a tile of 64 go in two sets of 3 and a last one of 2, which has its own loop of two sets of sums per step. Three things it took (all of them read in the code Peano generates, every line of a loop being a cycle):

 - **Two pointers for the same sums**, one to read them and one to write them, both `__restrict`: with one, the compiler does not know that a step reads only its own sums and does not overlap the steps: 87 cycles for 48 multiplies instead of 60. And no `__restrict` on the function that hands that one pointer to the loops as the two: with it, the same 87.
 - **A position of B the compiler cannot work out**: the groups of B of the last set are the same in every step of its loop, and the compiler reads them once before the loop and keeps them in the stack (2.3 KiB), which makes the loop twice as slow. The position carries a 0 read from a `volatile` variable (a 0 derived from the arguments does not do: the compiler works it out).
 - With that, every loop overlaps its steps without the stack: 56 cycles for 48 multiplies when starting the sums and 60 when adding to them (86 and 80 % of the peak), 45 and 44 for the 32 of the last set (71 and 73 %). The function of the last tile of K, which writes C rounded, still takes 81 for 48; it is one call in K / 128.

**The order of the groups in the tiles** changes with it, and the host follows the name of the kernel in the manifest (`kernel xdna2_mm_bfp16_s3`, which `overlay_manifest.py` reads in the build): A `[M / 8][K / 8][group]`, B `[sets][K / 8][groups of the set]`, and C and the sums `[M / 8][sets of 3][3][8 x 8]` and then `[M / 8][2][8 x 8]`. With the overlays of the kernel before, the host lays them out as before: checked byte for byte against the binary before this change (A and B of 512 x 1024 x 3584 with both roundings; C of 512 x 1024 x 3584 and of 300 x 1000 x 3500).

**Measured on the NPU**: every value checked equals the reference rounded to bfloat16 (512 x 1024 x 3584, 512 x 256 x 256, 512 x 1024 x 16128, 300 x 1000 x 700 and 4096 x 4096 x 4096), and the instruction stream is identical to the one of mlir-aie. Every sum adds up K in the same order as with the kernel before, so the model gives the same results bit for bit: the same text as the processor, KL divergence 0.001135 and perplexity 24.131128, as before. Times against the kernel before, alternated, 10 runs each:

| Multiplication (M x K x N) | Kernel before (fastest, median) | Kernel in steps of three (fastest, median) |
|---|---|---|
| 4096 x 4096 x 4096 | 5.12 ms (27 TOPS), 5.40-5.74 | 4.49-4.59 ms (30.6 TOPS), 4.72-4.92 |
| 2048 x 3584 x 1024 | 0.77 ms, 0.84 | 0.68 ms, 0.71-0.75 |
| 4096 x 1024 x 8224 | 2.60 ms, 2.64-2.65 | 2.60-2.62 ms, 2.64-2.69 |
| 512 x 1024 x 8224 | 0.41-0.42 ms, 0.44-0.45 | 0.40-0.41 ms, 0.44-0.45 |
| 512 x 3584 x 1024 | 0.28-0.30 ms, 0.31 | 0.27-0.28 ms, 0.30 |
| 512 x 2048 x 1024 | 0.20-0.21 ms, 0.23-0.24 | 0.21 ms, 0.21-0.23 |

 - **11 to 13 % less where the multiplication counted**, and the same where the data bounds, which is every shape of the model with batches of 512: the batch of 512 of Qwen3.5 0.8B stays at 1,844-1,848 tokens per second (1,807-1,884 with the kernel before).
 - 4096 x 4096 x 4096 is still 18 % over the overlay without the multiplication (3.8 ms): about 1.1 µs per call of a core, where the data of a call takes 0.93 µs and the kernel about 0.82. What is left is the start and the end of every loop (the compiler fills and empties the overlap of the steps in every call, about 150 cycles) and the waits of the core for its tiles.

### Weights through eight channels

Tried on 2026/11/13: `bfpmm_resident.py --b-split 2`. Kept as an option (`_/xdna2/overlays-split/`, C in bfloat16, kernel in steps of three), not as the default: the model does not notice it.

 - **Why the weights had four channels**: a memory tile has 6 input channels, and the 4 that took B used all of them: the band of A, the tiles of B and the 4 tiles of C of its column (read in the placement of the build, `input_with_addresses.mlir`).
 - **What the variant does**: every tile of B comes in two halves, each through a shim channel of its own (row r of cores through the shims of columns 2r and 2r + 1), and the memory tile of column 2r + 1 joins them before it sends the tile to the eight cores of the row. The `join` of IRON keeps the order, as every half fills a fixed part of every tile; its `merge` would not (it gives whole objects in the order they arrive). To leave an input free there, the memory tile of every column joins the tiles of C of the rows of cores 0 to 2 only, and the last row sends its tiles straight to the host through the second output channel of its shim. The 16 input and the 16 output channels of the shims are all in use.
 - **Placed by hand**: left to itself, the placer of mlir-aie put up to three bands of A in one memory tile, which with K = 4096 (295 KB each) do not fit in its 512 KB, and with 8 tiles of B queued per row the memory tiles ran out of buffer descriptors (24 per group of channels). The variant pins every link and every shim (`tile=` of IRON) and queues 4 tiles of B.
 - **Layouts**: B `[4 rows][2 halves][N / n / 4][K / k][half of a tile]`; C `[8 columns][M / m / 8][N / n / 4][rows 0 to 2][tile]` and then `[8 columns][M / m / 8][N / n / 4][tile of row 3]`. The manifest says `split 2` and lists the streams of row 3 as `stream D` (`overlay_manifest.py` reads them in the build); the host follows it in `fXdnaGemmPlan`, `fXdnaGemmPackB` and `fXdnaGemmUnpackC`.
 - **Checked**: the instruction stream of 4096 x 4096 x 4096 is identical to the one of mlir-aie (40,464 bytes, against 25,680 with four channels); every value checked equals the reference rounded to bfloat16 with 512 x 1024 x 8192, 300 x 1000 x 700, 1024 x 2048 x 1024, 512 x 1024 x 8224, 512 x 3584 x 1024, 2048 x 1024 x 7168 and 4096 x 4096 x 4096; the model gives the same results bit for bit (the text of the processor, KL divergence 0.001135, perplexity 24.131128).

Times, first with the streams of mlir-aie for one shape (512 x 1024 x 8192, medians of 50 runs, two rounds):

| Overlay | C in bfloat16 | C in bfloat16, without the multiplication | C in bfp16 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Four channels, 8 tiles of B queued (the default) | 0.358-0.360 ms | | 0.328-0.353 ms |
| Four channels, 4 tiles of B queued | 0.350-0.357 ms | 0.358-0.381 ms | 0.327-0.337 ms |
| Eight channels | 0.329-0.334 ms | 0.323-0.329 ms | 0.315-0.317 ms (0.275-0.282 without the multiplication) |

And with the streams of the host, as the backend runs them (medians of 30 to 50 runs, two or three rounds):

| Multiplication (M x K x N) | Four channels | Eight channels |
|---|---|---|
| 512 x 1024 x 8224 | 0.444-0.448 ms | 0.397-0.410 ms |
| 512 x 1024 x 7168 | 0.382-0.393 ms | 0.342-0.374 ms |
| 512 x 1024 x 5120 | 0.316-0.318 ms | 0.277-0.299 ms |
| 512 x 3584 x 1024 | 0.240-0.241 ms | 0.240-0.253 ms |
| 512 x 2048 x 1024 | 0.183-0.185 ms | 0.182-0.183 ms |
| 512 x 1024 x 1024 | 0.149-0.164 ms | 0.151-0.152 ms |
| 4096 x 4096 x 4096 (streams of mlir-aie, 20 runs) | 4.50-4.67 ms | 4.63-4.72 ms |

 - **The wide multiplications gain 7 to 9 %, and then memory bounds them**: with C in bfloat16 the variant without the multiplication takes what the real one takes, which fits about 76 GB/s between reading and writing plus about 0.08 ms per command. With C in bfp16 the data takes 0.275 ms and the multiplication adds 0.04: there the kernel counts again.
 - **The narrow ones do not change**, or lose up to 4 %: with N = 1024 every core makes 4 tiles of C of K / 128 calls each (112 with K = 3584), and before the first one it waits for the whole band of A in its memory tile (258 KB with K = 3584). Every shim now reads a band of A and a half of B at once.
 - **The model does not notice it**: the batch of 512 of Qwen3.5 0.8B at 1,790-1,860 tokens per second against 1,770-1,858, and the time of the NPU the backend reports per batch is the same (about 33 ms: the variant saves some 1.4 ms of them, under the noise); its commands also carry 312 operations instead of 260, some 10 µs more each. Qwen3.5 2B, the same: 1,254-1,302 against 1,276-1,284 tokens per second, **5 times the processor alone (250)**.

### The part of the processor

Measured and changed on 2026/11/13. With the multiplications on the NPU, the processor took two thirds of a batch of 512 tokens of Qwen3.5 0.8B (about 183 of 271 ms). Timed node by node with a timer in the loop of the CPU backend (thread 0, from the start of a node to the end of its barrier; a temporary change in `ggml-cpu.c`, removed after), over 6 batches:

| Operation | Before (ms per batch) | After | What it is |
|---|---|---|---|
| `GATED_DELTA_NET` | 73.8 | 34.5 | the recurrence of the linear attention, 18 layers |
| `SSM_CONV` | 41.5 | 18-19 | the short convolution before it |
| `FLASH_ATTN_EXT` | 15.8 | 15.8 | the attention of the 6 full layers |
| `CONCAT` | 14.0 | 14-15 | the state of the convolution next to the batch, transposed |
| `RMS_NORM` | 13.9 | 13.9 | 115 per batch |
| `SWIGLU`, `MUL_MAT`, `SILU`, `ADD` and the rest | 30 | 30 | |

What took the time, and what was changed (`ggml/src/ggml-cpu/ops.cpp`, marked `llama.cpp-xdna`; upstream has the same code as the fork had):

 - **`SSM_CONV`**: every output is 4 products along a row of its channel, and the loop went through the 768 channels of a thread for every token, with the indices worked out at every step: about 25 cycles per output, most of them in arithmetic of indices (read in `perf`). Channel by channel, all the tokens of a channel at once, was slower still (every output of a channel goes to another page). Now: blocks of 16 channels through all the tokens, the 4 products written out, and with AVX-512 16 tokens of 16 channels at a time (a vector per channel, the 4 products in the same order, and a transposition in registers that leaves the 16 channels of every token side by side). It now moves 25 MB per layer at about 25 GB/s: bound by memory.
 - **`GATED_DELTA_NET`**: for every token and head the loop went four times through the state (128 x 128, 64 KiB: decay, delta, outer product, output), from the L2 cache. Doing the four steps row by row did not change anything (the row stayed in the cache, but the state still went through it for every token), and neither did a dot product in line. What works: every row of the state changes on its own (it only reads itself, k, q, its value of v and the gate), so with AVX-512 and heads of 128 with one gate per head (Qwen3.5) two rows stay in registers through blocks of 64 tokens and the state does not leave them. Then in pieces of 32 rows of a head instead of whole heads: 16 heads for 8 threads, with half of the cores (Zen 5c) slower, left threads waiting. About 70 cycles per row and token; the floor of its arithmetic on this processor (512-bit operations on a 256-bit path) is about 32.
 - **Not changed**: `CONCAT` is bound by memory, not by its copy value by value: a transposition of 16 x 16 in registers gave the same time (it reads the output of a multiplication that the backend wrote past the cache, and writes 12.7 MB per layer). The chain of the input of the convolution (the output of the multiplication, `CONCAT`, `SSM_CONV`, `SILU`) moves about 12.6 MB per step and per layer; joining them in one operation would save one or two of those steps.
 - **Exactly the same results**: every output takes the same operations in the same order as before (the compiler fuses the products and sums of `SSM_CONV` in the same way in both; the dot products of the delta rule follow `ggml_vec_dot_f32` to the last bit: 4 sums of 16, 64 values at a time, the same reduction). Checked with the logits of 4,096 tokens of the processor alone, byte for byte equal to the ones of the code before (`llama-perplexity --kl-divergence-base`, BF16 model, batches of 512), both with the variant of the CPU backend for AVX-512 (`zen4`) and with the one for AVX2 (`haswell`), which takes the other paths; and with the NPU the text is the one of the processor and the divergence the same as before (KL 0.001135, perplexity 24.131128).
 - **The host side of the backend** (`xdna-gemm.cpp`, `xdna-workers.cpp`): with a batch of 512 a conversion of A or a reading of C had 8 parts (bands of A, columns of cores) for 8 threads, so the slower cores left the others waiting: now every part is cut into pieces of consecutive tiles along K or of consecutive turns, about 4 per thread. And with OpenMP (as ggml has) the loops run on its threads, the same ones the CPU backend uses, which wait awake for a while after each of its parallel regions: the threads of the pool slept between loops of 0.2 ms. Converting the rows of a batch went from about 21 to 13 ms; reading the results, from about 35 to 31-33 ms. With the OpenMP threads told to sleep at once (`OMP_WAIT_POLICY=PASSIVE`) the backend converts a little faster but the processor loses more (2,274 against 2,376-2,401 tokens per second), and writing C through the cache instead of past it still loses (2,406-2,423 against 2,462-2,482). More threads lose too: 2,141-2,245 with 12 and 1,199-1,241 with 16, against 2,354-2,499 with 8.
 - **Measured, alternating the library of the CPU backend before and after** in the same state of the laptop (it runs slower after hours of measures, so only alternated measures compare): the batch of 512 of Qwen3.5 0.8B, **2,121-2,126 tokens per second with the NPU against 1,617-1,640 (30 % more)**, and 523-529 with the processor alone against 451-482. With the NPU it is now 4 times the processor alone; with Qwen3.5 2B, 7 times (1,398 against 196). Generation is the same as before (48 and 20 tokens per second; it goes to the processor). In a cooler state of the laptop the same build gave 2,500 tokens per second.
 - Where a batch of 512 goes now (about 205 ms at 2,500 tokens per second): the processor about 125 ms (the recurrence 35, the convolution 18, the attention 16, `CONCAT` 15, the norms 14, the rest 27) and the backend about 80 (NPU 32, reading results 31-33, converting rows 13, streams 1.5).

### A call of the kernel, and the banks of a core

Measured on 2026/11/13, to bring the large multiplications nearer to the 36 TOPS of the data path.

**A call of the kernel in steps of three** (read in the code Peano generates, every line of a loop a cycle): adding to the sums, the entry takes 1 cycle, the start of the loop of the sets of 3 takes 63, the loop 15 x 61, the end of it and the start of the loop of the last set 72, that loop 7 x 45, and its end 37: about 1,400 cycles for 1,024 multiplies (73 %), 170 of them outside the loops. One loop over the groups of rows, every step with its sets of 3 and its set of 2, would start and end once: Peano cannot keep its larger body in registers and sends almost everything to the stack (a frame of 15 KB, 1,961 lines instead of 571). Discarded.

**What bounds 4096 x 4096 x 4096 is not the kernel**: without the multiplication the overlay takes 0.93 µs per call of a core (1,674 cycles at 1.8 GHz), more than the kernel, and with it 1.1 µs. The tiles of B go to the 8 cores of a row and those of A to the 4 cores of a column at once, so every core waits for the slowest one of its row and of its column, and with two buffers per operand (the memory of a core is full) there is nothing to absorb it.

**The banks of the memory of a core**: it is 4 banks of 16 KB (`getNumBanks` of the target model of mlir-aie), and mlir-aie put the two tiles of B in bank 0, the second one also in bank 1 with the first tile of A, and the second tile of A in banks 1 and 2: while the kernel reads one tile its DMA writes the next one into the same banks. B is what the kernel reads most (every group 8 times per call). The buffers of an object fifo cannot be pinned to a bank in mlir-aie 1.4.5, so `ggml/src/ggml-xdna/overlays/designs/core_banks.py` moves them in the `input_with_addresses.mlir` of a build, and aiecc builds the overlay again from it (`aiecc --unified --peano=<llvm-aie> --get-npu-insts --npu-insts-name=<file> --get-xclbin --xclbin-name=<file> --fold-ddr-addr-offset=false --tmpdir=<dir> --output-dir=<dir> <edited mlir>`, with the objects of the kernel in `<dir>`):

| Bank | mlir-aie | Moved |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | stack, B 0, start of B 1 | stack, B 0, start of C |
| 1 | rest of B 1, A 0, start of A 1 | rest of C, B 1, parameters, start of A 0 |
| 2 | rest of A 1, C, parameters | rest of A 0, A 1 |
| 3 | sums | sums |

Every tile of B then shares its bank with nothing the DMA writes while the kernel reads it (A 0 and A 1 still share bank 2: the 9 KB tiles do not fit otherwise). The instruction stream only changes in the addresses of the parameters of the cores (64 writes); the manifest is written from the new build. On the NPU every value checked equals the reference, and the model gives the same results bit for bit. Times, alternated with the overlay before (medians of 20 or 30 runs):

| Multiplication (M x K x N) | Layout of mlir-aie | Moved |
|---|---|---|
| 4096 x 4096 x 4096 (streams of mlir-aie) | 4.58-4.67 ms (fastest 4.15-4.25) | 4.38-4.45 ms (fastest 3.96-4.02: 34.7 TOPS) |
| 2048 x 3584 x 1024 | 0.695-0.696 ms | 0.663-0.672 ms |
| 512 x 1024 x 8224 | 0.452-0.454 ms | 0.422-0.451 ms |
| 512 x 3584 x 1024 | 0.239-0.243 ms | 0.231-0.263 ms |

4 to 5 % less where the multiplication counts, and the batch of 512 of Qwen3.5 0.8B 1 to 2 % faster (2,446-2,540 tokens per second against 2,404-2,486). It is the overlay in `_/xdna2/overlays/` since then (the one before, in `overlays-bf16-s3/`).

### Qwen3.5 4B

Measured on 2026/11/15 with the overlay in `overlays/`, `llama-bench -p 512 -n 32 -r 3 -t 8`, the NPU and the CPU alone one after the other for every format. The four files are the user's (`Qwen3.5-4B-MTP-Abliterated-*.gguf`, architecture `qwen35`: 33 blocks, width 2560, feed-forward 9216; 3.69 G weights in matrices and 0.64 G in the embeddings, which are also the output matrix). Precision: KL with 2048 tokens of `backend-test/texto.txt` against the CPU with Q8_0, the most precise file there is of this model.

| File | Prompt of 512, NPU | Prompt of 512, CPU alone | Generation, NPU / CPU alone | KL, NPU | KL, CPU alone |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q8_0 | 752 t/s | 98 t/s | 10.1 / 10.0 t/s | 0.001558 (same token 96.96 %) | the reference |
| Q6_K | 703 t/s | 218 t/s | 12.8 / 12.8 t/s | 0.005600 | 0.008021 |
| Q5_K_M | 700 t/s | 220 t/s | 13.2 / 13.2 t/s | 0.012550 | 0.013611 |
| Q4_K_M | 689 t/s | 147 t/s | 15.1 / 15.0 t/s | 0.041168 | 0.043188 |

 - The NPU reads prompts at about the same speed whatever the file, since every format becomes bfp16. The CPU alone depends on its kernel for each format, and Q8_0 is its slowest here.
 - In every format the NPU is as precise as the CPU with the same file, or a little more. With Q8_0 the generated text is the same as on the CPU for the first tokens and then takes another path, as rounding allows.
 - Generation stays on the CPU (threshold of 72 rows): 42 to 46 GB/s from memory (the bytes of the file times the tokens per second). With every multiplication on the NPU (`GGML_XDNA_MIN_BATCH=1`) it falls to 4.1 tokens per second with Q8_0: 231 commands per token, and every row is computed as 512.
 - A batch of 512 with Q8_0 (report of the backend): NPU 188 ms (about 19 TOPS, with 224 commands), converting rows 51 ms, reading results 134 ms. The CPU, with the other operations, takes about 300 ms of the 681.

### Fixed costs: commands, contexts and overlays

Measured on 2026/11/15 with `llama-xdna-probe`, with instruction streams that move nothing (`llama-xdna-insts empty`: a header and no operations), so the times are what a command costs by itself. Overlays: the one in `overlays/` (`work/bank-exp2/prj/main.pdi`) and the one in `overlays-f32/` (`work/bfpres-4096-b8/final.prj/main.pdi`), both for 8 columns.

| How the commands go | Per command |
|---|---|
| One by one (submit, wait, the next) | 0.055 ms |
| Up to 4 in flight, the most the driver keeps for a context (`HWCTX_MAX_CMDS`, `aie2_pci.h`) | 0.015-0.016 ms |
| In chains of 8 (`ERT_CMD_CHAIN`) | 0.020 ms |
| In chains of 32 | 0.017 ms |
| Alternating two contexts, each with its own overlay (also with the same overlay in both) | 2.4-2.7 ms |
| Alternating two compute units of one context, each with its own overlay | 2.4-2.6 ms |

 - The firmware spends about 15 µs on a command; the rest of the 0.055 ms is the way there and back (the submission, the interrupt and the thread that wakes up). Commands that need nothing from the host in between can go 4 at a time or in chains: 300 commands then cost about 5 ms instead of 16.
 - **Changing overlay costs about 2.5 ms**, whether the two overlays are in two contexts or in two compute units of one context (`DRM_AMDXDNA_HWCTX_CONFIG_CU` takes up to 32, `MAX_NUM_CUS`): the firmware loads the whole overlay again. With two contexts open, commands that go to the same context one after another still cost 0.055 ms: the change costs, not having two. So everything that runs between two changes has to be in one overlay, and a token cannot go through several overlays; one change per phase (the prompt, then the generation) costs 2.5 ms once.
 - A chain carries the number of commands, two indexes that the firmware fills, 3 reserved words and the handle of every command (64 bits each), with no mask of compute units (`amdxdna_cmd_get_payload`, `aie2_cmdlist_multi_execbuf`). The driver copies every command into a slot of a 4 KiB buffer, about 68 bytes each with 5 buffer slots: about 60 commands at most.
 - The core program of the overlay in use takes 6,352 of the 16,384 bytes of program memory of a core (`llvm-size` of `work/bank-exp2/prj/elfs_main_core_2_2`): there is room for more kernels in the same overlay.

### An overlay for generating tokens

Generating a token multiplies one row by every matrix of weights: every weight is used once, so what bounds it is how fast the weights arrive. The overlay of the prompts brings them through 4 shim channels (8 split) and computes every row as 512; this one (`_/xdna2-lab/gemv_stream.py`, built on 2026/11/15) brings them through all 16 input channels of the shims, each straight into a core of its own (rows 2 and 3 of every column), in objects of 9216 bytes: 32 outputs by 256 values of K in bfp16, a size that leaves no padding in any matrix of Qwen3.5 from 0.8B to 27B (K a multiple of 256, N of 512). Every core makes N / 16 outputs. Before its weights, every stream carries X (1 to 8 rows, padded to whole objects), which the core keeps for the whole command (27,648 bytes: K up to 24,576 with one row, 3,072 with 8). The cores send back 32-bit sums, 8 rows by 32 outputs per object of 1024 bytes. The sizes reach the cores as runtime parameters, so one overlay serves every size.

Kernel: `ggml/src/ggml-xdna/kernels/xdna2-gemv-bfp16.cc` (`xdna2_gemv_bfp16`, and `xdna2_gemv_load_x` built with `-DXDNA2_GEMV_LOAD_X`). For every tile of K it pads the rows of X with zero rows up to 8 in a staging buffer, and then 4 groups of 8 outputs go through `mac_8x8_8x8T` along the 32 groups of K. It keeps its counters (the tile of K and the object of X) in the staging buffer, so the core needs no loop index. The core program takes 1,920 bytes (4,784 since it also decodes Q4_K, see [Weights in Q4_K for generating tokens](#weights-in-q4_k-for-generating-tokens)).

The data path alone (the first version of the design, with the cores only taking the objects), 50 runs, median:

| Bytes per command | Time | Rate |
|---|---|---|
| 2.9 MB | 0.109 ms | 27 GB/s |
| 7.4 MB | 0.166 ms | 44 GB/s |
| 26.5 MB | 0.392 ms | 68 GB/s |
| 110.6 MB | 1.473 ms | 75 GB/s (fastest 76.6) |

That fits about 78 GB/s and 0.05 ms per command: more than the 70 GB/s of the sixteen channels into sinks (see [Transfers alone](#transfers-alone)).

With the multiplication (`_/xdna2-lab/gemv_check.py generar|verificar`, `probar_gemv.sh`), **every value equals the reference** (the product of the operands rounded to bfp16, in 64-bit floats) in every size tried: 1 x 2560 x 2560, 1 x 2560 x 9216, 1 x 9216 x 2560, 4 x 2560 x 2560, 8 x 3072 x 512 and 1 x 2560 x 18432. Times, 50 runs, median:

| Rows x K x N | Weights | Time | Rate |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 x 2560 x 2560 | 7.4 MB | 0.181 ms | 41 GB/s |
| 1 x 2560 x 9216 | 26.5 MB | 0.434 ms | 61 GB/s |
| 1 x 9216 x 2560 | 26.5 MB | 0.434 ms | 61 GB/s |
| 1 x 2560 x 18432 | 53.1 MB | 0.781 ms | 68 GB/s |
| 4 x 2560 x 2560 | 7.4 MB | 0.374 ms | 20 GB/s |

 - With one row the multiplication adds about 10 % to the data path alone (0.434 against 0.392 ms).
 - With 4 rows it takes twice as long: the staging copies the rows of X byte by byte for every object (1,152 bytes with 4 rows), and then it no longer hides behind the arrival of the weights. To do: stage X once per tile of K and not once per object, or copy it in words.
 - What bounds generation, then, is bytes: in bfp16 a weight is 1.125 bytes, where Q4_K_M takes 0.5625 and the CPU reads that. Weights of 4 bits on the NPU are what can double the speed.

**From C++** (`xdna-gemv.{h,cpp}`, built on 2026/11/15). The manifest (`xdna2-gemv 1`; version 2 since the overlay takes Q4_K, 4 since the segments of formats; written by `_/xdna2-lab/gemv_manifest.py` from the `.prj` folder of a build) says where every core keeps its four parameters and which lock starts it, and which shim channel carries every stream W and Y. `fXdnaGemvShape` pads K to a multiple of 256 and N to a multiple of 512 (zeros, which add nothing). Checked without the NPU: the streams of `fXdnaGemvPlan` are identical, byte for byte, to those of mlir-aie for the six sizes, all from the manifest of one build (the six builds give the same PDI: one overlay serves every size); X and W packed in C++ are identical to those of `gemv_check.py`; Y read from what the NPU wrote equals the reference. `llama-xdna-insts gemv|gemv-pack|gemv-unpack` does each of these.

**In the runner and the backend.** `XdnaRunner::mOpen` takes the overlay for generating tokens as compute unit 1 of the same context (`XdnaDevice::mConfigureContextUnits`); `mLoadGemvWeights` converts a matrix one stream at a time (only the rows of one stream are floats at once: the output matrix of Qwen3.5 4B would be 2.5 GB of floats whole) and `mMultiplyRows` keeps the instruction stream of every size it meets, since generating repeats them. With `xdna2-gemv-bfp16.pdi` and `xdna2-gemv-bfp16.manifest` next to the overlay of the prompts (`_/xdna2/overlays-gemv/`), the backend sends every multiplication of 1 to 8 rows there, with the matrices of a layer that multiply the same rows in one command as before, and the logits too, since its sums are 32-bit floats; the weights that give the logits then live in the buffers of the backend. `GGML_XDNA_NO_GEMV` leaves it out. Checked with `llama-xdna-gemv`: every value equals the reference, also with padding (1 x 1000 x 3000, 3 x 2560 x 100), with 2 rows and K = 9216, and with the output matrix of Qwen3.5 4B (1 x 2560 x 248320, 10.35 ms in the NPU).

Reading Y went at first output by output, with a division and a remainder for each (2.9 ms for the 248,320 outputs of the logits, 13 ms of every token of Qwen3.5 4B); in the order of the buffer, 8 consecutive outputs at a time and with the threads for the large matrices, 0.24 ms.

**Qwen3.5 4B Q8_0 generating** (`llama-completion`, 32 tokens, on 2026/11/15): 84.7 ms per token, **11.8 tokens per second against 10.6 of the CPU alone** (+12 %), with every multiplication on the NPU, the logits included: 128 commands per token, about 82 ms of NPU, 2 ms reading results. The text is the CPU's for the first tokens and then takes another path, as rounding allows. The first time it converts the 4.2 G weights of 129 matrices for this overlay (1.4 s), and keeps them besides the ones of the overlay of the prompts.

**Threads that spin slow the NPU down.** Hours later the same run took 110 ms per token, with the NPU 110 ms of it, while `llama-xdna-gemv` gave the same times as before for the same shapes, also with 128 different matrices (3.4 GB) or 200 rounds in a row. The difference was the threads of OpenMP of the CPU backend: after every parallel region they spin for some milliseconds, and generating tokens the CPU backend runs a region every half millisecond, between two commands, so seven cores spin all the time the NPU works. With `OMP_WAIT_POLICY=passive` the NPU went back to 82 ms per token, and with 2 threads to 85 ms (passive threads slow down the operations of the CPU when it reads prompts, measured on 2026/11/13). Spinning cores probably take from the NPU the power of the processor, which a laptop shares: it showed only after hours of measuring, and it did not show in the first runs of the day. What the runner does since then (`XdnaWorkers::mWhileParked`, `XdnaRunner::mSubmitAndWait`): it waits for every command inside a parallel region of the same size as those of the CPU backend, where the other threads sleep on a condition, and between commands they spin as before. Generating with Qwen3.5 4B Q8_0, one run after the other: 83.8 ms per token parked against 109.8 not (`GGML_XDNA_NO_PARK` leaves them spinning, for comparing), with the same text.

**Qwen3.5 4B with both overlays** (`_/xdna2-lab/medir_4b_gemv.sh`, on 2026/11/15, threads parked; the laptop was hot from hours of measuring: the CPU alone came out 7 to 16 % slower than in the morning, see [Qwen3.5 4B](#qwen35-4b)):

| File | Prompt of 512, NPU | Prompt of 512, CPU alone | Generation, NPU | Generation, CPU alone |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q8_0 | 630 t/s | 82 t/s | 11.8 t/s | 9.3 t/s |
| Q6_K | 639 t/s | 186 t/s | 11.9 t/s | 12.3 t/s |
| Q5_K_M | 646 t/s | 190 t/s | 11.9 t/s | 12.8 t/s |
| Q4_K_M | 643 t/s | 127 t/s | 11.9 t/s | 14.5 t/s |

 - Generating, the NPU goes at the same speed whatever the file, since it reads every weight in bfp16: 26 % faster than the CPU with Q8_0, and 3, 7 and 18 % slower with Q6_K, Q5_K_M and Q4_K_M, which the CPU reads in 0.82, 0.69 and 0.56 bytes per weight against 1.125.
 - So weights of 4 bits for the NPU are what is left for generating faster than the CPU with every file. Until then the overlay for generating tokens stays out of the default folder of overlays (`_/xdna2/overlays-gemv/` has both).

### Weights in Q4_K for generating tokens

Built on 2026/11/15. The overlay for generating tokens takes a second format of objects: **Q4_K, the format of ggml, as it is**, which the cores decode themselves: 0.5625 bytes per weight instead of the 1.125 of bfp16. The analysis of [Weights of 4 bits](#weights-of-4-bits) held for the overlay of the prompts, where every tile of weights goes to 8 cores and each would decode it; here every core decodes only its own weights, once, so the decoding is paid once per weight, as the CPU pays it.

**The object.** 9216 bytes, the size of a bfp16 object, so the data path, the depth of the queues and the descriptors of the cores stay as they are (rewriting the lengths of the core descriptors per command would put the two buffers of a queue out of step): 32 outputs by 512 values of K, as 4 groups of 8 outputs by 2 blocks of 256 values, each a piece of 1152 bytes with the Q4_K blocks of 8 outputs laid out for the vector unit: d and dmin of the 8 outputs (bf16, 16 bytes each), the 12 bytes of 6-bit scales and minimums of each ([12][8]) and the 4-bit values ([8 sub-blocks][4 groups][32 bytes]: every group 8 outputs by 8 values of K, 64 values output after output, the first of every byte in its low half). 1152 bytes for 2048 weights: the 144 bytes per 256 of ggml. The host only moves bytes (`fXdnaGemvPackQ4KStream`), and rounds d and dmin from fp16 to bf16, to the nearest.

**The decoding, in the kernel** (`xdna2_gemv_bfp16` with format 1). AIE2P has no vectors of fp16 (`aie::vector<float16>` does not build) and no adders of bf16 (the AIE API makes them with the accumulators), so the decoding goes through bf16 products into 32-bit float accumulators:

 - A 4-bit value q, a 6-bit scale or a 6-bit minimum becomes bf16 exactly by putting the byte under the bits `0x43`: `0x4300 | q` is 128 + q. The 4-bit values arrive as vectors of `uint4` and `unpack()` to bytes; one interleave with a vector of `0x43` makes 64 bf16 values.
 - Per sub-block of 32 values: the factor D = bf16(d × sc), computed exactly as d × (128 + sc) − 128 d in an accumulator, and the base −128 D − dmin × m in 32-bit floats. Per value: base + (128 + q) × D in the accumulator (which is q × D − dmin × m), converted to bfp16 by the hardware (`to_vector<bfp16ebs8>`) and multiplied with `mac_8x8_8x8T`, as with bfp16 weights.
 - The core starts in the rounding mode `floor`: the kernel puts `conv_even` for Q4_K and gives the mode back after.
 - The scales of the 8 outputs are decoded the way `get_scale_min_k4` of ggml does it, on vectors of 64 bytes (lane 8 s + j: sub-block s, output j), once per piece, with the factors and bases of the 8 sub-blocks kept in the staging buffer: 7744 bytes with them. The core program takes 4,784 of the 16,384 bytes.

What it took to get there:

 - **A vector load of 32 bytes from an address that is not a multiple of 32 loads, without any error, from the multiple of 32 below it.** The dmin of a piece are at byte 16: loading them read the d again, and every value came out about 7 % off. They are taken from the vector of the d, shifted (`shuffle_down`).
 - The reference has to round the decoded values the way a core converts floats: to the nearest, and when the largest mantissa of a block rounds up to +128, with the exponent of the block one higher (`bfp.quantize(..., rounding="conv_even")` of mlir-aie). The encoder of the host keeps that mantissa at +127 instead; with it as the reference the error was 1.15·10⁻³.
 - In a rare block (one in millions of weights) the cores still round otherwise, which leaves an output one step of a weight away (2.4·10⁻⁴ of the largest value, one output in 2560 with K = 9216). The checks let through at most one output in 1000 up to 10⁻³ of the largest value with Q4_K, and 10⁻⁵ for all the others, as with bfp16.
 - The first kernel that gave exact results took 14,300 cycles per object. Decoding the scales once per piece instead of once per sub-block, a copy of X in words for one row, one set of sums and the values read as `uint4` brought it to about 6,000: 2.7 weights per cycle per core, 79 G weights per second with the 16 cores. The 16 channels bring 76 GB/s, 135 G weights per second of Q4_K: the kernel, not the data, still bounds it, and 3,450 cycles per object would be needed.

**From C++.** The manifest is `xdna2-gemv 2` (version 4 since the segments, see [Q5_K, Q6_K and segments of formats](#q5_k-q6_k-and-segments-of-formats)), with a line `formats bfp16 q4k`; the cores take the format as a fifth parameter, and every group of 8 values of K of X takes the bytes of its rows padded to a multiple of 4 (one row: 12 bytes), so that the kernel copies it in words. The overlay of version 1 is not taken. Checked without the NPU: the streams of `fXdnaGemvPlan` are identical to those of mlir-aie for Q4_K with 1 x 2560 x 2560 and 1 x 9216 x 2560, and X and W packed in C++ (W from blocks of ggml) identical to those of `gemv_check.py` for those two and 4 x 2560 x 2560. `XdnaRunner::mLoadGemvWeightsQ4K` takes the rows of blocks one stream at a time; `llama-xdna-gemv --format q4k` multiplies random blocks of Q4_K (d from 2⁻¹¹ to 2⁻⁷ and dmin from 0 to 2⁻⁸, subnormals of fp16 and both signs included) and checks them against the decoding of the kernel made on the processor (`fAsTheNpuQ4K`). On the NPU every size passes: 1 x 2560 x 2560, 1 x 9216 x 2560, 4 x 2560 x 2560, 3 x 2560 x 1000 and 8 x 512 x 700.

Times through the runner, 50 rounds, median, Q4_K and bfp16 one after the other, twice:

| Rows x K x N | Q4_K | bfp16 | Q4_K, G weights per second |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 x 2560 x 2560 | 0.154-0.155 ms | 0.184-0.186 ms | 43 |
| 1 x 9216 x 2560 | 0.315 ms | 0.416-0.428 ms | 75 |
| 1 x 2560 x 9216 | 0.313 ms | 0.424-0.432 ms | 75 |
| 1 x 2560 x 12352 | 0.398 ms | 0.557-0.566 ms | 79 |
| 4 x 2560 x 2560 | 0.251-0.256 ms | 0.213-0.214 ms | 26 |

 - Q4_K is 1.2 to 1.4 times as fast as bfp16 with one row, where the bytes would allow 2.
 - With 4 rows Q4_K is slower than bfp16: the copy of X in words has a fast path only for one row (3 words per group), and Q4_K copies 64 groups per object instead of 32.

**In the backend.** A matrix whose tensors are all Q4_K (`fGemvFormatOf`) goes as it is; any other one, and a group of tensors held together for one command (see [Fewer commands](#fewer-commands)) with any tensor of another type, goes in bfp16 as before. Splitting such a group into one command per format was tried and measured with Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M: 30 more commands per token (5,183 against 4,226 in 32 tokens) and no gain (70.9 against 70.3 ms per token), since what a tensor saves in Q4_K (about 5 ps per weight with this kernel, 50 µs for an `attn_gate` of 10.5 million weights) is about what a command costs. It will pay with a kernel bound by the data.

**Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M generating** (`llama-completion`, 32 tokens, on 2026/11/15): **14.2 tokens per second, against 11.9 when every weight went in bfp16** and 16.7 of the CPU alone right after (the laptop was cooler than for the table above), with the same text as the CPU. 83 of the 129 matrices go in Q4_K. The file has more than Q4_K: `attn_qkv` is Q5_K (503 million weights, and with it `attn_gate`, `ssm_beta` and `ssm_alpha` of its group go in bfp16), and Q6_K are half of the `ffn_down` (401 million), some `attn_v` (with the `attn_q` and `attn_k` of their group) and the embeddings that are also the output matrix (636 million): 46 matrices in bfp16. Per token the NPU reads about 3.5 GB and the CPU the 2.77 GB of the file; about 67 of the 70 ms of a token are the NPU, with 132 commands.

Speed with `llama-bench -p 512 -n 32 -r 3 -t 8` and precision (`_/xdna2-lab/medir_4b_q4k.sh`, results in `_/xdna2/work/backend-test/4b-q4k/`), the NPU and the CPU alone one after the other, twice:

| What multiplies | Prompt of 512 | Generation |
|---|---|---|
| NPU (both overlays) | 715 / 681 t/s | 14.19 / 14.21 t/s |
| CPU alone | 149 / 147 t/s | 15.49 / 15.58 t/s |

Precision of the path for generating tokens: KL against the CPU with Q8_0, with the 2048 tokens of `backend-test/texto.txt` in batches of 8 (`-b 8`), so that every multiplication goes through the overlay for generating tokens, and the CPU alone in the same batches (the CPU with Q8_0 gives a perplexity of 15.18):

| What multiplies | Perplexity | KL divergence, mean | Same most likely token |
|---|---|---|---|
| NPU | 15.68 | 0.044512 ± 0.002746 | 88.82 % |
| CPU alone | 15.67 | 0.043275 ± 0.002569 | 88.33 % |

 - Generating, the NPU went from 18 % slower than the CPU alone (all in bfp16) to 9 % slower. The prompt does not change: the overlay of the prompts still reads every weight in bfp16.
 - Decoding Q4_K in the cores and rounding it to bfp16 costs nothing measurable: the NPU is as precise as the CPU with the same file, within the error.
 - What is left for generating faster than the CPU, in milliseconds of the 67 of NPU per token (estimates from the times above): the kernel of Q4_K bound by the data instead of by its computing (2.37 G weights go in Q4_K: 30 ms now, about 18 bound by the data), Q5_K and Q6_K as they are and groups of several formats in one command (about 10 ms: 1.95 G weights still go in bfp16), and fewer commands (132 per token, about 7 ms of fixed costs; 4 in flight or chains need what runs between them on the NPU too). With the first two, about 45 ms of NPU per token: some 20 tokens per second.

### Thirty-two cores for generating tokens

Built on 2026/11/15 (`gemv_stream.py --split 2`, the default since). Every input channel of the shims goes into the memory tile of its column, which gives the first half of every object of 9216 bytes to one core and the second half to another (`split` of IRON at the memory tile), and joins the sums of the two in the same order (`join`): all 32 cores (rows 2 to 5), each with 16 outputs of every block of 32 of its stream. An object is laid out by groups of 8 outputs, so its halves are groups 0 and 1, and 2 and 3: W and Y stay as they were in the host. X goes in objects of a core (4608 bytes), each twice in a row, so that both cores of a channel get all of it (`fXdnaGemvPackX`). Per column the memory tile uses its 6 input channels (2 from the shim, 4 from the cores) and its 6 output ones (4 to the cores, 2 to the shim). With the objects of a core half as large, a core keeps up to 36,864 bytes of X (3 rows with K = 9216). The manifest says how many cores a stream feeds (`split 2`).

Checked: the streams of `fXdnaGemvPlan` identical to those of mlir-aie for 4 shapes (one PDI for all), X and W packed in C++ identical to those of `gemv_check.py ... <split>`, and on the NPU every value as the reference with 5 shapes (also 3 rows with K = 9216). Times, 50 rounds, median, through the runner:

| Rows x K x N | Q4_K, 16 cores | Q4_K, 32 cores | bfp16, 32 cores |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 x 2560 x 2560 | 0.154 ms | 0.145 ms | 0.188 ms |
| 1 x 9216 x 2560 | 0.315 ms | 0.267 ms | 0.424 ms |
| 1 x 2560 x 12352 | 0.398 ms | 0.331 ms | 0.566 ms |

**With 32 cores, Q4_K is bound by the data path, not by the cores**: with the same bytes it takes what bfp16 takes (1 x 2560 x 6144 in bfp16, 0.319 ms; 1 x 2560 x 12288 in Q4_K, 0.323-0.326 ms; both 17.7 MB). That path brings about 71 GB/s plus 0.05 ms per command. Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M: 15.06 tokens per second (66.4 ms per token), against 14.22 with 16 cores.

### Q5_K, Q6_K and segments of formats

Built on 2026/11/15. The overlay takes four formats of weights (bfp16, and the Q4_K, Q5_K and Q6_K of ggml as they are), and the outputs of a command come in up to 4 segments, each in a format: the tensors that a layer multiplies by the same rows (see [Fewer commands](#fewer-commands)) go in one command even when their files gave them different types.

**A stream of pieces.** The cores take every block of 16 outputs as a stream of pieces, one for every group of 8 outputs and tile of 256 values of K, tile after tile, cut into objects of 4608 bytes (`kernels/xdna2-gemv-bfp16.cc`, which gives the layouts):

| Format | Bytes of a piece | Bytes per weight, K = 2560 | K = 9216 | In the file |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| bfp16 | 2304 | 1.125 | 1.125 | (floats) |
| Q4_K | 1152 | 0.5625 | 0.5625 | 0.5625 |
| Q5_K | 1408 | 0.7875 | 0.6875 | 0.6875 |
| Q6_K | 1696 | 0.9 | 0.844 | 0.8203 |

 - A piece of Q6_K (8 outputs by 210 bytes, and 16 bytes so that its parts start at multiples of 32) does not divide an object: a piece that does not fit in what is left of an object goes on in the next one, and the kernel puts its two parts together in a buffer of its own before decoding it. Every block starts a new object, since a core releases its sums at the end of a block and only between objects: the rest of the last object of a block is padding, which is what separates the bytes per weight above from those of the file (10 % with Q6_K and K = 2560, 3 % with K = 9216).
 - Q5_K: the low 4 bits as in Q4_K, and the fifth bits apart, 8 bytes per group, bit t of byte j for lane 8 j + t: the two words of a group are a mask of its 64 lanes (`aie::mask<64>::from_uint32`), and the value is `select(low, low | 16, mask)`.
 - Q6_K: the 8-bit scales are signed. With its low 7 bits as l, d × scale is d × (128 + l) − 128 d for a positive scale and d × (128 + l) − 256 d for a negative one: two products of bf16, exact in 32 bits. The top 2 bits of the values go in fields of 4 bits, field k of a group the bits of lane k and, two bits up, those of lane k + 32: one unpack gives both halves of the lanes. A value is (128 + q) × D − 160 × D, exact.
 - The X of a tile is copied into the staging buffer once per tile (32 groups), with a copy for every number of rows (`fStageWords`): with a loop over the words of a group, 4 rows took up to 68 % longer than one.

**Segments.** Every segment is shared out between the 16 streams by itself (stream s makes 1 / 16 of the outputs of every segment), padded to a multiple of 512 outputs, so that all the cores do the same: one W of a stream is [segment][block][objects]. A core takes 18 parameters: the objects of X after the first, the rows, and for each of the 4 segments its blocks, its objects per block after the first, its tiles of K and its format; the manifest (`xdna2-gemv 4`) has the lines `segments 4` and `formats bfp16 q4k q5k q6k`. In C++: `XdnaGemvPart` (what a segment is asked to be), `XdnaGemvSegment` (as the array runs it), `fXdnaGemvPackStream` (the weights of a segment and a stream, from floats or from the blocks of ggml) and Y read segment by segment; `XdnaRunner::mLoadGemvMatrix` takes the members of a matrix (`XdnaGemvMember`: format, outputs and how to read their rows), and the backend gives every tensor its format (`fGemvFormatOf`).

**One write per core.** With 18 parameters written word by word, as mlir-aie does, a command carried 784 operations and everything went 7 to 19 % slower than with 5 parameters; written in one block write per core (`XdnaInstructions::mBlockWrite`), 240 operations, and the times went back to those before or below. The streams are then no longer byte for byte those of mlir-aie (they were, word by word, for 6 shapes, also with 2, 3 and 4 segments); the results on the NPU check them.

**Checked.** On the NPU every value as the reference in every size tried, at the first run of every format: 1 to 8 rows, K = 512, 2560 and 9216, N padded, and commands of 2, 3 and 4 segments. `llama-xdna-gemv` also compares its decoding (the one of the kernel, in floats) with that of ggml (`ggml_get_type_traits`): 0.5 to 0.7 % of the largest weight, what rounding d and d × scale to bf16 leaves. Times, 50 rounds, median:

| Rows x K x N | bfp16 | Q4_K | Q5_K | Q6_K |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 x 2560 x 2560 | 0.181 ms | 0.141 ms | 0.158 ms | 0.167 ms |
| 1 x 2560 x 9216 | 0.423 ms | 0.265 ms | 0.330 ms | 0.362 ms |
| 1 x 9216 x 2560 | 0.421 ms | 0.261 ms | 0.300 ms | 0.349 ms |
| 4 x 2560 x 2560 | 0.184 ms | 0.149 ms | 0.162 ms | 0.171 ms |
| 6 x 2560 x 2560 | 0.188 ms | 0.154 ms | 0.165 ms | 0.173 ms |

 - A group of 8192 outputs in Q5_K and 4160 in Q4_K (`attn_qkv` with `attn_gate`, `ssm_beta` and `ssm_alpha` of Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M): 0.389-0.393 ms in one command, against 0.301 + 0.178 ms in two and 0.574 ms in bfp16.
 - Q6_K and Q4_K go at the rate of the data path; Q5_K with K = 9216 at about 65 GB/s, near it (before the mask for its fifth bits, 54).
 - 4 to 6 rows cost 2 to 9 % more than one (before the copy of X per number of rows, up to 68 %): the prediction of several tokens at once (MTP) can use them.

**Qwen3.5 4B with every format** (`_/xdna2-lab/medir_4b_formatos.sh`, on 2026/11/15, before the mask of Q5_K and the copy of X per number of rows; results in `_/xdna2/work/backend-test/4b-formatos/`): `llama-bench -p 512 -n 32 -r 3 -t 8`, the NPU and the CPU alone one after the other for every file, and KL against the CPU with Q8_0 in batches of 8 tokens (every multiplication through the overlay for generating tokens; the CPU in the same batches):

| File | Prompt of 512, NPU / CPU | Generation, NPU / CPU | KL, NPU | KL, CPU |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q8_0 | 723 / 98 t/s | **12.14** / 10.00 t/s | 0.001493 (same token 97.94 %) | the reference |
| Q6_K | 686 / 218 t/s | **14.36** / 12.85 t/s | 0.006128 (95.10 %) | 0.007660 (94.31 %) |
| Q5_K_M | 679 / 222 t/s | **14.87** / 13.31 t/s | 0.012959 (92.94 %) | 0.013925 (93.14 %) |
| Q4_K_M | 676 / 149 t/s | **17.35** / 15.66 t/s | 0.042503 (88.53 %) | 0.043275 (88.33 %) |

 - **The NPU generates faster than the CPU alone with every file**: 21 % with Q8_0 (which goes in bfp16, 1.125 bytes per weight against the 1.0625 of the file), 12 % with Q6_K and Q5_K_M and 11 % with Q4_K_M, as precise as the CPU with the same file or a little more. With Q4_K_M, `llama-completion` gives the same text as the CPU.
 - Every matrix of Q4_K_M goes now in its own format: of the 129 matrices the backend keeps for this overlay, 48 are one tensor of Q4_K, 35 groups of Q4_K, 24 groups of Q5_K and Q4_K, 5 groups of Q4_K and Q6_K and 17 one tensor of Q6_K; none is converted to bfp16.

With the mask of Q5_K and the copy of X per number of rows, the same day, with the laptop hot from hours of measuring (`medir_4b_formatos.sh 4b-formatos-paso3 velocidad`; the CPU alone 7 to 12 % slower than in the table above, the NPU about the same):

| File | Prompt of 512, NPU / CPU | Generation, NPU / CPU |
|---|---|---|
| Q8_0 | 639 / 80 t/s | **11.99** / 9.12 t/s |
| Q6_K | 632 / 183 t/s | **14.29** / 11.55 t/s |
| Q5_K_M | 628 / 186 t/s | **14.96** / 12.41 t/s |
| Q4_K_M | 618 / 127 t/s | **17.00** / 14.59 t/s |

 - Generating one token at a time, the mask and the copy of X change nothing measurable (only `attn_qkv` is Q5_K in Q4_K_M); they are for Q5_K_M and for several rows. The NPU now holds its speed when the laptop is hot, and the CPU does not: 17 to 31 % faster than it here.
 - What bounds the NPU now, for Q4_K_M: about 2.85 GB per token (with the padding of the blocks) at the 71 GB/s of the data path, about 40 ms, and 132 commands at about 0.05 ms, 7 ms, of the 57.6 ms of a token. Fewer commands per token need the operations between the multiplications on the NPU too (phases 2 and 3 of `_/TareasPorHacer.md`).
 - Since it generates faster than the CPU with every file, the overlay for generating tokens is also in the default folder of overlays, `_/xdna2/overlays/` (built in `work/gemv9-mix-1-2560`). With Q5_K_M from there, `llama-completion` gives the text of the CPU for the first lines and then takes another path at a token where two are almost even, as rounding allows (14.75 against 12.31 tokens per second).

### The prompt of Qwen3.5 4B: host, processor and overlap

Measured and changed on 2026/11/15 with Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M and batches of 512 tokens (`llama-bench -p 512 -n 0 -t 8`). At the start a batch took about 704 ms: the NPU 171, converting rows 48 and reading results 109 on the host, and about 313 the operations of the processor. Every change below keeps the results bit for bit: the logits of the processor alone byte for byte against the code before (`llama-perplexity --kl-divergence-base` and `cmp`, the variants `zen4` and `haswell` of the CPU backend), and the logits with the NPU byte for byte with and without each change of the backend.

 - **The host converts the rows of a group once** (`XdnaRunner::mPackRows`, `mMultiplyPacked`): the pieces of a matrix along its outputs share the conversion, the pieces along its inputs read their columns in place (`fXdnaGemmPackA(..., pStride)`) and add as C is read (`fXdnaGemmUnpackC(..., pAdd)`), and the instruction streams are kept by shape (`aGemmPlans`). The backend without the NPU went from about 211 to 168 ms per batch.
 - **The SwiGLU of a feed-forward layer is read with C** (`fMulMatGlu`, `XdnaRunner::mUnpackSwiglu`, `fXdnaGemmUnpackSwiglu`, `xdna-glu.h`): the backend takes the `GLU` node; when it follows the multiplications by `ffn_gate` and `ffn_up` and nothing else reads them (the use counts of the whole graph), they go in two commands and their two C are read together into `silu(gate) * up`, with the vector exponential of the CPU backend of the machine, in blocks of 8 x 8. `GGML_XDNA_NO_GLU` leaves it to the processor. 737 to 792 tokens per second (+7.4 %).
 - **The norms of the processor sum 8 rows at a time**: `RMS_NORM` added the squares of a row in a double precision chain where every addition waited for the one before; now 8 rows go in the lanes of a vector, each in the same order (`ggml_rms_norm_sums_8` in `ggml/src/ggml-cpu/ops.cpp`). 30.5 to 23.4 ms per batch.
 - **The short convolution reads its input by tokens** (layers of `qwen35` on the processor without rollback states): `build_conv_state` (`src/models/delta-net-base.cpp`, `by_tokens`) concatenates the states (transposed) and the projection along dimension 1, which copies whole rows, and gives `ggml_ssm_conv` a transposed view; the state to keep goes back to the cache transposed. The CPU backend goes along the rows, vectors across the channels, the same fused multiply-adds in the same order (`ggml_compute_forward_ssm_conv_f32_by_tokens`, the weights by tap in its work buffer), fused with the `SILU` after it (`ggml_cpu_try_fuse_ops`, `ggml_vec_silu_f32` on runs of 512 channels). Before, the concatenation transposed the projection (16.8 MB per layer, reading a whole page apart for every token) and the convolution slid along rows a page apart. Checked with batches whole, in pieces of 128 (the state goes from piece to piece) and token by token. `CONCAT` 33.9 to 16.9 ms per batch, `SSM_CONV` with the `SILU` inside 34.1 to 18.9, the `SILU` left 11.9 to 5.7: 833 to 914 tokens per second. Elsewhere (RPC, tensor mode) the graph is that of upstream: an RPC server of upstream would abort on the transposed input. A first attempt, a copy of the transposed concatenation in tiles of 16 x 16 in registers, gave nothing (bound by memory) and was removed.
 - **The NPU and the host overlap** (`XdnaRunner::mSubmitPacked`, `mFinishPacked`, two buffers of A and one command buffer per buffer of C): `fMultiply` keeps up to two commands in flight; each one goes before the C of the one before it is read, and the rows of the next range of inputs are converted into the other buffer of A while the NPU works. C is still read in the order the commands went, so the pieces along the inputs add up in the same order. The gate and the up of a SwiGLU go one right after the other. `GGML_XDNA_NO_OVERLAP` waits for every command. Interleaved: 852, 881 and 842 tokens per second without, 967, 901, 907 and 900 with it (+5 %): the multiplications of several pieces (`ffn_down`, K = 9216, goes in three). Then a matrix that would go in one command, of several members with a first one of 4096 outputs or more, is cut in two after it (`fRangesForOverlap`: the input of the linear attention in 8192 + 4160, `attn_q/k/v` in 8192 + 2048), so that the host reads the C of the larger while the NPU multiplies the smaller (32 commands more per batch, the same logits byte for byte): 884 and 843 against 943, 914 and 905 (about 860 against 920 without and with both, +7 %).

Where a batch of 512 goes now (about 568 ms, from the report of the backend and a temporary timer in the node loop of the CPU backend):

| Part | ms per batch |
|---|---|
| NPU | 170 |
| Converting rows (host) | 59 |
| Reading results (host) | 94 |
| `GATED_DELTA_NET` | 92 |
| `FLASH_ATTN_EXT` | 44 |
| `RMS_NORM` (with the `MUL` of its weights) | 25 |
| `SSM_CONV` (with its `SILU`) | 19 |
| `CONCAT` | 17 |
| `ADD`, `SIGMOID`, `SILU`, `MUL`, `CONT`, `SCALE`, `ROPE` | 34 |

 - The table is from before the overlap; with it, part of the host hides behind the NPU, and the backend takes about 300 ms of a batch instead of about 325 (the reading of results is a little slower while the NPU streams from memory at the same time).
 - The NPU part is bound by the weights: 4.7 GB per batch in bfp16 through the 4 shim channels that bring them. The overlay that brings them through 8 channels (`overlays-split`) gives the same within the noise with the 4B (921 against 909 tokens per second, two passes each). Larger batches read them less often, but the processor loses what the NPU saves: a prompt of 2048 tokens at 723, 717 and 682 tokens per second with batches of 512, 1024 and 2048.
 - `GATED_DELTA_NET` and `FLASH_ATTN_EXT` run near the 32-bit float peak of the processor (about 0.55 TFLOPS): only the NPU can take them further (phase 2).
 - Then, the same day, the rest of the host side, every one with the logits byte for byte as before, and `GGML_XDNA_NO_FUSE` to compare (it gives the last three back to the processor):
   - **The SwiGLU in two halves**: the gate and the up are cut in halves (`fRangesForOverlap`), and the SwiGLU of the first half is read while the NPU multiplies the second (`fMulMatGlu`, four buffers of C in `XdnaRunner`; `fXdnaGemmUnpackSwiglu` takes the column of the half in the whole row, so that every column takes the vectors of the CPU backend).
   - **The residual `ADD` read with C** after `ssm_out`, `attn_output` and `ffn_down`: every output becomes `(base + C) + residual` (`XdnaGemmTarget::vBase` and `vAlso`), base being the pieces along K added up before in the result of the multiplication, so the order of the sums is that of the multiplication followed by the `ADD` of the CPU; generating tokens, the `ADD` goes on the host (`fAddOnHost`).
   - **The `CONCAT` of the convolution written by the multiplication**: the result of `attn_qkv` goes straight into it, after its 3 rows of states (`XdnaBackend::vRedirect`, `fConcatHead`), also generating tokens. For that it has to come right after the projections: `qwen35.cpp` reads the states from the cache before the norm of the layer (`build_conv_states`).
   - **The `RMS_NORM` of the residual stream and its `MUL` applied as the rows are converted**: the scales of the rows are computed as the CPU does (`fXdnaRmsScales`: the squares added in double in the same order, 8 rows in the lanes of a vector), and every value is converted as `(x * scale) * weight` (`XdnaRowNorm` in `fXdnaGemmPackA`); the normalised rows are not written. The scheduler did not give the norms to the backend at first: `llama-context.cpp` puts every tensor called `norm` on the device of its layer when the layers are offloaded (`-ngl` high, even without a GPU); now an accelerator that works on host memory takes the norm of a layer of the processor when it accepts it. `GGML_SCHED_DEBUG=2` with `-lv 5` prints where every node goes.
   - Interleaved three times: 876, 871 and 881 tokens per second without, 895, 900 and 894 with them (+2.3 %), and 16.8 against 16.5 generating. Less than the estimate (about 36 ms): C is written past the cache, so what the processor read from the cache before (the `CONCAT` it copied itself) now comes from memory (`SSM_CONV` from 19 to 22.5 ms per batch).

### Against the integrated GPU (Vulkan)
