Measured on 2026/11/15 with llama.cpp v1.0.0 of upstream (the last release, of 2026/11/13), built with `-DGGML_VULKAN=ON` in `_/upstream/build-vulkan`, on the Radeon 860M of the laptop (RADV GFX1152, Mesa 25.0.7), against this fork with the NPU, one after the other: `llama-bench -m <model> -p 512 -n 64 -r 3` (with `-ngl 99` for Vulkan), Qwen3.5 4B:

| File | Prompt of 512, NPU | Prompt of 512, Vulkan | Generation, NPU | Generation, Vulkan |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q8_0 | **919** | 352 | 12.0 | **15.8** |
| Q6_K | **908** | 346 | 14.3 | **19.0** |
| Q5_K_M | **917** | 329 | 15.2 | **21.2** |
| Q4_K_M | **900** | 342 | 17.2 | **23.9** |

 - The NPU reads prompts 2.6 to 2.8 times as fast as the GPU; the GPU generates 32 to 39 % faster.
 - The GPU runs a whole token at once, at about 66 GB/s; the NPU loses about 13 ms per token in the fixed cost of its 129 commands and 6.7 ms on the host and the processor (see [What a command costs, and where a token goes](#what-a-command-costs-and-where-a-token-goes)). Without them, its data at about 80 GB/s would give some 28 tokens per second with Q4_K_M: fewer commands per token is what generation needs.

### What a command costs, and where a token goes

Measured on 2026/11/15 with Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M generating (`llama-bench -n 64`, 17.24 tokens per second, 58 ms per token):

 - **The NPU, 50.8 ms in 129 commands**, exactly what the same commands take one by one: the medians of `llama-xdna-gemv` with the shapes and formats of the model add up to 50.84 ms. Nothing is lost between commands on the NPU beyond the fixed cost of each.
 - The host of the backend, 2.5 ms (converting X 0.6, reading Y 1.8, of them 0.58 flushing the cache), and the operations of the processor, 4.2 ms; 2.4 of them are copies of the recurrent state (`GET_ROWS` 1.6 and `CPY` 0.8: 2 MB per layer and token, read from the cache into the graph and back).

One command of the overlay for generating tokens, alone (`llama-xdna-gemv --repeat 50`):

| Shape and format | Median | Rate beyond 0.05 ms |
|---|---|---|
| 1 x 2560 x 18432 Q4_K (`ffn_gate` with `ffn_up`) | 0.433 ms | 69 GB/s |
| 1 x 2560 x 8192 Q5_K + 4160 Q4_K (`attn_qkv` with the gate, beta and alpha) | 0.389 ms | 70 GB/s |
| 1 x 2560 x 248320 Q6_K (the logits) | 8.062 ms | 71 GB/s |
| 1 x 9216 x 18432 Q6_K | 1.901 ms | 77 GB/s |
| 1 x 9216 x 2560 Q6_K (`ffn_down`) | 0.351-0.469 ms | 47-66 GB/s |
| 1 x 9216 x 2560 Q4_K (`ffn_down`) | 0.262 ms | 62 GB/s |
| 1 x 4096 x 2560 Q4_K (`ssm_out`, `attn_output`) | 0.171-0.176 ms | 47-49 GB/s |
| 1 x 2560 x 2560 Q4_K | 0.147 ms | 38 GB/s |

From the large ones, a command costs about 0.10-0.12 ms plus its bytes at about 80 GB/s. That fixed cost, measured with streams that only write again and again into the runtime parameters of a core that waits for its barrier (`llama-xdna-insts empty --writes <n> [--words <w> | --patches 1] --at <col>,<row>,<register>`, which nothing reads meanwhile):

| Command | Median |
|---|---|
| empty | 0.054 ms |
| 256 writes | 0.067 ms (0.05 µs each) |
| 1024 writes | 0.116 ms (0.06 µs each) |
| 256 block writes of 18 words | 0.088 ms (0.13 µs each) |
| 256 patches of an address | 0.103 ms (0.19 µs each) |

 - The 240 operations of a command for generating tokens (64 for the parameters and barriers of the 32 cores, 64 for draining Y, 96 for X and W, 16 waits) cost about 23 µs; the rest of the fixed cost, about 25 µs, is filling and draining the data path. With 129 commands, about 13 ms of every token.
 - Fewer operations (the parameters inside the stream of data, Y joined by columns, addresses written without patches) would save about 2 ms per token; removing the padding of Q5_K and Q6_K (126 MB per token), about 1.3 ms, but it needs an object shared by two blocks, which the object FIFOs of IRON do not allow (their acquisitions are fixed per loop); not copying the recurrent state, about 2 ms, but the operation of the linear attention would have to read and write the state in the cache.
 - The large lever is fewer commands per token: with the light operations between the multiplications (norms, residual sums, SwiGLU) on the NPU too, the 4 commands of a layer could be 1 or 2, some 6 to 10 ms per token. That is the way towards everything on the NPU (phase 2).

### The chained overlay

A second overlay for generating tokens, built on 2026/11/15 (design `ggml/src/ggml-xdna/overlays/designs/gemv_chain.py`, kernel `ggml/src/ggml-xdna/kernels/xdna2-gemv-chain.cc`, manifest `xdna2-gemv 5` written by `ggml/src/ggml-xdna/overlays/designs/gemv_chain_manifest.py`; version 6 the same day, see "Version 6 of the chained overlay" below), so that one command runs several multiplications one after the other with the light operations between them. **Measured** on the laptop:

 - **The two cores of a channel share K, not the outputs.** In the overlay of [An overlay for generating tokens](#an-overlay-for-generating-tokens) the memory tile gives each core of a pair half of every object, so X has to go twice. A phase that reads its X from the Y of the phase before cannot duplicate it piecewise: the shim keeps its strides minus one, so an inner stride of 0 does not exist (read in mlir-aie). Here core 0 (rows 2 and 4) makes the first half of the tiles of K of every block of 32 outputs and core 1 (rows 3 and 5) the second, and sends its sums over the cascade (`CascadeFlow` of IRON, `put_mcd` and `get_scd_v16float`); core 0 adds them, applies the epilogue and sends the block of Y. Every object of a channel is [4608 bytes of core 0 | 4608 bytes of core 1].
 - **The parameters travel with the data**: the first object of every command, the same in both halves, a table of 5 rows of 32 integers (one row per phase, up to 4, and the number of phases). No runtime parameters, barriers or writes of registers of the cores: 128 operations for a command of one phase instead of 240.
 - **Phases**: a prologue (X in bfp16 from the host, 1 to 8 rows; X in floats, 1 row, which the cores round with `conv_even`; or the RMS norm of h, whose sums of squares core 1 sends over the cascade and whose scale core 0 applies to the sums), then the blocks of up to 2 segments, then an epilogue on row 0 (Y + r kept as h; Y + h; or the SwiGLU of two segments, with an exponential of bf16 precision). The X of a phase can come from the Y of a phase before it through a pattern of four dimensions (sizes [objects, 2, 144, 8], strides [144 x 64, K / 16 x 64, 64, 1] words), with a wait for the drains of Y in between.
 - Program memory is the tight resource: core 0 uses 16,080 of its 16,384 bytes with 2 segments per phase (4 did not fit; `-Os` for the prologue, while it crashes Peano on the epilogue). Version 6 brought it down to 14,560 (see below).
 - Lab results: one phase of 1 x 2560 x 2560 Q4_K with X in floats, exact (1.07 x 10^-7 of the largest value), 0.131 ms; the feed-forward block of a layer of Qwen3.5 4B in Q4_K in one command of three phases (4096 -> 2560 plus the residual; the norm, 2 x 9216 and their SwiGLU; 9216 -> 2560 plus h), h within 6 x 10^-8, the gate within 7 x 10^-8, the SwiGLU within 2 x 10^-3 (the exponential) and the output within 8 x 10^-3 of their largest values, in 0.796 ms. Weights centred on zero as a model's are: with uniform random blocks the gates are all very negative and the SwiGLU is not tried.

The host side (`ggml/src/ggml-xdna/xdna-chain.h`, `xdna-chain.cpp`), **measured**:

 - `XdnaShimDma` writes buffer descriptors of up to four dimensions (`XdnaShimTransfer::vSizes`, `vStrides`, `vIterations`): words 3 to 6 of a descriptor hold the two inner sizes and the strides minus one, and the iterations and their stride; the queue of the channel runs the descriptor once per iteration, which is the repetition of the push. The patterns it writes are those of mlir-aie word for word (the X from Y of the feed-forward test and the drain of two segments of the same blocks). `mPrepare` and `mStart` write a descriptor and queue it apart, and `mSettle` says that a channel is empty because what it fed has been waited for.
 - A command of the chained overlay has one buffer of the host (slot 0: the parameters, the residuals of every stream, the X that come from the host and the weights of the norms, and the Y of every phase from a page of its own on) and up to 4 matrices (slots 1 to 4), each laid out as `[stream][segment][block][objects]`, with K rounded up to 512 so that every core gets whole tiles. Its plan (`fXdnaChainPlan`): the drains of every stream (two segments of the same blocks in one descriptor of two iterations), then per stream the parameters (with the residual of the stream, two objects of one descriptor with an iteration stride), X, the weights of the norm and the weights; a phase that reads a Y waits first for the drains of the phases before.
 - The same feed-forward block from C++ (`llama-xdna-gemv --ffn`): the same errors as the lab, 0.805 ms (the weights of every matrix in a buffer of their own add 128 operations); one phase of 1 x 2560 x 2560 Q4_K, 0.139 ms, as the other overlay. Every format, 4 rows with X in bfp16, unpadded sizes in bfp16 and 4 segments in two phases agree with the reference.
 - In the backend, with `xdna2-gemv-chain.pdi` and `xdna2-gemv-chain.manifest` next to the overlay of the prompts, the chained overlay takes the place of the other one (`GGML_XDNA_NO_CHAIN` keeps the other): the multiplications of 1 to 8 rows go to it (a matrix of 2 tensors as 2 segments, as a SwiGLU needs; of more, consecutive tensors of one format together; 4 segments at most, in two phases), and when a token is generated, the feed-forward block of every layer (`MUL_MAT` -> `ADD` -> `RMS_NORM` -> `MUL` -> 2 x `MUL_MAT` -> `GLU` -> `MUL_MAT` -> `ADD`, all in one part of the scheduler) goes in one command of three phases (`fFeedForward`; `GGML_XDNA_NO_CHAIN_FFN` sends its multiplications apart). The last layer was not chained at first: llama.cpp puts a `GET_ROWS` of the rows it outputs in the middle (see "The last layer and the logits" below).
 - Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M generating (`llama-bench -n 64`, alternating): 18.30 tokens per second against 17.32 with the other overlay, about 72 commands per token instead of 129; the prompt of 512 does not change (it is the other overlay). The precision of the path with batches of one token (`llama-perplexity -b 1`, against the processor with Q8_0): KL 0.0421 and the same token 89.0 % of the times, against 0.0418 with the other overlay and 0.0437 for the processor alone with the same file.
 - **One command per layer** (step E, 2026/11/15). When a token is generated, the scheduler already gives the backend the end of a layer and the start of the next in one part (seen with `GGML_SCHED_DEBUG=2 -lv 5`: the feed-forward block, `l_out`, the `RMS_NORM` and `MUL` of the next layer, its projections and, in a layer of linear attention, the `CONCAT` of the input of the convolution; the states of the convolution are read before, with the operations of the processor). `fFeedForward` takes them as a fourth phase: the norm of the output, read from the Y of phase 2, and the matrix of the projections as they go alone (consecutive tensors of one format as one segment), when it has the segments of one phase; the projection that feeds the `CONCAT` is written straight into it. Every file of Qwen3.5 4B fits: in Q4_K_M a layer of linear attention is 8192 outputs in Q5_K and 4096 + 32 + 32 in Q4_K, a layer of attention 9216 in Q4_K and 1024 in Q6_K (or 10240 in Q4_K), two segments at most; in Q6_K and Q8_0, one. `GGML_XDNA_NO_CHAIN_NEXT` leaves the next layer out. The kernel and the overlay did not change: a phase with the prologue of the norm and no epilogue was already there.
 - Checked first with `llama-xdna-gemv --ffn --next-layer` (the projections of a layer of linear attention of Q4_K_M after the block, against a reference computed from the output the NPU gave): within 1.7 x 10^-7 of the largest value. The fourth phase adds 0.356 ms to the 0.777 ms of the block (its 23 MB of weights at about 70 GB/s); the same projections alone take 0.395 ms. Its stream (`llama-xdna-insts chain --ffn --next-layer`) has 896 operations, 10 descriptors per shim at most.
 - Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M generating (`llama-bench -p 0 -n 64 -r 3`, alternating): 18.61 and 18.65 tokens per second against 18.29 and 18.33 without the fourth phase (+1.8 %, about 0.9 ms a token, what the NPU saves: 0.034 ms per command, 31 commands a token). 36 commands per token instead of about 67: the projections of layer 0, 31 commands of a block and the next layer, and the last layer and the logits in 5. The same text as without it; with batches of one token, KL 0.0420 (maximum 1.89, median 0.0271), the same token 88.7 % of the times, PPL 15.62. The files in Q5_K_M, Q6_K and Q8_0 also take it in every layer.
 - **Descriptors before the wait** (2026/11/15, point 1 of the improvements after step E). A change of phase cost about 0.02 ms: after the wait for the drains of the phase before, the firmware wrote the buffer descriptors of the next phase one by one (some 64, a block write and a patch of its address each, about 0.3 µs) and only then queued them, while the cores waited. Two consecutive phases of a feed-forward block never need more than the 16 descriptors of a shim tile (per tile, 8 and 8, 8 and 6, 6 and 10 with the projections of the next layer, 6 and 8 with the logits), so `fXdnaChainPlan` writes the descriptors of a phase that waits for a Y before the wait, while the array works on the phase before, and after the wait only pushes the queues; when they do not fit, as before. The kernel and the overlay did not change. `GGML_XDNA_NO_EARLY_DESCRIPTORS`, and `--no-early` of `llama-xdna-gemv` and `llama-xdna-insts chain`, write them after the wait again, which gives the streams of before byte for byte (checked). The feed-forward block with the next layer (`llama-xdna-gemv --ffn --next-layer --repeat 50`, alternating): the same errors, 1.087 and 1.088 ms against 1.131 (about 14 µs less per change of phase).
 - **The last layer and the logits** (point 2). llama.cpp picks the rows it outputs in the last layer with a `GET_ROWS` of the output of the attention and another of the residual, between `attn_output` and the residual `ADD`; with one token they are all its rows. The backend now takes a `GET_ROWS` of one id from the only row of the result of an operation (`fRowsTaken`: not of a cache, as the `GET_ROWS` of one row of the recurrent states, which stays with the processor), and the residual `ADD` through them, so that the scheduler gives it the whole layer and the logits in one part. `fFeedForward` reads those rows where they are and takes the output norm and the logits as the fourth phase (248,320 outputs in Q6_K with Q4_K_M, one segment). The output of that norm (`result_norm`) is an output of the graph (llama.cpp always marks it: the embeddings and the hidden state of multi-token prediction), so the host writes it from the output of the layer after the command, as the processor computes it; the logits cannot fall where that output is, or the command goes with three phases as before. A `GET_ROWS` that does not go with a command is copied on the host (`fRowsOnHost`). 33 commands per token instead of 36; the stream of the command has 848 operations, 16 descriptors per shim at most (`llama-xdna-insts chain --ffn --logits`).
 - **The `ADD` of a bias** (point 3). In a layer of linear attention the `ADD` of `alpha` and the bias `ssm_dt` (32 values) went to the backend, which made two more parts of the scheduler per layer: llama.cpp places that bias as a matrix (`LLM_TENSOR_SSM_DT` is a matrix in Mamba), so it lives in a buffer of the backend, and `fAddTaken` took an `ADD` of a product. `fAddTaken` no longer takes an `ADD` whose other operand is a weight of the model (`fIsWeight`). With points 2 and 3, 66 parts of the scheduler per token instead of 116.
 - Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M generating (`llama-bench -p 0 -n 64 -r 3`, alternating): 19.17 and 19.19 tokens per second against 18.59 and 18.65 with the binaries of step E (+3.0 %); points 2 and 3 alone (with `GGML_XDNA_NO_EARLY_DESCRIPTORS`), 18.68 and 18.69. The same text; with batches of one token, KL 0.0420 (step E, 0.0420), the same token 88.7 % of the times as before, PPL 15.65; NPU 41.1 ms per token (step E, 42.5), the backend 43.4 ms (44.8). The prompt of 512 does not change (926 tokens per second). With Q6_K one text turns at a near tie: the logits now come from the norm of the cores, which apply its scale to the sums instead of rounding an X already scaled; the KL is the same (0.00649 with both, maximum 0.208).
 - **Every file** (point 4), one after the other in the same session (`llama-bench -p 0 -n 64 -r 3`, tokens per second):

| File | Chained overlay | Overlay for generating tokens | Processor alone |
|---|---|---|---|
| Q8_0 | 12.84 | 11.96 | 8.82 |
| Q6_K | 15.42 | 14.20 | 11.68 |
| Q5_K_M | 16.54 | 15.16 | 13.14 |
| Q4_K_M | 19.18 | 17.04 | 14.16 |

The chained overlay goes 7 to 13 % faster than the other one, and 1.26 to 1.46 times the processor alone (slower in this session than on other days: 15.7 with Q4_K_M then). Q5_K_M, Q6_K and Q8_0 generate the same text as in step E, but for that turn of Q6_K, with every layer in one command and the last one with the logits.

**A second trap of the cache** (measured on 2026/11/15). After a run of stores of the host, the processor leaves the next cache line modified with what it held, though nothing wrote it (likely its prefetching for stores: on AMD processors `PREFETCHW` leaves a line modified). In the buffer of the host the Y of a command can lie where another command wrote its input, so the flush before reading Y wrote that line back over the results of the NPU. It took a command of K = 9216, whose input ends at byte 46080, before the logits, whose Y covers that byte: in every token but the first, outputs 544 to 551 of the logits came back as what the command before had left there, which was only seen when the model wanted to write `##` (a KL of 8.5 in one token). The host now flushes what it writes up to the end of its page and one page more (prefetching does not cross pages). Found with `llama-xdna-gemv --x-from`, `--blocks-from` (the rows of a model and the real weights) and `--reverse` (the order of commands of the model), and `_/xdna2-lab/kl_tokens.py` (the KL token by token between two files of `--kl-divergence-base`). The first trap is in [Buffers](#buffers).

What was left after step E (Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M, batches of one token, 2,048 tokens): 42.5 ms of NPU per token, of which about 38 were the weights at 70 GB/s; the rest, the fixed costs of 36 commands (about 2 ms) and some 96 changes of phase (about 0.02 ms each: the wait for the drains, then the descriptors of the next phase, written one by one). That led to the descriptors before the wait, the last layer and the bias above.

What is left of a token of Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M generating after them (52.1 ms with `llama-bench`): the NPU, about 45 ms (41.1 on average with batches of one token, where half the tokens have no logits; the logits are about 8 ms), of which about 38 are the weights at 70 GB/s, and the rest the fixed costs of 33 commands (about 2 ms) and 96 changes of phase (some 6 µs each now); the host of the backend, about 2 ms, more than half of it for the logits: their Y has room for 8 rows (7.9 MB, of which one row is read) and all of it leaves the cache before it is read (about 0.9 ms per token); and the operations of the processor (the recurrence, the attention, the copies of the recurrent states) and the scheduler, about 5 ms. Next: a Y of one row laid out as the host reads it (fewer bytes from the NPU and less to flush), fewer bytes of weights (the padding of Q5_K and Q6_K, about 1.3 ms with Q4_K_M), and the operations of the processor on the NPU (towards everything on the NPU). The next round (below) did the reads of the host without touching the kernel, the padding with version 6, and more rows of X.

**The reads of the host** (2026/11/15, point 1 of the round confirmed after the improvements above; the kernel and the overlay did not change). Around a command the host flushed the cache with the `SYNC_BO` of the driver: before it what it wrote, after it the whole Y of the phases it reads. The driver does `drm_clflush_virt_range` on the range (`clflushopt` on every line between two full barriers; read in `amdxdna_gem.c` of Linux, which flushes every page of the buffer instead when it has no mapping in the kernel), so `fXdnaFlushCache` (device layer) does the same from the process, without a call to the driver, and only on the lines it has to: `mRunChain` flushes what the host wrote (up to the end of its page and one more, as before) and, after the command, only the lines `fXdnaChainReadY` will read (`fXdnaChainReads`: with one row, the first 32 bytes of every group of 256, a line in four; with the logits of Qwen3.5 4B, 2 of their 7.9 MB). The other lines may stay in the cache as they were: nothing reads them, and nothing wrote them, as what the host writes leaves the cache before every command. And `fXdnaChainReadY` asks for the group 32 groups on while it copies one (`__builtin_prefetch`): every group is a trip to memory, which the processor does not fetch ahead by itself (64 groups gave the same). `GGML_XDNA_NO_USER_FLUSH`, and `--sync-bo` of `llama-xdna-gemv`, go back to the driver. **Measured**: the results of a run of 24 tokens of Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M were read in 18.5 ms instead of 41.3 (flushing, 4.7 instead of 13.5; the reads, 14 instead of 27.8); the logits alone (`llama-xdna-gemv -M 1 -K 2560 -N 248320`), in 0.28 ms instead of 0.53; generating, 19.47 and 19.45 tokens per second against 19.18 and 19.17 (+1.5 %, alternating); the same text and, with batches of one token, the same KL to the last digit (0.042044).

**Version 6 of the chained overlay** (points 2 and 4; manifest `xdna2-gemv 6`, the only one the host takes; built with `_/xdna2-lab/compilar_chain.sh <folder> --test ffn`, which writes the manifest too). **Measured** on the laptop:

 - **No padding between blocks.** With version 5 every block of a core started an object of its own, so the last object of a block had padding: some 9 % of the bytes of Q6_K and 14 % of those of Q5_K with K = 2560 (2 to 3 % with K = 4096 and 9216; none with Q4_K and bfp16, whose pieces fill an object). Now the pieces of the blocks of a segment follow each other in the objects of a core, and only the last object of a segment has padding. The kernel stops where a block ends inside an object and says so in the parameters (row 4, item 1); the program of the core loops over the objects of a segment and, in a loop of 0 or 1 turns over that item, finishes the block (core 0: its sums and those of core 1 into an object of Y) or sends it (core 1), then calls the kernel again on the same object. mlir-aie lowers the acquisitions of an object fifo in a loop whose count is known only at run time with the two buffers swapped at run time (read in the lowered MLIR of the overlay), so a loop of 0 or 1 turns needs nothing else. Core 0 sums a block in a buffer of its own (1 KB) instead of the object of Y. The stream of the shims is the same as with version 5 (the one of mlir-aie for the feed-forward test, byte for byte): only the lengths of the weights change.
 - **X in 7 objects of a core** (32256 bytes; before 3 objects, 13824 bytes, as X in bfp16 comes in whole halves of objects): 8 rows of K = 4096 fit (5 before) and 6 of K = 9216 (2 before). To make room, the buffer of the epilogues went from 2048 to 1664 floats: the residual and h take 320 outputs per stream (5120 in all; 8192 before), the gate 1024 (16384). The data memory of core 0 is full up to byte 65,024 of 65,536, and the two buffers of W now share a bank (they were in banks 0 and 2).
 - **Program memory.** Core 0 used 16,080 of its 16,384 bytes with version 5, and the first build of version 6 did not fit by 160 bytes; a single place calling the function of a piece made the compiler put it inline, 500 bytes more (`-Os` still crashes Peano on the epilogue, and `-O1` saves nothing). With the function of a piece out of line and the copy of X into the stage unrolled for 1, 2, 4 and 8 rows instead of every number of rows (with 3, 5, 6 or 7 rows the copy for the next of those takes a few words more of every group, which go to rows the phase does not have), 14,560 bytes.
 - **Rows that do not fit in one command.** 7 and 8 rows of K = 9216 need 41472 bytes of X. The backend sends them in a few commands with the rows shared out evenly (`fRowsPerCommand`: two of 4), each reading all the weights, which takes less than the processor with the 8 rows.
 - With `llama-xdna-gemv` (1 x 2560 x 248320, 10 rounds, against version 5 with the binaries of before): Q5_K in 6.340 ms instead of 7.090 (-10.6 %); Q6_K in 7.810 instead of 7.992 (-2.3 %, with 8 % fewer bytes: the cores decode Q6_K at some 67 GB/s of useful bytes, so its padding cost only the time to transfer it); Q4_K the same (5.318 and 5.295 ms). Small commands lose a little: 1 x 2560 x 2560 Q6_K takes 0.170 ms instead of 0.155. Every result is identical to version 5. Every format with 1 to 8 rows in the sizes of Qwen3.5 4B agrees with the reference (bfp16 exactly), and so does the feed-forward block with the next layer (the same errors, 1.097 ms); the streams of the last layer with the logits (848 operations) and of the next layer (896) use 16 descriptors per shim at most, as before.
 - Qwen3.5 4B, both changes of this round against the binaries and the overlay of before (`llama-bench -p 0 -n 64 -r 3`, alternating, two rounds; the processor alone in the same session):

| File | Chained overlay, version 6 | Version 5, before | Processor alone |
|---|---|---|---|
| Q8_0 | 12.96 | 12.81 | 9.12 |
| Q6_K | 16.41, 16.38 | 15.48, 15.38 | 11.88 |
| Q5_K_M | 17.78, 17.57 | 16.60, 16.47 | 12.50 |
| Q4_K_M | 19.69, 19.48 | 19.18, 18.98 | 14.72 |

 - Prompts of a few tokens with Q4_K_M (`llama-bench -n 0 -r 5`, tokens per second, version 6 against version 5): 2 tokens, 29.5 against 29.0 (within the noise); 4, 60.6 against 48.3; 6, 89.7 against 63.3; 8, 101.0 against 73.2 (75.3 without the split, with the 8 rows of `ffn_down` on the processor). The prompt of 512 does not change (858.2 against 858.7 in this session, 926 earlier in the day). The same text; with batches of one token, the same KL to the last digit (0.042044); with batches of 8, KL 0.0416 against 0.0432 (more multiplications on the NPU, whose sums are 32-bit floats; 8064 commands were the second of a split).
 - The default folder of overlays of the laptop (`_/xdna2/overlays/`) has the chained overlay of version 6 next to the other two (point 3); `overlays-chain/` keeps version 5, which only the binaries of before (`work/bin-f123/`) take.

What is left of a token of Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M generating (50.95 ms with `llama-completion`, 23 tokens): about 47 ms of NPU (33 commands, 96 changes of phase, and the weights at some 67 GB/s), under 1 ms of reads of the host, and some 3 ms of the operations of the processor and the scheduler. (This paragraph first said that the decoders of the cores bound Q4_K and Q6_K: the measurement below shows they do not.)

**The decoders are not the limit** (2026/11/15, point 1 of the next round). With the variant of the overlay whose kernel only counts the pieces (`compilar_chain.sh <folder> --test ffn --compute 0`, built with `-DXDNA2_CHAIN_NO_COMPUTE`), the logits of 1 x 2560 x 248320 take as long without computing as computing with Q4_K (5.365 against 5.318 ms) and Q5_K (6.337 against 6.340), and 2 % less with Q6_K (7.65 against 7.81). What bounds the overlay is the transport of the weights, 67 to 69 GB/s in large commands. In the machine code a piece of Q4_K costs about 700 cycles (some 10 per group of 64 weights and some 270 to prepare its factors) and one of Q6_K about 620, against the 980 and 1400 cycles their bytes take to arrive. On that ceiling: the NPU reads the buffers of the host by the virtual addresses of the process (the driver binds the device to the process with `iommu_sva_bind_device`; the address of a `SHARE` buffer for the NPU is that of its mapping in the process), translated with the page tables of the process, and the `shmem` of those buffers is in pages of 4 KB. The driver of Linux 7.3.4 creates buffers from memory of the process (`amdxdna_drm_va_tbl`), whose anonymous memory would come in pages of 2 MB, but such a buffer also needs a mapping through the driver to get an address for the NPU, again of 4 KB pages; giving the NPU other addresses risks a fault of translation, a hung NPU. Not tried: the integrated GPU reads the weights at some 66 GB/s on average with Vulkan, so the memory of the laptop does not give much more.

**The banks of the core** (point 2): moving buffer 1 of W to bank 1, with X after it into bank 3 (`_/xdna2-lab/chain_banks.py`, and `bancos_chain.sh` to rebuild the overlay from the MLIR with addresses), changes nothing measurable: with Q6_K, alternating with 30 rounds, 7.432 against 7.391 ms. The layout of mlir-aie stays.

**The transitions** (point 3). After the wait of a phase, the C++ queued first the 16 drains of its Y (a write of the register of the controller of the tokens and a push each, the same as mlir-aie writes before every push with a token) and only then the fills, while the cores waited for data; at the start of a command the drains went with their whole descriptor (about 0.4 µs each). The Y of a phase is needed only when a block ends, several microseconds later, so `fXdnaChainPlan` now queues the fills first (`pFillsFirst`; `GGML_XDNA_NO_FILLS_FIRST`, and `--drains-first` of `llama-xdna-gemv` and `llama-xdna-insts chain`, keep the order of before, which with `--no-early` gives the stream of before byte for byte). **Measured**: the feed-forward block with the next layer, 1.070 ms against 1.087 and 1.090 (alternating, 100 rounds); Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M generating, 20.38 and 20.30 tokens per second against 20.05 and 20.07 (+1.4 %); the same text, the same KL with batches of one token (0.042044). Writing the register of the controller before the wait gives nothing more once the fills go first.

**The operations of the processor when a token is generated** (point 4, measured with the temporary timer of `ggml-cpu.c`, `XDNA2_PROF_OPS=1`, Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M, 128 tokens on the NPU): 3.85 ms per token. `GET_ROWS` 1.41 ms and `CPY` 0.96 ms, mostly the copies of the recurrent state of the 24 layers of linear attention (2 MB a layer); `GATED_DELTA_NET` 0.72; `SSM_CONV` 0.19; `FLASH_ATTN_EXT` 0.15; `RMS_NORM` 0.11; `SCALE` 0.07; `SIGMOID` 0.07; the rest, some 0.2. Nearly two thirds of the work of the processor were copies of the state, not computation.

**The recurrent states in place** (point 4, first step; **measured**). Per layer of linear attention the state of 2 MB was copied three times: `build_rs` gathered it from the cache into a temporary (`GET_ROWS`), `ggml_gated_delta_net` copied it into its output before updating it row by row, and the model wrote it back into the cache (`ggml_cpy`). When every sequence of the ubatch goes on in its own cell (`llm_graph_rs_in_place`: no cell to clear and `s_copy(i) == head + i`, the usual case when generating and in the ubatches of a prompt after the first), `src/models/qwen35.cpp` gives the recurrence a view of its cell, and `build_recurrent_attn` asks the operator to write its final state there (`ggml_gated_delta_net_set_state_in_place`, op param 1, a function new to `ggml.h`), with no copy back. Only with the fused operator, without rollback states, with the cache in F32 and in host memory; `llm_graph_input_rs::in_place` takes part in the reuse of graphs, and an RPC server is not given the operator in place (`rpc_node_ok`). Every row of a head evolves alone, so updating it where it is gives the same values: the logits of the processor alone are identical byte for byte (Qwen3.5 0.8B BF16, batches of 128), and so are a text of 128 tokens on the NPU and the KL with batches of one token (0.042044). In 128 tokens, `GET_ROWS` went from 181.8 to 13.5 ms, `CPY` from 123.6 to 61.7 and the recurrence from 93.4 to 126.6 (it now reads the state from memory, what the copy did before): the processor from 497 to 311 ms, some 1.4 ms less per token. Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M generating (`llama-bench`, alternating): 20.78 and 20.77 tokens per second against 19.88 and 19.96 (+4.3 %); the processor alone, 63.9 ms per token against 69.3 (+8 %). It is also what the recurrence on the NPU needs: to read and write the cell of the cache with no copy.

What the processor still does per token after it (2.4 ms): the recurrence 0.98 ms (bound by the memory: 2 MB read and written per layer), the copies of the states of the convolution 0.45 (`CPY` of a transposed view, 3 x 8192 values per layer), the convolution 0.21, the attention 0.18, and some 0.6 of small operations (norms, the sigmoid of beta, the softplus of alpha, RoPE, the gates, and nodes of zero size that `build_rs` adds).

**The states of the convolution and the empty nodes** (2026/11/16, point B of the next round; **measured**). Three changes, with the logits of the processor alone identical byte for byte (Qwen3.5 0.8B BF16, batches of 128) and the same text of 128 tokens on the NPU: when every sequence goes on in its own cell, `build_conv_states` reads the states of the convolution where the cache keeps them (a view, no `GET_ROWS`); `build_rs` leaves out the clearing of a state and the copy of the extra states when they would be empty (an empty node still costs a dispatch and a barrier of the threads, and there was one of each per layer and state), and with both states in place no node reads `s_copy`, which then has no memory, so its `set_input` skips it; and the transposed copy of the new states of the convolution into the cache (3 tokens by 8192 channels per layer) goes row by row of its source: it went through `ggml_compute_forward_dup_bytes`, which with one type copies element by element with a `memcpy` of 4 bytes (about 19 µs per layer, now 6). In 128 tokens, `CPY` from 61.7 to 18.9 ms, `GET_ROWS` from 13.5 to 3.4 and `SCALE` from 9.0 to 7.4; the processor, some 2.0 ms per token. Generating with Q4_K_M, 20.62 and 20.61 tokens per second against 20.44 and 20.47 (+0.8 %), the same KL with batches of one token.

**A row of Y per object** (version 7 of the chained overlay, point C; **measured**). An object of Y was the whole block of 32 outputs with room for 8 rows (1 KB, [4 groups][8 rows][8 outputs]), so with one row 7 of every 8 floats the NPU sent to memory were nothing. Now an object of Y is one row of a block (32 outputs, 128 bytes): core 0 finishes the block in its buffer of sums (`xdna2_chain_finish`) and sends as many rows as the phase has (`xdna2_chain_emit`, in a loop of `rows` turns). The Y of a segment is [block][row][32 outputs], with the blocks of all the streams one after the other: with one row, the outputs in their order, so the X of a phase that comes from a Y is a read in line (its descriptors are those of mlir-aie word for word), and the host flushes and copies one piece (`fXdnaChainReads`, `fXdnaChainReadY`). Program memory of core 0, 15,040 of 16,384 bytes (480 more); its two objects of Y take 256 bytes instead of 2 KB. With `llama-xdna-gemv`: the logits of 1 x 2560 x 248320 Q6_K in 7.249 ms instead of 7.430 (reading Y, 0.082 instead of 0.271 ms); the feed-forward block with the next layer, 1.042 ms instead of 1.070; every format with 1 to 8 rows with the same values as before. Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M generating: the same text of 128 tokens, 21.32 and 21.27 tokens per second against 20.65 and 20.66 with version 6 (+3.1 %, more than the 0.6 % expected from the logits: the writes of Y went down in every phase of every command). Version 7 is the default overlay; `overlays-chain6/` keeps version 6 for the binaries of before.

**Towards a layer of linear attention on the NPU** (a design, not built yet; point A of the round of 2026/11/16, left for the next session). What it would save is not so much the 2.0 ms the processor still takes per token after point B as the round trips: with the whole layer on the NPU, a token would need a few commands instead of 33 (each costs some 0.1 ms, and the host and the processor wait for each other between them). What it needs, as read in the code and measured above:

 - **The recurrent states in memory the NPU reads** (done, point A1 below). The buffer type of the backend is memory of the process (`ggml_aligned_malloc`), and the recurrent cache lived in buffers of the processor; the NPU reads only the buffers of the driver (it translates the addresses of the process, and the driver pins and tracks only its buffers).
 - **A mapping that fits the array.** Qwen3.5 4B has 32 heads of values (state of 128 x 128 floats, 64 KB each) and 16 heads of keys, each shared by two heads of values: one head of values per core, and the two cores of a pair share a head of keys, as they share a channel. Per token and layer every core reads its 64 KB of state and writes them back (row by row: every row evolves alone, the same property as above), and needs its v, k, q and z (128 values each), its alpha and beta, its 3 x 384 values of the state of the convolution and the 4 x 384 weights of the convolution. The state through the channels of the shims is 2 MB in and 2 MB out per layer, some 1.4 ms per token at 67 GB/s, about what the processor takes now for the recurrence and less than before.
 - **Room in the cores.** Program memory is the tight resource (with version 7, core 0 uses 15,040 of its 16,384 bytes, core 1 12,416), and so is data memory (with version 7, core 0 has some 3.4 KB free in three gaps: 2,624 bytes in bank 0, after the parameters, and 384 bytes each after the first object of Y and at the top). The convolution, the norms of q and k, the recurrence and the gated norm would be new functions; the state of a head does not fit in a core, so it goes row by row through it, and core 1 would need its own way out for the state it writes (today only core 0 sends Y).
 - **The backend**: recognising the operations between the projections and `ssm_out` of a layer (`SSM_CONV`, `SILU`, the norms, `GATED_DELTA_NET`, the gated norm), as `fFeedForward` recognises the feed-forward block, and running them as phases of the chained command.

**The recurrent states in buffers of the driver** (2026/11/16, point A1; **measured**). The backend offers a second buffer type, `XDNA_State` (`fStateBufferType`): `SHARE` buffers of the driver (`XdnaRunner::mCreateShared`, flushed from the cache when they are created, as every buffer the NPU may write), host memory like the other buffers of the backend, so the processor works on them as before, with its tensors a page apart (`cStateAlignment`). llama.cpp asks for it through a new function of the registry, `ggml_backend_dev_get_state_buft` (its type in `ggml-backend.h`): `llama-memory-recurrent.cpp` (`llama_memory_recurrent_accel_buft`) gives the states of every layer that runs on the processor the buffer type an accelerator offers for them. The layer is recognised by its device (`model.dev_layer`): the buffer type of the processor has no device in ggml (`ggml_backend_cpu_buffer_type`, `device = NULL`), and the first try, which looked at the device of the buffer type, did nothing. The NPU stays open while such a buffer lives (`fCloseIfIdle` counts `dStateBuffers`, and `fOpenRunner` opens it when a buffer is asked for first). `GGML_XDNA_NO_STATE_BUFFERS` keeps the states in buffers of the processor. Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M: `XDNA_State RS buffer size = 50.25 MiB`, the same text of 128 tokens, the same KL with batches of one token (0.042044, the same token in 88.725 %), and the same speed (three rounds alternating: 21.25, 21.28 and 21.21 tokens per second against 21.26, 21.31 and 21.21 with the states in buffers of the processor; the pages of 4 KB of the buffers of the driver cost the recurrence of the processor nothing measurable). What it gives is the place: a phase of the NPU can now read and write the cell of a state where the cache keeps it, with the buffer of the driver that holds it among the buffers of its command.

**A phase for the recurrence** (version 8 of the chained overlay, 2026/11/16, point A2; **measured**). A phase can now run the recurrence of a layer of linear attention (the gated delta net of ggml with heads of 128 and one gate per head) instead of a matrix: one head of values per core, head 32 h + 2 s + c on core c of stream s (`XdnaChainRecurrence`). The states live in a buffer of the driver among the buffers of the command (after its matrices; 5 buffers at most), head j at `vStateOffset` + j * 64 KB, as llama.cpp keeps a cell of its recurrent cache. They come through the channels of the weights, 9 rows to the half of an object of a core and 15 objects a head (the last one with 2 rows; the other 7 are read past the head, so the buffer needs 3.5 KB of room after the last head), and go back through Y where they were, row j of both heads of a pair one after the other (core 1 hands its rows to core 0 over the cascade); the outputs of the phase (row . q for every row, without the 1 / sqrt(128) of ggml) follow in its Y, [head][128]. The inputs of a head (q, k, v, the decay e^g of its gate, which the host computes, and beta) come in an object of the prologue, copied as it is by the prologue of X in bfp16. The descriptors of the C++ (states in: {128 words, 9 rows, 2 heads} x 15 iterations 1152 words apart; out: {128, 2 heads, 128 rows}) are those of mlir-aie word for word (`gemv_chain.py --test gdn`, `llama-xdna-insts chain --gdn`). With `llama-xdna-gemv --gdn`, 32 heads for 64 tokens one after the other: outputs and states within 1.2e-7 of the largest value of a reference in 64-bit floats, with no error piling up. But 0.283 ms a command: the phase is bound by its arithmetic, as a product of 32-bit floats is emulated with 9 of bf16 and the splitting of both operands into three parts (some 25 bundles of instructions each, four per value of a row); the 24 layers would take some 6.8 ms a token, where the processor takes about 1 ms. Next: the products emulated by hand, with k, q and the decay split into bf16 by the host (6 products, and no splitting of the constant operands), and the outputs with less precision (a norm and a product in bfp16 follow them), so that the phase goes at the pace of memory (some 4 MB a layer, about 0.06 ms).

**Where a prompt of 512 goes now** (2026/11/16, Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M, `llama-bench -p 512 -v`, the report of the backend divided by the batches): some 500 ms a batch at about 1,000 tokens per second; the backend about 291 ms (the NPU 110, converting rows 60, reading results 121, of which some 14 flushing the cache) and the processor about 210 (the recurrence by chunks of 64 tokens, the attention, the norms). Reading the results is bound by memory: the C of the batch in bf16 is read and some 2.4 GB of floats are written, with the SwiGLU (its exponential already in AVX-512, as ggml does it), the residual `ADD` and the `CONCAT` fused into that reading. What was looked at:
 - The rows of an RMS norm by its weights were normalised one value at a time with scalar products (the library is built for the baseline of x86-64) before the encoder took them: they now go through the registers of the AVX2 encoder (`fXdnaBfp16EncodeNearestNormed`), the same products in the same order, so the logits are the same byte for byte; converting rows from 393 to 378 ms over six batches (some 2.5 ms a batch, 0.3 %).
 - The encoder in AVX-512: this processor runs AVX-512 on paths of 256 bits, so it would save instructions, not time; not done.
 - `ffn_down`, in three pieces along K: the second and the third read the result in floats back and write it again; adding the three C in one pass after the last one (in the same order, the same values) moves some 16 bytes less per value, but the reading of every piece now overlaps the multiplication of the next one, so it would save some 6 ms a batch (about 1 %); not done.
 - What would change the picture is structural: the NPU writing C as floats straight into the tensors of the processor (buffers of the driver, a pattern per tile into rows), which would take most of the reading off the host but make the multiplications slower (C in floats is twice the bytes, and the data path bounds them); or the recurrence by chunks on the NPU, which on the processor is bound by its arithmetic (about 26 G multiply-adds a batch) and on the NPU would have to be written as products of matrices (the delta rule by chunks), not row by row.

**What bounds the multiplications of a prompt** (2026/11/16, measured with `llama-xdna-gemm`, 20 rounds, median). With the shapes of the prompt of 512 of Qwen3.5 4B, the variant of the overlay of the prompts without the multiplication (`work/bfpres-4096-b8-bf16-c0`) takes as long as the default one: 512 x 2560 x 9216 in 0.820 ms against about 0.81, 512 x 2560 x 12352 in 1.093 against about 1.10, 512 x 4096 x 2560 in 0.429 against about 0.46, 512 x 1024 x 2560 in 0.240 against 0.24. The large shapes already reach 24 to 30 TOPS, and the data path sets their time, not the kernel: a faster kernel would give the model nothing. `ffn_down` (K = 9216) goes in three pieces (4096, 4096 and 1024, the last at 11 TOPS) because a band of 64 rows of K = 9216 takes 648 KB, more than a memory tile; in the columns that also hold the queue of B, a band of K = 4096 (295 KB) with that queue (8 tiles, 72 KB) and C (64 KB) nearly fills the tile already, and two pieces would need a shorter queue of B, which cost 3.5 % with 4096 x 4096 x 4096 in every multiplication.

**The recurrence made faster, and what bounds it** (2026/11/16, `work/chain8b-gdn`, the default overlay since then; **measured**). Every row is split once into three parts of bf16 (`fSplit3`: a conversion, and the part taken out of the accumulator as a product by 1.0, exactly), and k, q and e^g once a head by its first row; a product of two split values is the 6 products of bf16 of `fMac3` (those whose weight is 2^-16 of the product or more). The host gives beta v and beta e^g, so that delta = beta v[j] - beta e^g (row . k) with the row before its decay, one product of 32-bit floats a row (emulated by the compiler); the output takes two parts of the new row by two of q (some 16 bits; a norm and a product in bfp16 follow it). From 0.283 to 0.185 ms a command, with the outputs within 5e-6 and the states within 2e-7 of the reference after 64 tokens, and the same text of Qwen3.5 4B. Three accumulators so that the chains of products overlap were slower (0.201 ms): the core has too few registers of accumulators, and they went to the stack. The variant without the arithmetic (`--compute 0`, which now leaves it out of the recurrence too) takes 0.130 ms a command: without the fixed cost of a command (some 0.05 ms), moving the 4.3 MB of the states of a layer in and out takes the NPU some 0.08 ms, about 54 GB/s. **The recurrence is bound by memory, and there the processor is faster**: it runs the recurrence of the 24 layers in about 1 ms, reading and writing the states at some 98 GB/s, while the NPU would take 1.9 ms on the data alone (3.2 ms as phases today). What the NPU can still give is a processor left free, and, with the convolution and the norms on it too, consecutive layers of linear attention in one command (from 33 commands a token to about 9), with little to gain in speed.

**The rest of a layer of linear attention on the NPU, designed and paused** (2026/11/16, points A3 and A4; built later that day, see the next paragraph). The design that fits the cores: the pair of stream s takes heads s and s + 16, which share their head of keys (head j of the values takes the keys of head j % 16), so that the 512 channels of the convolution of a pair (q and k of head s, v of heads s and s + 16) lie 2048 channels apart in the cache of the states of the convolution ([channel][3 tokens]) and in the weights of the convolution ([channel][4]), one pattern of the shims each; both cores compute the convolution, the SiLU and the L2 norms of q and k for their own head (the duplicated q and k cost nothing), core 0 writes the new states of the convolution back through Y (and those of v of head s + 16 come from core 1 over the cascade), the decay and beta of every head come from the host (it computes them from alpha and beta of the projections, which end the command before), the recurrence takes its inputs where the convolution left them, and the gated norm of every head (RMS norm by the weights of the norm by silu(z), its eps times 128 for the scale of ggml that the recurrence leaves out) goes at the end of the last row of the head. The numbers that paused it: as a phase, the recurrence costs some 0.13 ms a layer (0.185 ms a command without the fixed cost of a command) and the convolution some 0.02, plus some 0.02 per change of phase: some 4.6 ms a token for the 24 layers, against some 1.5 ms of the processor saved; with one command per layer, as today, generating tokens would go some 6 to 7 % slower, and with consecutive layers in one command (from 33 commands a token to about 9, which needs the decay computed on the NPU with the precision of 32-bit floats, as it compounds token after token, and the parameters given phase by phase), some 2 % slower. The processor sleeps while the NPU works already, so the energy would hardly change. The bound is that of the recurrence: 96 MB of states a token, which the processor reads and writes faster than the NPU.

**The whole layer of linear attention on the NPU, as built** (2026/11/16; version 9 of the chained overlay, manifest `xdna2-gemv 9`, the only one the host takes since; built with `bash _/xdna2-lab/compilar_chain.sh chain9-la2 --test la` into `work/chain9-la2`, kept in `_/xdna2/overlays-chain9/` and the default overlay in `overlays/`, where `overlays-rows/` keeps the one before; finished after the user said «Si, termina la 1 primero»). **`GGML_XDNA_LINEAR=1` turns it on; it is off by default, because it is slower.** What was built:

 - **A phase of a whole layer** (`XdnaChainRecurrence::vLinear`, item 26 of the parameters of a phase; the prologue in mode 3 copies 3 objects from X[1] on): stream s takes the head of keys s and the value heads s (core 0) and s + 16 (core 1), which share it. X[1] and X[2] of every stream come from the host every token (`fXdnaChainWriteLinear`): in the half of each core the scalars of its head (beta, e^g, beta e^g, E = 16384 times the eps of the gated norm, the eps of the L2 norms), and in that of core 1, in bf16, the 512 channels of the pair (q and k of head s, v of heads s and s + 16, 2048 channels apart) of x and of the 3 old states, taken from the CONCAT of the convolution by tokens as it is, and z of both heads. X[3] is the matrix of the phase: its fixed objects (`fXdnaChainPackLinear`: w' = ssm_norm × √128 and the weights of the convolution of the pair, in bf16), loaded once a layer (`XdnaRunner::mLoadChainLinear`, one object a stream).
 - **The cores**: core 1 computes the convolution of the 512 channels (products of bf16, sums in 32-bit floats), its SiLU, the L2 norms of q and k and the gates w' silu(z) of both heads (`xdna2_chain_conv`), and hands q, k, v of head s and its gate to core 0 over the cascade (`xdna2_chain_conv_recv`); both then run the recurrence of their head as in version 8 (`fGdnRow`), which at the last row of a head applies the gated norm (`fGatedNorm`: o × invsqrt(Σ o² + E) × gate; E and w' make up for the 1/√128 the outputs of the recurrence lack). Only products of bf16: those of 32-bit floats are emulated (some 25 bundles each), and the program of core 0 takes 16,288 of its 16,384 bytes, that of core 1 15,840 (the SiLUs and the gates had to go to core 1, in functions that are not inlined).
 - **Checked**: `llama-xdna-gemv --la` (32 heads, random weights, states and inputs, against a reference in 64-bit floats on the same inputs rounded to bf16): after 32 tokens the outputs within 3.5 % and the states within 1.1 % of their largest value, with no error piling up; 0.204 ms a command. The stream of the C++ (`llama-xdna-insts chain --la`) has the descriptors of mlir-aie word for word for the states, their way back and the outputs (the inputs go in three transfers instead of one). Version 9 changes nothing else: the feed-forward block with the next layer, 1.067 ms (1.061 with version 8) with the same error (1.7·10⁻⁷); in the model, the same text and the same KL with batches of one token (0.042063).
 - **In the backend** (`fLinearTaken`, `fMatchLinear`, `fLinearAttention`, `fLinearOnHost` in `ggml-xdna.cpp`): it takes the operations of the layer by their shapes and by what they read: the copy of the new states of the convolution into the cache, the `SSM_CONV`, the `SILU`s, the `RMS_NORM`s and `SCALE`s of the L2 norms, the `ADD` of `ssm_dt`, the `SOFTPLUS`, the `MUL`s by `ssm_a`, by `ssm_norm` and by silu(z), the `SIGMOID` and the recurrence of one token in place. The scheduler gives them to it from their weights (the backend takes any buffer of the host) and from the nodes next to them, and `graph_get_cb` (`src/llama-context.cpp`) gives it the recurrence. When the operations of a layer are whole in the part of the graph (`fMatchLinear`: none of their results has another use or is an output of the graph), the layer runs as one command when the graph gets to its last operation, with e^g and beta from the host as the CPU backend computes them; otherwise they go one by one on the host, as the copy always does. The recurrence has to be in place: the probe of llama.cpp for the fused recurrence (`resolve_fused_ops`) builds it on a cache with a cell to clear, so not in place, and would turn the fused recurrence off for good if it went to another device than its layer.
 - **The cache of the processor**: the NPU reads and writes the cell of the states directly, and the processor may have written it (a recurrence of a prompt on the processor, a copy of a cell, a state loaded from a file), so the host flushes the 2 MB of the cell before every command. Measured with `clflushopt`: 0.017 ms for 2 MB that are not in the cache, 0.030 ms clean in it, 0.040 ms dirty: cheaper and safer than keeping track of who wrote what.
 - **Measured** with Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M: the same text of 128 tokens, byte for byte; the KL with batches of one token 0.040959 ± 0.0023, the same token in 88.92 % (without it 0.042063 and 88.82 %); generating (`llama-bench -p 0 -n 64 -r 3`, two rounds alternating): **19.79 and 19.81 tokens per second against 21.66 and 21.64 without it, 8.6 % slower**, as estimated before it was built. Per layer and token the backend takes 0.29 ms (the NPU 0.226, writing X 0.04, the flushes 0.02) and the processor saves some 0.11 ms: some 7 ms more a token against 2.7 less. The bound is that of the recurrence: some 4.3 MB of states a layer in and out at some 54 GB/s, plus the fixed cost of a command. Even inside the command of the feed-forward block (which needs more phases and buffers in a command than version 9 has) it would be slower than the processor, which reads and writes the states at some 98 GB/s; what it gives is a processor free during the layers of linear attention.

**Room in core 0 for it.** The core has no scalar floating point: a product of scalar floats calls `__mulsf3`, a division `__divsf3` (1,168 bytes of program) and `__floatunsisf` (160), and an exponential of 32-bit floats took 1.1 KB. The mean of the squares of the RMS norm of a prologue is now the sum times 1 / K from the host (item 25 of a phase), in the lanes of a vector, which took the division out of both cores: the same text, the KL with batches of one token from 0.042044 to 0.042063 (the rounding of a product instead of a division), the same speed (21.47 and 21.36 tokens per second). Core 0 uses 16,064 of its 16,384 bytes with the recurrence, core 1 13,408.

**The weights compiled for size** (2026/11/16, `gemv_chain.py --gemv-os 1`, the default since; `work/chain8e-os`, the overlay in `overlays/`; the same with `-O2` in `overlays-chain8b-o2/`). The decoders of the weights (`fPiece`) built with `-Os` take 2,608 bytes instead of 3,984, so core 0 uses 14,816 bytes (1,568 free) and core 1 12,192 (4,192 free), and they are no slower, a little faster even (alternating: the feed-forward block with the next layer, 1.061 against 1.076 ms; the logits of 1 x 2560 x 248320 Q6_K, 7.165 against 7.267 ms; 8192 Q5_K + 4160 Q4_K, 0.465 against 0.472 ms; bfp16 the same; generating, 21.08 and 19.39 against 20.41 and 19.28 tokens per second, the same text): the decoders have room under the pace of the data, and `-O2` unrolled more than they need.

**The recurrence of a prompt on the NPU** (2026/11/16; option 2 of the round the user confirmed with «Si, haz las tres en el orden propuesto»; in use, see «In the model» below). The processor spends some 90 ms of a batch of 512 of Qwen3.5 4B in `GATED_DELTA_NET`, some 26 G multiply-adds, bound by its arithmetic. The delta rule by chunks (`build_delta_net_chunking`) turns it into products of small matrices. It has to live in the overlay of the prompts: two changes of overlay per layer of linear attention would cost some 120 ms a batch.

The precision, measured with `_/xdna2-lab/gdn_chunk_check.py` (synthetic heads like those of Qwen3.5: q and k of norm 1, gates from long to short memory). The table gives the largest error of the outputs and of the final state, as a part of the largest value, against the recurrence token by token in 64-bit floats, over 512 tokens:

| Variant | Outputs | State |
| --- | --- | --- |
| By chunks, the products in 32-bit floats | 4.5e-7 | 3.3e-7 |
| Token by token, the operands of the products in bf16 | 0.0047 | 0.0032 |
| The planned kernel, the products in bfp16 | 0.0172 | 0.0182 |
| The same, every product with both operands split into two parts of bfp16, v exact | 0.0029 | 0.0032 |
| The same, with the native products of bf16 | 0.0053 to 0.0058 | 0.0056 to 0.0058 |
| **As it will run:** q, k and v in bf16, every product split, Γ and the decays from `exp2` in bf16, the inverse by blocks | 0.0039 (chunks of 16), 0.0041 (32) | 0.0032, 0.0034 |

 - Token by token with bf16 operands is what the model already tolerates: the processor's recurrence with the operands of its products rounded to bf16 (a temporary switch in `ops.cpp`, removed after) gave a KL of 0.041496 against 0.041168 (± 0.0023), the same token in 89.1 % against 88.9 %, with Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M and 4 chunks of 512.
 - No single product carries the error of bfp16: making exact one group of them (k k^T, q k^T and the solve, or the products with the state) still leaves 0.014 to 0.017. Every product needs the split, and v and k need more than one part of bfp16 (8 bits shared by 8 values); with 2048 tokens and a random initial state the kernel as planned stays at 0.0048 (token by token in bf16, 0.0040 to 0.0047).
 - **The native products of bf16 of AIE2P do not help.** `mac_4x8_8x8_bf16` is 8 vector instructions (`vmac.f` with broadcasts), some 32 multiply-adds a cycle, while one product of bfp16 (`mac_8x8_8x8T`) is one `vmac.f` of 512 (`_/xdna2-lab/bf16_mac_probe.sh`, the schedule of the loops). That matches the 5.43 µs per 64^3 measured for bf16 earlier. A product split into two parts of bfp16 (hi·hi + hi·lo + lo·hi) is three instructions: more precise and some 5 times faster.
 - In the core, a split takes four instructions per 64 values: the values into a 32-bit float accumulator, `to_v64bfp16ebs8` for the high part, the high part taken out of the accumulator as a product by the identity, and `to_v64bfp16ebs8` again. AIE2P also has `exp2` of floats to bf16 (`aie::exp2<bfloat16>`), which gives Γ and the decays.

Room: the program of a core of the overlay of the prompts takes 6,352 of its 16,384 bytes. Its data memory is full: A 2 × 9,216 bytes, B 2 × 9,216, C 8,192, the sums 16,384 and the stack 3,840. So the recurrence uses as its own memory the buffers of A (held through two empty objects for the whole round), the half of the sums its state leaves, and its inputs where they land in the buffers of B.

The design:

 - **Formulation**, per head and chunk of C = 16 tokens, with the state as rows of value indices (S^T): cs = cumsum(g) in the chunk; Γ[t][t'] = e^(cs_t - cs_t') for t' <= t, E_t = e^cs_t, G_t = e^(cs_last - cs_t); A is the strictly lower part of β_t (k_t · k_t') Γ; T = (I + A)^-1; R = V - E ⊙ (K S); new = T D_β R; o = E ⊙ (Q S) + (Γ ⊙ Q K^T) new; and S <- e^cs_last S + K^T (G ⊙ new). It is `build_delta_net_chunking` with W S written as T D_β D_E (K S), so no W of C × 128 is needed.
 - **Mapping:** a round takes 4 key heads, one per row of cores; core (r, x) takes the two value heads of its key head (s and s + 16, which share k and q) and their value indices 8x to 8x + 7, a state of 8 KB. 4 rounds a layer.
 - **Data:** each chunk is two objects of B for the row. The first carries k and q in bf16, in tiles of 8 × 8, and the scalars of the chunk (β and cs·log2(e) of both heads, and e^cs_last); the second carries v^T of both heads in bf16. The states of a round come through A, one object per row in the band of the column. The outputs leave through C, 8 chunks an object, which the shim writes straight in [token][head][128]. The state goes back to its cell through C.
 - **The inverse** goes by blocks of 8: each diagonal block by doubling, (I - A)(I + A^2)(I + A^4), and the block under them as T10 = -T11 A10 T00, all on the matrix unit.
 - **Estimate:** some 1,000 products of 8 × 8 × 8 per chunk and core, plus the splits, so about 1 µs a chunk. 4 rounds of 32 chunks a layer make some 0.15 ms of arithmetic. B moves some 9.4 MB a layer, some 0.28 ms at 34 GB/s, so the data bound it: some 7 ms a batch, against some 90 ms of the processor.

**As built** (2026/11/16; kernel `ggml/src/ggml-xdna/kernels/xdna2-gdn-prompt.cc`, design `bfpmm_resident.py --gdn 1`, test `--test gdn` with `gdn_prompt_data.py`). What changed from the plan above, and why:

 - **Two heads of keys a round, not four.** With 8 value indices of 2 value heads per core, 4 rows by 8 columns cover 64 of the 128 value indices of 8 value heads, not all of them: the states of a round of 4 heads of keys are 512 KB, 16 KB per core, which leave no room for the work in the sums. So a round takes 2 heads of keys, each on two rows (row r: the head of keys r / 2 of the round, the half r % 2 of the value indices; the core of column x the block 8 (r % 2) + x), 8 rounds a layer. The two rows of a head of keys get the same stream of B, so B moves some 19 MB a layer instead of 9.4 (the host can write it once and let both shims read it).
 - **A chunk is two calls of the kernel**, one per object of B (k, q and Γ; v^T and the factors): mlir-aie gives a fifo the largest acquire of a core plus one buffers when all its ends state the same depth (`AIEObjectFifoSplit.cpp`, `objectCountOn`), and a third buffer of B does not fit. The two objects of work of A can be held at once because the ends of that fifo state different depths (1 and 2), which mlir-aie takes at their word.
 - **β out of the inverse, and the scalings as products by diagonal matrices.** With (I + M D_β) X = R and new = D_β X, every scaling by β, E or G is a product by a diagonal matrix on the matrix unit (column scalings X D, or D X^T with the operands swapped), and only Γ goes lane by lane. Γ, E, G and the decay come from the host (vector exponentials, some microseconds a layer), so the core needs no `exp2` and no patterns of rows and columns, which took kilobytes of program.
 - **Program memory.** The first version took 18.5 KB: a block of bfp16 passed by reference to a function that is not inlined is read byte by byte (320 `lda.s8` plus `vpush.hi.8` per block), so every function builds its own `-I` in registers and no function returns a pair; and arrays of accumulators go to the stack. As built it takes some 7.1 KB, and the cores 14,352 of 16,384 bytes.
 - **Loops the compiler overlaps.** The core has one stream register for writing: three block writers in one loop made the compiler keep it in the stack and empty it at every push (48 cycles for three groups; one writer per loop, 8 cycles a group). A branch inside a loop (the transposition, now a parameter of a template) and pointers the compiler cannot tell apart also stop the overlapping (`__restrict` on the reading and the writing pointer, as in the kernel of the multiplication). A loop with five accumulators alive is not overlapped either: the decay, lane by lane, took 30 to 37 cycles a group, and the decay on the matrix unit (the state in three parts of bfp16 by e^cs on the diagonal in three parts, six products) still takes 39 because its splits take three registers of sums.
 - **Measured** (one round of 4 value heads over 512 tokens, median of 20 commands): 0.281 ms (0.303 before the loops above); without the inverses, new and the outputs 0.222; without the decay and the update of the state 0.236; without the splits, the products along d and Γ 0.213; the data alone 0.166. Four rounds in one command, 0.84 ms. Results: outputs within 0.0014 and states within 0.00026 of the recurrence token by token in 64-bit floats on the same inputs in bf16 (largest error as a part of the largest value; mean square 0.00047 and 0.00021), and within 1.3e-4 of the model of the kernel in Python.
 - So a layer of 8 rounds takes some 1.9 ms on the NPU against some 3.75 ms of the processor, before the work of the host (writing the streams of B, the states, and the outputs back into the tensor).

**In the model** (2026/11/16; the host side in `xdna-gdnp.{h,cpp}` and `XdnaRunner::mRunGdn`, the backend in `fGdnTaken`, `fGdnTokensTaken` and `fGatedDeltaNet`). The overlay of the prompts with the recurrence (manifest line `gdn 1`, built in `work/bank-gdn`, kept in `_/xdna2/overlays-gdn/`) was the default one that day until the one with C as strips took its place (below); the one before the recurrence is kept in `overlays-before-gdn/`. `GGML_XDNA_NO_GDN` leaves the recurrence with the processor.

 - **The scheduler never gave it to the backend.** ggml's scheduler moves an operation to an accelerator only from its weights or from the nodes next to it, and `GATED_DELTA_NET` has no weights and always has nodes of the processor before and after it (the convolution, the norms, the copies to the cache). So `build_delta_net` (`src/models/delta-net-base.cpp`) passes the finished node through the callback of the graph (`gdn_fused`), and `llama_context::graph_get_cb` gives it to an accelerator that takes it on a layer of the processor, as the norm already was. The probe of `resolve_fused_ops` builds the operator with 16 tokens, which the backend refuses, so the probe stays on the processor and the fused operator stays enabled: if the backend ever took 16 tokens, the probe would see the operator on another device than its layer and turn the fused operator off for everything.
 - **Several commands a layer.** The rounds go in up to 4 commands (`GGML_XDNA_GDN_PARTS`, 4 by default), sent at once without waiting (the driver keeps 4 per context) and read in order. Every command has the same instruction stream and its own part of the same three buffers: the addresses in the command are moved by the bytes of a part, and a part is laid out as a command of its rounds. The host packs part p + 1 while the NPU works on part p, and reads part p while it works on p + 1. One layer of 512 tokens (`llama-xdna-gemm gdn --parts <n>`, median): 3.20 ms with 1 command, 2.30 with 2, 2.08 with 4. With 4, the host sets the pace: 1.04 ms to pack and 0.82 to read, the NPU's time all but hidden.
 - **Any number of tokens.** The host fills the last group of 128 with empty tokens: k, q, v and β zero and g zero (no decay). They change neither the state nor the outputs of the real tokens (the matrix of a chunk is strictly lower triangular, their keys and new values are zero, and cs_15 stays the one of the last real token), and their outputs are not read. Layer times with 4 commands: 0.78, 1.19, 1.60 and 2.08 ms for 128, 200 (256 computed), 333 (384) and 512 tokens; the processor takes some 4.25 ms for 512. So the backend takes 128 tokens or more, when they are at least 9/16 of the ones computed (`fGdnTokensTaken`).
 - **Precision in the model** (Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M against the processor with Q8_0, 4 chunks of 512): KL 0.041319 against 0.041168 with the recurrence on the processor (± 0.0023), the same token in 88.9 % in both, and the same KL with 1 or 4 commands (the same values). With batches of 200 (`-ub 200`: 200, 200 and 112 tokens; the second with the state in its cell), 0.040897 against 0.041168. The generated text can part where two tokens are almost tied: after a prompt of 1,122 tokens, `llama-server` with `n_probs` gives the same first 10 tokens with probabilities within 0.016, and then « exposes» 0.100 against « implements» 0.092 with the NPU, and 0.093 for both with the processor. The processor alone already parts at that token between `llama-server` and `llama-completion`.
 - **Speed** (batch of 512, `llama-bench -p 512 -n 0 -r 5`, two interleaved rounds): 1,141 and 1,146 t/s with 4 commands, 1,097 and 1,101 with 1, and 1,019 and 1,003 with the overlay before (the recurrence on the processor): 13 % more.

**C as strips** (2026/11/16; option 3 of that round, «the NPU writing C into the tensors», first step). Reading C was 29 % of a batch of 512: the host turned the groups of 8 × 8 of every tile into rows of floats, a run of 32 bytes in each of 8 rows at a time. In isolation (`llama-xdna-gemm unpack`, 512 × 9216, 8 threads) that took 0.24 to 0.29 ns per value, and reading the same values as rows of C 0.07: the order costs more than the bytes.

 - **The kernel writes its tile as rows** (`xdna2_mm_bfp16_s3_last_rows`, `-DXDNA2_C_ROWS`, `bfpmm_resident.py --c-rows 1`, manifest line `rows 1`, `XdnaOverlay::vRowsC`): every 8 × 8 is 8 stores of a row of 8 values, 16 bytes at 16-byte boundaries. 1,536 bytes of program against 1,568, and no time: with C written in one run a column, the same overlay takes 0.813 ms for 512 × 2560 × 9216 against 0.824 with the kernel before.
 - **The shims lay the tiles out as strips of C**, `[N / 64][M][64]`, where the tile of a core is 8 KB in a row: one descriptor a column and row block, the rows of a tile one after the other, every tile of a turn one strip on, and every turn 4 strips on (`fXdnaGemmPlan`). Written as the rows of C, in runs of one row of a tile (128 bytes), the NPU took 1.23 ms instead of 0.82: the shims could not keep up. Strips keep it at 0.827 ms. Interleaving the 4 tiles of a turn in the memory tile, for runs of 512 bytes, is not possible: in mlir-aie a join gives every input a slot of its own, to the offset of the next input (`ObjectFifoLinkOp`), and walks its output slot by slot.
 - **The host reads strips** (`fUnpackStrips`, `fUnpackSwigluStrips`, `fForEachStrip`): every piece takes 8 strips and some rows, so that a row of the piece is a run of 512 values of its row of a target. 0.37 to 0.45 ms for 512 × 9216 (one strip at a time, 0.51; the blocks, 1.15 to 1.37), the same floats byte for byte; with the cache flushed before, 0.74 ms against some 1.5.
 - **In the model** (Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M): the same KL as before to the last digit (0.041319, perplexity 15.622035, the same token in 88.922 %). Reading results, 424.6 ms in 4 batches against 501.6 (15 % less: what is left is mostly the SwiGLU, the sums of the pieces of K and the residual sums, with the memory shared with the NPU). Batch of 512, interleaved: 1,208 and 1,196 t/s against 1,150 and 1,154 (+4.3 %). It is the default overlay since that day (`_/xdna2/overlays/`, built in `work/bank-rows`; a copy in `overlays-rows/`, and the one before it in `overlays-gdn/`).
 - **C in 32-bit floats written by the NPU into the tensors does not fit this overlay.** The data memory of a core is full: A 2 × 9,216, B 2 × 9,216, C 8,192 in bf16, the sums 16,384 and the stack 3,840, 65,288 of 65,536 bytes; a tile in floats takes 16 KB, 8 KB more. Accumulating straight into the object of C instead (one buffer, no sums) fits, but every core then waits for the drain of every tile (the overlay of C in floats was 5 % slower on 0.8B), the recurrence loses the sums it works in, and the SwiGLU, the residual sums and the sums of the pieces of K, done now while reading C, would need another place.

**The SwiGLU into the rows of ffn_down** (2026/11/16; «the others» after the layer of linear attention, as the user put it: «Y, cuando acabes, ya hacemos las otras»). A new line of the report of the backend (`vDownPackMs`, `vDownUnpackMs`, `vSwigluUnpackMs`) split a batch of 512 of Qwen3.5 4B Q4_K_M: reading the gates and the ups with their SwiGLU, 44 ms; converting the rows of `ffn_down` from that SwiGLU, 34 ms; reading the results of `ffn_down`, with the sums of its 3 pieces along the inputs (4096, 4096 and 1024), 21 ms. The SwiGLU was written as floats (512 × 9216 × 4 bytes, 19 MB a layer) only to be read again right after:

 - **Now the SwiGLU goes straight into the rows of `ffn_down`.** When the multiplication right after the SwiGLU reads it and nothing else does, `fMulMatGlu` asks for the matrix of `ffn_down` (`fMatrixOf`) to know its pieces along the inputs and, as it reads every half of the gate and the up, computes every tile of A of those pieces (64 rows by 128 values of K) into a tile of floats with the same `fXdnaSwiglu` and converts it with `fPackTile` into the buffer of A of its piece (`fXdnaGemmPackSwiglu`, `XdnaRunner::mUnpackSwigluRows`). The runner has 5 buffers of A instead of 2 (`cXdnaRunnerSlotsA`; the 3 from `cXdnaRunnerFirstReadyA` on are made ready with `mPrepareRowsA` and usable with `mReadyRowsA`), and `fMultiply` takes them without converting anything (`XdnaBackend::vPacked`). Every tile of a piece has to lie inside a half of the gate (the halves of 4608 columns and the pieces are multiples of 128: `fXdnaGemmSwigluFits`); otherwise, or with `GGML_XDNA_NO_PACK_GLU`, the SwiGLU is written as before.
 - **The same bytes**: the same floats into the same encoder, so the logits are identical byte for byte with batches of 512 and of 200 (where the last batch of a chunk has 112 rows and the rest of a band is zeros), and the same text of 128 tokens.
 - **Measured** (`llama-bench -p 512 -n 0 -r 5`, two rounds alternating): 1,238 and 1,231 tokens per second against 1,194 and 1,193 (+3.4 %). In 4 batches, converting the rows of `ffn_down` went from 144 ms to 0 and reading the gates and the ups with their SwiGLU from 182 to 160 ms; part of what went away was done while the NPU worked, so the batch gains less than that.
 - **The pieces of `ffn_down` along its inputs, measured and left as they were.** With its rows ready, the three commands of `ffn_down` can go at once (the driver keeps 4 per context), and the first two pieces can be read together (the float of the first plus that of the second, the same floats as writing the first and adding the second; the logits identical byte for byte): reading the results of `ffn_down` went from 84.5 to 71.5 ms in 4 batches, but the batch was 1.1 % slower (1,229 and 1,225 against 1,243 and 1,239 tokens per second), because the first piece was no longer read while the NPU multiplied the second. Sent at once and read one by one as before, the same speed (1,241 and 1,232 against 1,243, 1,239 and 1,229). The reading of those pieces already overlaps the NPU, so it was taken out again.

**Where a batch of 512 goes now, and the gated norm into the rows of ssm_out** (2026/11/16, after the user said «ok, dale» to measuring before choosing). The report of the backend (`llama-bench -p 512 -n 0 -r 3 -v`, 4 batches, at 1,233 tokens per second, some 415 ms a batch) gave per batch: the multiplications some 250 ms in the backend (waiting for the NPU 121, converting rows 24, reading results 101, of which the SwiGLU 41 and `ffn_down` 21), the recurrence of the prompt some 49 ms (the NPU 11, writing its inputs 18, reading its results 20) and the processor some 116 ms with the rest. `perf record -e cycles:u -D 6000` over 20 batches (allowed without root with `perf_event_paranoid` at 2; the first seconds left out, as converting the weights at load dominates them) split the time of the processor, all threads together, some 1.55 s a batch: reading the C of the multiplications (`fUnpackStrips`) 18 %, the threads of OpenMP spinning 18 % (time of the processor, not of the batch), the attention of the CPU backend (`flash_attn_ext`) 11 %, converting rows 8 %, `ggml_vec_silu_f32` 5 %, the convolution with its SiLU 4 %, the RMS norms 5 %, `ggml_compute_forward_mul` 2 %, writing and reading the recurrence of the prompt some 7 % (`fCopy8`, 4 %, is its outputs written 8 floats at a time 16 KB apart). Chosen: the gated norm of the 24 layers of linear attention, four passes of the CPU backend over the 2 M values of a layer (RMS_NORM, the MUL by `ssm_norm`, SILU of z and their MUL) right before the rows of `ssm_out` are converted:

 - **The gated norm goes straight into the rows of `ssm_out`** (`fGatedTaken`, `fGatedNorm`, `XdnaRunner::mPackGatedNormRows`, `fXdnaGemmPackGatedNorm`). A tile of K of the overlay (128 values) is a head, so every tile of A of `ssm_out` is computed from the outputs of the recurrence and from z into a tile of floats: the scales of its 64 rows as the CPU backend computes them (`fXdnaRmsScales`), x * scale * w[i] (the order of `ggml_compute_forward_rms_norm_f32` with its MUL), and silu(z) times that with the vectors of the CPU backend for rows of 128 (`fXdnaSwiglu`); then `fPackTile` as `fXdnaGemmPackA` converts it, into a buffer of A from `cXdnaRunnerFirstReadyA` on, which `fMultiply` takes as it takes the rows of `ffn_down` (`XdnaBackend::vPacked`). The four operations are taken for batches of at least the rows a multiplication needs for the NPU (`GGML_XDNA_MIN_BATCH`), as `ssm_out` is then; when they do not come in that order right before it, they are computed one by one on the host with the same arithmetic (`fGatedOnHost`). `GGML_XDNA_NO_GATED` leaves them with the CPU backend.
 - **Checked**: the logits identical byte for byte with batches of 512 and of 200 (the last of 112 rows), and the same text of 128 tokens. Converting 96 gated norms (4 batches) took 64.5 ms and flushing their rows 3.4 ms; the first time, `fMatrixOf` converts the weights of `ssm_out` there.
 - **Measured** (three rounds alternating, `-r 5`): 1,273, 1,253 and 1,250 tokens per second against 1,246, 1,222 and 1,232 with `GGML_XDNA_NO_GATED` (+2.1 %, every round above its pair).

**The L2 norms of q and k and the gates into the recurrence of a prompt, and its outputs by rows** (2026/11/16, points 1 and 2 of the three the user confirmed with «ok»):

 - **The L2 norms** (`build_gdn_l2_norm`: an RMS_NORM of a view of the SiLU of the convolution and a SCALE by 1/√128) go into the writing of the inputs of the recurrence: `fGatedDeltaNet` reads q and k before their norms, the threads compute the scales of the RMS_NORM of every head of keys as the CPU backend does (`fXdnaRmsScales`, [head of keys][token]), and the writing takes every value as (x * scale) * factor of the SCALE, and q then times the scale of the recurrence (`XdnaGdnIo::vQNorms`, `vKNorms`, `vNormScale`; the same floats). The norms are not computed (`fL2Taken`, `fL2Fold`; on the host as the CPU backend computes them when they are not those of a recurrence of the backend, `fL2OnHost`).
